=== py2-01 | Comprehensions: list, dict and set
--- teach
Welcome to the intermediate course. The basics course ended with classes. The lesson before that one was about list comprehensions: one line that builds a new list out of an old one. This lesson comes back to comprehensions, because Python code is full of them. You will learn a third shape, the set, and a rule about "empty" values that makes the filter part much handier.

Think of a cookie cutter. The dough is the same every time, but the cutter decides the shape you get: a star, a heart, a circle. In a comprehension, the loop is the dough, and the brackets around it are the cutter.

### A quick reminder: the list shape

```python
nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6]
big = [n * 10 for n in nums if n > 3]
print(big)    # [40, 50, 90, 60]
```

Read it left to right, as a sentence: "n times 10, for each n in nums, if n is greater than 3". The square brackets `[ ]` say "the answer is a list". The `if` part is the filter: items that fail it are skipped.

### A new kind of collection: the set

A **[[set|set-word]]** is a collection with no duplicates and no fixed order. You write one with curly braces `{ }` and commas:

```python
colours = {"red", "blue", "red"}
print(colours)         # {'blue', 'red'}   (the second "red" is dropped)
print(len(colours))    # 2
print("red" in colours)   # True
```

Each value can be in a set only once. Add it again and nothing changes. And a set has no positions, so there is no `colours[0]`. You ask a set one kind of question: "is this in you?", with `in`. Sets answer that question [[very fast|set-speed]], even when they are huge.

Python may print the items in a different order from the one you typed. That is fine: a set does not promise any order.

### The set comprehension

Use curly braces around a comprehension, with one value at the front (no colon), and you get a **set comprehension**. It builds a set:

```python
nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6]
leftovers = {n % 3 for n in nums}
print(leftovers)    # {0, 1, 2}
```

Remember that `%` means "the remainder after dividing". Read it as "the remainder of n divided by 3, for each n in nums". There are eight numbers, but only three different remainders are possible, so the set keeps only `0`, `1` and `2`. The duplicates are gone.

### The dict shape, again

Curly braces with a key, a colon `:` and a value at the front make a **dict comprehension**. You met it in the basics:

```python
nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6]
squares = {n: n * n for n in nums if n % 2 == 0}
print(squares)    # {4: 16, 2: 4, 6: 36}
```

Read it as "n maps to n times n, for each n in nums, if n is even". (`==` means "is equal to", and a number is even when dividing by 2 leaves a remainder of 0.)

So the brackets decide what you get:

| You write | You get |
| --- | --- |
| `[x for ...]` | a list |
| `{x for ...}` | a set |
| `{k: v for ...}` | a dict |

### Looping over a dict's pairs

In the basics you looped over `d.items()` with two names, one for the key and one for the value. That works inside a comprehension too. Put the value first and the key second, and you build a new dict that is the old one [[turned inside out|swap-keys]]:

```python
ages = {"ada": 36, "lin": 12}
by_age = {v: k for k, v in ages.items()}
print(by_age)    # {36: 'ada', 12: 'lin'}
```

Read it as "v maps to k, for each key k and value v in the pairs of ages". The comprehension builds a new dict. `ages` is not changed.

### Why bother with comprehensions?

A comprehension says *what* the result is, in one line. A loop says *how* to build it, over four lines: make an empty list, loop, test, append. A reader sees "a list of these, filtered by that" at a glance. And there is no half-built variable in the middle that you might get wrong. Programmers call this [[saying what, not how|what-not-how]].

### Truthiness: when a value counts as false

Every `if` asks a yes-or-no question. But you can put any value after `if`, not only `True` or `False`. Python then decides whether that value counts as true or false. This is called **[[truthiness|truthy-falsy]]**.

The rule: **empty or zero counts as false**. These all count as false:

- `""` (an empty string)
- `[]` (an empty list)
- `{}` (an empty dict)
- `set()` (an empty set)
- `0` (zero)
- `None` (the "nothing" value)

Everything else counts as true: a string with something in it, a list with items, any number that is not zero.

```python
name = ""
if name:
    print("hello", name)
else:
    print("no name given")    # this line runs
```

So `if name:` means "if `name` is not empty". The same works in a comprehension's filter. This keeps only the strings that have something in them:

```python
names = ["ada", "", "lin", ""]
real = [name for name in names if name]
print(real)    # ['ada', 'lin']
```

### Reading one letter out of a string

A string lines up its letters in positions, like a list. So you can use an [[index|string-index]] on it, counting from 0. `word[0]` is its first letter:

```python
word = "Orbit"
print(word[0])            # O
print(word[0].lower())    # o
```

`word[0].lower()` takes the first letter and then makes it lowercase. An empty string has no first letter, so `""[0]` stops with `IndexError`. That is one more reason to filter out empty strings first.

**Watch out:** three mistakes people really make with comprehensions.

- `{}` on its own is an empty *dict*, not an empty set. Write `set()` for an empty set.
- Swapping keys and values with `{v: k for k, v in d.items()}` silently loses entries when two keys share a value. Each key can appear only once, so the later one wins: `{v: k for k, v in {"a": 1, "b": 1}.items()}` is `{1: 'b'}`.
- A comprehension that *does* something, like `[print(x) for x in xs]`, builds a useless list of `None`s. Use a plain `for` loop when you want to do something, and a comprehension when you want to [[make something|side-effects]].

::: context set-word A word borrowed from maths
In maths, a set is a collection of different things, where only membership matters: is it in, or not? The set of vowels is {a, e, i, o, u}, and writing "a" twice does not make it bigger. Python's set works the same way, and it can do the classic set sums: `a | b` (union, "everything in either") and `a & b` (intersection, "only what is in both"). The advanced lessons use those. For now, think of a set as a bag of labels where each label can appear only once.
:::

::: context set-speed Why "is it in?" is fast
To answer `"red" in some_list`, Python has to walk down the list and compare, one item at a time. With a million items that can mean a million comparisons. A set is stored differently. Python turns each value into a number, called a hash, and uses that number to decide which shelf the value lives on. To check `"red" in colours`, Python works out the hash of `"red"` and looks on that one shelf. So the check takes about the same time whether the set holds ten items or ten million. Dicts use the same trick for their keys, which is why dict lookups are fast too.
:::

::: context swap-keys Turning a table around
A dict is a one-way lookup: name to age, part number to part name, sensor id to sensor location. Sometimes you need the other direction. Ground software for a spacecraft might keep a table from each telemetry channel's number to its name, and a person at a console types a name and wants the number. Swapping keys and values gives you the reverse table in one line. It only works cleanly when every value is different, as the "watch out" at the end of the lesson explains.

```svg

```
:::

::: context what-not-how Describing the result
There are two ways to ask for a cup of tea. You can say "a cup of tea with milk, please". Or you can say "boil water, put a bag in a cup, pour, wait three minutes, remove the bag, add milk". The first describes the result; the second gives the steps. A comprehension is closer to the first kind. It reads like a description of the list you want. Code that describes results is usually shorter and has fewer places for a bug to hide, which is why experienced Python programmers reach for comprehensions whenever the job is "make a new collection from an old one".
:::

::: context truthy-falsy Truthy and falsy
Programmers have two slang words for this: a value that counts as true is "truthy", and one that counts as false is "falsy". So `"hi"` is truthy and `""` is falsy. You can ask Python directly with `bool(...)`: `bool("")` is `False` and `bool("hi")` is `True`. Careful with look-alikes: `"0"` is a string with one character in it, so it is truthy, even though the number `0` is falsy. The same goes for `" "`, a string holding one space: not empty, so truthy.
:::

::: context string-index Strings are sequences too
A list and a string are both sequences: things in a row, with positions counted from 0. So the same square-bracket tricks work on both. `"Orbit"[0]` is `"O"`, and `"Orbit"[-1]`, counting back from the end, is `"t"`. The one difference is that you cannot change a letter in place: `word[0] = "o"` stops with an error, because a string can never be changed after it is made. Instead you build a new string. You will see why that is useful in the string-methods lesson.
:::

::: context side-effects Doing versus making
Printing, saving a file or sending a message changes something outside the code. Programmers call that a side effect. A comprehension is meant for making a new value, and a reader expects it to have no side effects. `[print(x) for x in xs]` does print, but it also builds a list of the values `print` hands back, which are all `None`, and then throws that list away. It works, but it tells the reader the wrong story. A two-line `for` loop says "do this for each item" honestly.
:::
--- task
Write three functions. Each one should `return` a single comprehension. You can delete the starter lines inside them.

- `squares_of_odds(nums)` returns a **list** of the squares (`n * n`) of the odd numbers in `nums`, in their original order. A number is odd when `n % 2 == 1`. For example, `squares_of_odds([1, 2, 3, 4, 5])` gives `[1, 9, 25]`.
- `invert(d)` returns a new **dict** with the keys and values of `d` swapped. Loop over `d.items()`. For example, `invert({"a": 1, "b": 2})` gives `{1: "a", 2: "b"}`.
- `initials(names)` returns the **set** of lowercase first letters of the names, skipping empty strings. Use `name[0].lower()` for the first letter and `if name` to skip the empty ones. For example, `initials(["Ada", "alan", "Grace", "", "guido"])` gives `{"a", "g"}`.
--- starter
def squares_of_odds(nums):
    result = []
    return result


def invert(d):
    return d


def initials(names):
    return set()
--- solution
def squares_of_odds(nums):
    return [n * n for n in nums if n % 2 == 1]


def invert(d):
    return {v: k for k, v in d.items()}


def initials(names):
    return {name[0].lower() for name in names if name}
--- hint
Each function body can be one `return` followed by a comprehension. Decide on the brackets first: square brackets for a list, curly braces with `key: value` for a dict, curly braces with one value for a set.
--- hint
`squares_of_odds` is like the `big` example: `[n * n for n in nums if n % 2 == 1]`. `invert` is like the `by_age` example: `d.items()` gives each key and value, and you put the value first.
--- hint
For `initials`, the value at the front is `name[0].lower()`, and the filter at the end is `if name`, which skips empty strings because an empty string counts as false: `return {name[0].lower() for name in names if name}`.
--- check case | squares_of_odds keeps odd numbers, squared, in order
squares_of_odds([1, 2, 3, 4, 5])
=> [1, 9, 25]
--- check case | Negative odd numbers count too
squares_of_odds([-3, -2, 0, 7])
=> [9, 49]
--- check case | squares_of_odds of an empty list is empty
squares_of_odds([])
=> []
--- check case | invert swaps keys and values
invert({"a": 1, "b": 2})
=> {1: "a", 2: "b"}
--- check test | invert does not change the dict you pass in
(lambda d: (invert(d), d)[1] == {"x": 9})({"x": 9})
--- check case | initials is a set of lowercase first letters
initials(["Ada", "alan", "Grace", "", "guido"])
=> {"a", "g"}
--- check case | initials of no names is an empty set
initials([])
=> set()

+++ practice | Readings above a limit
--- task
Write `above(readings, limit)`. It returns a **list** of the readings that are greater than `limit`, in their original order. A reading equal to `limit` is not kept. Return a single list comprehension.

For example, `above([12, 40, 7, 55, 40], 30)` gives `[40, 55, 40]`, and `above([], 5)` gives `[]`.
--- starter
def above(readings, limit):
    return readings
--- solution
def above(readings, limit):
    return [r for r in readings if r > limit]
--- hint
This is the list shape with a filter at the end: square brackets, a value, a `for`, then an `if`.
--- hint
The value is the reading itself, and the filter is `if r > limit`, so a reading equal to the limit fails the test and is skipped.
--- check case | Keeps the readings over the limit, in order
above([12, 40, 7, 55, 40], 30)
=> [40, 55, 40]
--- check case | A reading equal to the limit is not kept
above([30, 31, 29], 30)
=> [31]
--- check case | Nothing above the limit gives an empty list
above([1, 2, 3], 10)
=> []
--- check case | No readings at all
above([], 5)
=> []

+++ practice | How many different lengths?
--- task
Write `word_lengths(words)`. It returns the **set** of the lengths of the words, so each length appears only once. Return a single set comprehension.

For example, `word_lengths(["sun", "moon", "star", "sky"])` gives `{3, 4}`, and `word_lengths([])` gives `set()`, the empty set.
--- starter
def word_lengths(words):
    return [len(w) for w in words]
--- solution
def word_lengths(words):
    return {len(w) for w in words}
--- hint
The starter builds a list. Which brackets make a set instead?
--- hint
Curly braces with one value at the front (no colon) make a set: `{len(w) for w in words}`.
--- check case | Each length appears once
word_lengths(["sun", "moon", "star", "sky"])
=> {3, 4}
--- check case | Words that are all different lengths
word_lengths(["a", "to", "the"])
=> {1, 2, 3}
--- check case | No words gives the empty set
word_lengths([])
=> set()
--- check test | The answer really is a set
type(word_lengths(["x"])) is set

+++ practice | Who passed?
--- task
`scores` is a dict mapping each name to a score, like `{"ada": 91, "lin": 64}`. Write `passed(scores, mark)`. It returns a **new dict** holding only the names whose score is `mark` or more, each with its score. Loop over `scores.items()` in a single dict comprehension, and do not change `scores`.

For example, `passed({"ada": 91, "lin": 64, "sam": 70}, 70)` gives `{"ada": 91, "sam": 70}`. If nobody reaches the mark, return `{}`.
--- starter
def passed(scores, mark):
    return scores
--- solution
def passed(scores, mark):
    return {name: score for name, score in scores.items() if score >= mark}
--- hint
You need the dict shape, `{key: value for ...}`, and two names in the `for`: one for the name and one for the score.
--- hint
Keep a pair only when `score >= mark`, which reads "score is greater than or equal to mark".
--- check case | Keeps the names at or above the mark
passed({"ada": 91, "lin": 64, "sam": 70}, 70)
=> {"ada": 91, "sam": 70}
--- check case | Nobody passes
passed({"ada": 10, "lin": 20}, 50)
=> {}
--- check case | An empty dict gives an empty dict
passed({}, 50)
=> {}
--- check test | The dict you pass in is not changed
(lambda d: (passed(d, 50), d)[1] == {"a": 10, "b": 90})({"a": 10, "b": 90})

+++ practice | Drop the missing readings
--- task
A sensor log is a list where each reading is a number, or `None` when the sensor sent nothing. A reading of `0` also means "no signal". Write `real_readings(readings)`. It returns a list of the readings that are not `None` and not zero, in order. Negative readings are real and must be kept.

For example, `real_readings([3, 0, None, -2, 5])` gives `[3, -2, 5]`. Use one list comprehension.
--- starter
def real_readings(readings):
    return [r for r in readings if r is not None]
--- solution
def real_readings(readings):
    return [r for r in readings if r]
--- hint
Which values count as false in an `if`? Look back at the truthiness rule in the lesson.
--- hint
`None`, `0` and `0.0` all count as false, and every other number counts as true, negative ones included. So the filter can be just the reading itself.
--- check case | None and zero are dropped, negatives stay
real_readings([3, 0, None, -2, 5])
=> [3, -2, 5]
--- check case | Zero written as a decimal is dropped too
real_readings([0.0, 1.5, None])
=> [1.5]
--- check case | Only missing readings gives an empty list
real_readings([None, 0, None])
=> []
--- check case | A single negative reading is kept
real_readings([-1])
=> [-1]
--- check case | No readings at all
real_readings([])
=> []

+++ practice | Debug: the vowels that were a dict
--- task
`vowels_in(word)` should return the **set** of vowels (a, e, i, o, u) that appear in `word`, ignoring capitals. For `"Orbit"` it gives `{"o", "i"}`, and that part works.

The bug: for a word with no vowels, such as `"sky"`, it returns `{}`, which is an empty **dict**, not an empty set. Code that later asks `type(...)` or compares with `set()` goes wrong. Fix it so a word with no vowels gives `set()`.
--- starter
def vowels_in(word):
    found = {c for c in word.lower() if c in "aeiou"}
    if not found:
        return {}
    return found
--- solution
def vowels_in(word):
    return {c for c in word.lower() if c in "aeiou"}
--- hint
What does `{}` on its own make: an empty set or an empty dict? Look at the lesson's "Watch out".
--- hint
The set comprehension already gives an empty set when nothing passes the filter. The special case is not needed at all.
--- check case | Finds the vowels, ignoring capitals
vowels_in("Orbit")
=> {"o", "i"}
--- check case | A word with no vowels gives the empty set
vowels_in("sky")
=> set()
--- check test | The answer is always a set
type(vowels_in("rhythm")) is set and type(vowels_in("aeiou")) is set
--- check case | Each vowel is listed once
vowels_in("Banana")
=> {"a"}

+++ practice | Sensors that are silent
--- task
A ground station keeps a dict from each sensor's name to the list of readings it sent this minute. A sensor that sent nothing has an empty list. For example:

```
{"temp": [21, 22], "fuel": [], "volts": [28, 27, 29]}
```

Write two functions, each returning a single comprehension:

- `peaks(log)` returns a **dict** from each sensor that sent at least one reading to its largest reading (use `max`). Sensors with an empty list are left out. For the log above it gives `{"temp": 22, "volts": 29}`.
- `silent(log)` returns the **set** of names of the sensors that sent nothing. For the log above it gives `{"fuel"}`.
--- starter
def peaks(log):
    return {name: max(values) for name, values in log.items()}


def silent(log):
    return set()
--- solution
def peaks(log):
    return {name: max(values) for name, values in log.items() if values}


def silent(log):
    return {name for name, values in log.items() if not values}
--- hint
An empty list counts as false. So `if values` keeps the sensors that sent something, and `if not values` keeps the ones that did not.
--- hint
`max` of an empty list stops with an error, so `peaks` must filter out the empty lists before `max` ever sees them. `silent` is a set comprehension over the same `log.items()`.
--- check case | peaks gives each sensor's largest reading
peaks({"temp": [21, 22], "fuel": [], "volts": [28, 27, 29]})
=> {"temp": 22, "volts": 29}
--- check case | silent names the sensors that sent nothing
silent({"temp": [21, 22], "fuel": [], "volts": [28, 27, 29]})
=> {"fuel"}
--- check case | Every sensor silent
peaks({"a": [], "b": []})
=> {}
--- check case | No sensor silent gives the empty set
silent({"a": [1], "b": [-4]})
=> set()
--- check case | A single negative reading is still a reading
peaks({"cold": [-40]})
=> {"cold": -40}

=== py2-01b | Two loops and a choice inside a comprehension
--- teach
Last lesson you built lists, sets and dicts with one `for` and an optional `if` filter. This lesson adds two more things a comprehension can hold: a second `for`, and a choice between two values.

Think of the seats in a small theater. There are rows A and B, and each row has seats 1, 2 and 3. To list every seat, you go along row A first (A1, A2, A3), then along row B (B1, B2, B3). That is a loop inside a loop.

### The long way: a loop inside a loop

You can already write this with two `for` loops, one inside the other:

```python
seats = []
for row in ["A", "B"]:
    for seat in range(1, 4):
        seats.append(f"{row}{seat}")
print(seats)    # ['A1', 'A2', 'A3', 'B1', 'B2', 'B3']
```

The outer loop picks a row. For each row, the inner loop runs all the way through the seats. `range(1, 4)` gives 1, 2 and 3 (it stops before 4). The f-string `f"{row}{seat}"` glues the row letter and the seat number into one label.

### The short way: two `for`s in one comprehension

```python
seats = [f"{row}{seat}" for row in ["A", "B"] for seat in range(1, 4)]
print(seats)    # ['A1', 'A2', 'A3', 'B1', 'B2', 'B3']
```

The two `for` parts come in **the same order** as in the nested loops: the outer one first, the inner one second. Read it as "row-and-seat, for each row, for each seat". The last `for` changes fastest, like the [[right-hand digit of a counter|odometer]].

### Choosing between two values

Sometimes you do not want to drop an item. You want to turn every item into one of two answers. For that Python has the **[[conditional expression|conditional-expression]]**: a value that picks one of two options.

```python
n = 4
label = "even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd"
print(label)    # even
```

Read `a if condition else b` as "a if the condition is true, otherwise b". Here: "`"even"` if n divided by 2 leaves remainder 0, otherwise `"odd"`". It is one value, so it works anywhere a value does: after `=`, inside `print( )`, as a function's `return`, and at the front of a comprehension.

### A choice inside a comprehension

Put a conditional expression at the front, where the value goes:

```python
labels = ["even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd" for n in [1, 2, 3]]
print(labels)    # ['odd', 'even', 'odd']
```

Every item makes it into the new list. The `if ... else` only decides *what* each one becomes. That is why it goes *before* the `for`: it is part of the value, not a filter.

Compare the two kinds of `if`:

| Where the `if` is | What it does | Has `else`? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| before `for` | chooses what each item becomes | yes, always |
| after `for` | decides which items are kept | never |

**Watch out:** a filter `if` at the end cannot have an `else`. `[n for n in nums if n > 0 else 0]` stops with `SyntaxError`. If you want every item kept but changed, move the `if ... else` to the front: `[n if n > 0 else 0 for n in nums]`. And do not stack too much in one line. [[Two loops is about the limit|readable-nesting]]; beyond that, write ordinary loops.

::: context odometer The last loop turns fastest
A car's distance counter shows 0019, then 0020: the right-hand digit spins through 0 to 9 before the digit to its left moves on by one. Nested loops work the same way. The inner loop (the last `for` in a comprehension) runs all the way through for every single step of the outer one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="22" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">outer: row</text>
  <text x="200" y="22" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">inner: seat 1, 2, 3</text>
  <text x="40" y="58" font-size="14" fill="#1d6fd1" font-weight="700">A</text>
  <text x="40" y="100" font-size="14" fill="#1d6fd1" font-weight="700">B</text>
  <rect x="120" y="38" width="56" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="180" y="38" width="56" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="240" y="38" width="56" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="120" y="80" width="56" height="30" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="180" y="80" width="56" height="30" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="240" y="80" width="56" height="30" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="148" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A1</text>
  <text x="208" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A2</text>
  <text x="268" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A3</text>
  <text x="148" y="100" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">B1</text>
  <text x="208" y="100" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">B2</text>
  <text x="268" y="100" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">B3</text>
  <text x="310" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">1st</text>
  <text x="310" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">2nd</text>
  <text x="180" y="126" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">a whole row is finished before the next row starts</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context conditional-expression A one-line if
Many languages have this idea. In C, C++ and JavaScript it is written `condition ? a : b` and is often called the ternary operator, because it takes three parts. Python chose words instead of symbols, so it reads almost like English: `"even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd"`. The difference from an ordinary `if` statement is that this one *gives back a value*. An `if` statement runs lines; a conditional expression is something you can store in a variable.
:::

::: context readable-nesting Keep it readable
Python allows three, four or more `for` parts in one comprehension, plus filters on each. That does not mean you should write them. A good test: can a teammate read it aloud in one breath and know what list comes out? In 2006 Gerard Holzmann of NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory published "The Power of Ten", ten rules for safety-critical code. The first asks for simple control flow, because code that is easy to follow is easy to check. Every line of flight software is reviewed by someone else, and readable code gets its bugs caught early.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. Each one should `return` a single comprehension.

- `seat_labels(rows, seats)` returns a list of every seat label, row by row. `rows` is a list of row letters and `seats` is how many seats each row has, numbered from 1. Use two `for`s and the f-string `f"{row}{seat}"`, with `range(1, seats + 1)` for the seat numbers. For example, `seat_labels(["A", "B"], 3)` gives `["A1", "A2", "A3", "B1", "B2", "B3"]`.
- `parity(nums)` returns a list with `"even"` or `"odd"` for each number in `nums`, in order. Use a conditional expression at the front: `"even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd"`. For example, `parity([1, 2, 3])` gives `["odd", "even", "odd"]`.
--- starter
def seat_labels(rows, seats):
    return []


def parity(nums):
    return []
--- solution
def seat_labels(rows, seats):
    return [f"{row}{seat}" for row in rows for seat in range(1, seats + 1)]


def parity(nums):
    return ["even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd" for n in nums]
--- hint
`seat_labels` is the seats example with `rows` and `seats` in place of the fixed values. `parity` is the `labels` example with `nums` in place of `[1, 2, 3]`.
--- hint
The outer loop goes first: `for row in rows for seat in range(1, seats + 1)`. `range` stops one short, so `seats + 1` makes the last seat number `seats`.
--- hint
`return [f"{row}{seat}" for row in rows for seat in range(1, seats + 1)]` and `return ["even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd" for n in nums]`.
--- check case | seat_labels goes row by row
seat_labels(["A", "B"], 3)
=> ["A1", "A2", "A3", "B1", "B2", "B3"]
--- check case | One row with two seats
seat_labels(["C"], 2)
=> ["C1", "C2"]
--- check case | No rows, no seats
seat_labels([], 4)
=> []
--- check case | parity labels each number
parity([1, 2, 3])
=> ["odd", "even", "odd"]
--- check case | Zero is even, negative odd numbers are odd
parity([0, -3, 10])
=> ["even", "odd", "even"]
--- check case | parity of an empty list is empty
parity([])
=> []
--- check source | Uses a conditional expression
\bif\b[^\n]*\belse\b
?? Put "even" if n % 2 == 0 else "odd" at the front of the comprehension.

+++ practice | A times table as one list
--- task
Write `times_table(n)`. It returns a list of every product `i * j`, where `i` goes from 1 to `n` and, for each `i`, `j` goes from 1 to `n`. The `i` loop is the outer one. Return a single comprehension with two `for`s.

For example, `times_table(2)` gives `[1, 2, 2, 4]` (that is 1×1, 1×2, 2×1, 2×2), and `times_table(0)` gives `[]`.
--- starter
def times_table(n):
    return [i * i for i in range(1, n + 1)]
--- solution
def times_table(n):
    return [i * j for i in range(1, n + 1) for j in range(1, n + 1)]
--- hint
Two `for` parts in one comprehension come in the same order as nested loops: the outer one first.
--- hint
Both loops use `range(1, n + 1)`, so they run from 1 up to and including `n`. The value at the front is `i * j`.
--- check case | Two by two
times_table(2)
=> [1, 2, 2, 4]
--- check case | Three by three, row by row
times_table(3)
=> [1, 2, 3, 2, 4, 6, 3, 6, 9]
--- check case | One by one
times_table(1)
=> [1]
--- check case | Zero gives an empty list
times_table(0)
=> []

+++ practice | Cap every value
--- task
Write `cap(values, limit)`. It returns a new list with one item for each value, in order: the value itself if it is not above `limit`, or `limit` if it is. Every value stays in the list; only the big ones change. Use a conditional expression at the front of one comprehension.

For example, `cap([3, 12, 7, 20], 10)` gives `[3, 10, 7, 10]`.
--- starter
def cap(values, limit):
    return [v for v in values if v <= limit]
--- solution
def cap(values, limit):
    return [limit if v > limit else v for v in values]
--- hint
The starter uses a filter, which drops the big values. You want to keep every item and change some, so the `if` belongs before the `for`.
--- hint
The value at the front is a choice of two: `limit` when `v > limit`, otherwise `v`. A conditional expression always has an `else`.
--- check case | Big values are brought down to the limit
cap([3, 12, 7, 20], 10)
=> [3, 10, 7, 10]
--- check case | A value equal to the limit stays as it is
cap([10, 11], 10)
=> [10, 10]
--- check case | Negative values are never capped
cap([-5, 0], 1)
=> [-5, 0]
--- check case | No values
cap([], 10)
=> []

+++ practice | Tank status board
--- task
`levels` is a dict from each tank's name to how full it is, in percent, like `{"A": 80, "B": 12}`. Write `status_board(levels)`. It returns a new dict from each tank's name to `"LOW"` if its level is below 20, or `"OK"` otherwise. Use a single dict comprehension with a conditional expression for the value.

For example, `status_board({"A": 80, "B": 12, "C": 20})` gives `{"A": "OK", "B": "LOW", "C": "OK"}`.
--- starter
def status_board(levels):
    return {name: "OK" for name, level in levels.items()}
--- solution
def status_board(levels):
    return {name: "LOW" if level < 20 else "OK" for name, level in levels.items()}
--- hint
In a dict comprehension the part after the colon is the value. That value can itself be a conditional expression.
--- hint
Write the value as `"LOW" if level < 20 else "OK"`, and loop with `for name, level in levels.items()`.
--- check case | Low tanks are marked
status_board({"A": 80, "B": 12, "C": 20})
=> {"A": "OK", "B": "LOW", "C": "OK"}
--- check case | An empty tank is low
status_board({"X": 0})
=> {"X": "LOW"}
--- check case | No tanks, no board
status_board({})
=> {}

+++ practice | Every match-up
--- task
Write `matchups(teams)`. It returns a list of strings `"A vs B"` for every way to pick a home team and an away team, where the two are **different** teams. The home team is the outer loop, in the order of `teams`, and the away team is the inner loop. Use one comprehension with two `for`s and a filter.

For example, `matchups(["A", "B", "C"])` gives `["A vs B", "A vs C", "B vs A", "B vs C", "C vs A", "C vs B"]`. With one team, or none, there are no match-ups: return `[]`.
--- starter
def matchups(teams):
    return [f"{home} vs {away}" for home in teams for away in teams]
--- solution
def matchups(teams):
    return [f"{home} vs {away}" for home in teams for away in teams if home != away]
--- hint
The starter lets a team play itself, such as `"A vs A"`. A filter at the end can drop those.
--- hint
`!=` means "is not equal to". Put `if home != away` after the second `for`.
--- check case | Every pair of different teams, home team first
matchups(["A", "B", "C"])
=> ["A vs B", "A vs C", "B vs A", "B vs C", "C vs A", "C vs B"]
--- check case | One team cannot play itself
matchups(["Solo"])
=> []
--- check case | No teams
matchups([])
=> []
--- check case | Two teams play home and away
matchups(["Red", "Blue"])
=> ["Red vs Blue", "Blue vs Red"]

+++ practice | Debug: readings day by day
--- task
`days` is a list of lists: one inner list of readings for each day, such as `[[3, 5], [], [8]]`. `all_readings(days)` should return one plain list of every reading, day by day, in order. For that example it should give `[3, 5, 8]`.

Instead it stops with `NameError: name 'day' is not defined`. Fix the comprehension.
--- starter
def all_readings(days):
    return [r for r in day for day in days]
--- solution
def all_readings(days):
    return [r for day in days for r in day]
--- hint
Write it as two ordinary nested loops first. Which loop has to come first so that `day` exists when the other loop uses it?
--- hint
The `for` parts go in the same order as nested loops: the loop over `days` is the outer one, and the loop over the readings of one `day` is the inner one.
--- check case | Every reading, day by day
all_readings([[3, 5], [], [8]])
=> [3, 5, 8]
--- check case | One day
all_readings([[1, 2, 3]])
=> [1, 2, 3]
--- check case | No days
all_readings([])
=> []
--- check case | Only empty days
all_readings([[], []])
=> []

+++ practice | Pairs of tanks that hold exactly enough
--- task
A rocket stage needs exactly `target` kilograms of fuel, carried in two **different** tanks. `sizes` lists the tank sizes, all different, in order. Write `tank_pairs(sizes, target)`. It returns a list of strings `"a+b"` for every pair of sizes `a` and `b` where `a` is smaller than `b` and `a + b == target`. The outer loop is `a`, the inner loop is `b`, both over `sizes`. Use one comprehension.

For example, `tank_pairs([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 6)` gives `["1+5", "2+4"]`. The pair `3+3` is not allowed, because that would be the same tank twice. If no pair works, return `[]`.
--- starter
def tank_pairs(sizes, target):
    return [f"{a}+{b}" for a in sizes for b in sizes if a + b == target]
--- solution
def tank_pairs(sizes, target):
    return [f"{a}+{b}" for a in sizes for b in sizes if a < b and a + b == target]
--- hint
The starter lists every pair twice (`"1+5"` and `"5+1"`) and lets a tank pair with itself. One extra condition fixes both.
--- hint
Asking for `a < b` keeps only one order of each pair, and it also rules out `a` and `b` being the same tank. Join the two conditions with `and`.
--- check case | Each pair once, smaller size first
tank_pairs([1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 6)
=> ["1+5", "2+4"]
--- check case | The order of the pairs follows the outer loop
tank_pairs([4, 2, 5, 1], 6)
=> ["2+4", "1+5"]
--- check case | A tank cannot pair with itself
tank_pairs([3, 7], 6)
=> []
--- check case | No tanks
tank_pairs([], 6)
=> []

=== py2-02 | Tuples, unpacking, enumerate and zip
--- teach
Last lesson you put two `for`s in one comprehension to make seat labels like `"A1"`. The row and the seat were glued into one string. This lesson shows how to keep values like that side by side, as a pair, without gluing them, and how to take a pair apart again.

Think of a coordinate on a map, like (3, 4): three across, four up. The two numbers belong together, always in that order, and nobody should swap one of them for something else halfway through a trip.

### A fixed bundle: the tuple

A **[[tuple|tuple-word]]** is an ordered group of values that cannot be changed after it is made. You write it with commas. The round brackets around it are usually optional, but people often add them to make it easier to see:

```python
point = (3, 4)
print(point[0])     # 3
print(len(point))   # 2
```

A tuple has positions counted from 0, like a list, so `point[0]` is the first value and `len` counts them. The difference: a list can grow and change, and a tuple [[never changes|immutable-tuple]]. `point[0] = 5` stops with `TypeError: 'tuple' object does not support item assignment`.

That makes a tuple the natural choice for a *fixed bundle* of values that belong together: a coordinate, an `(x, y, z)`, or a function giving back two results.

Here is the seat example from last lesson, keeping each row and seat as a pair instead of one string:

```python
pairs = [(r, c) for r in range(2) for c in range(3)]
print(pairs)
# [(0, 0), (0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 0), (1, 1), (1, 2)]
```

### Unpacking: taking a tuple apart

**Unpacking** means pulling a tuple (or a list) apart into separate names in one go. Put names on the left of `=`, separated by commas, one per value:

```python
point = (3, 4)
x, y = point
print(x)    # 3
print(y)    # 4
```

The first value goes into the first name, the second into the second. You did this in the basics without the word for it: `for key, value in prices.items()` unpacks each pair.

The right-hand side can be written out directly, which gives a neat way to [[swap two variables|swap]]:

```python
a, b = 1, 2
a, b = b, a
print(a, b)    # 2 1
```

### Star unpacking: "and all the rest"

A star `*` in front of a name means "put all the leftover values here, as a list". Read `*rest` as "star rest" or "everything else":

```python
first, *rest = [10, 20, 30, 40]
print(first)   # 10
print(rest)    # [20, 30, 40]
```

`first` gets one value. `rest` gets a list of everything left over. If there is nothing left over, `rest` is an empty list `[]`.

### Returning two things from a function

A function can only `return` one value. But that one value can be a tuple. So to "return two things", return them with a comma between them, and the caller unpacks them:

```python
def div_mod(a, b):
    return a // b, a % b

q, r = div_mod(17, 5)
print(q, r)    # 3 2
```

`return a // b, a % b` gives back the tuple `(3, 2)`: 17 whole-number divided by 5 is 3, and the remainder is 2. Then `q, r = ...` unpacks it into two names.

### enumerate: numbering as you go

Often you want each item *and* its position. The built-in function **`enumerate`** gives you both, as a pair `(position, item)`. Give it `start=1` to count from 1 instead of 0:

```python
names = ["ada", "lin", "sam"]
print(list(enumerate(names, start=1)))
# [(1, 'ada'), (2, 'lin'), (3, 'sam')]
```

`list(...)` is only there so you can see all the pairs at once. In a loop, you unpack each pair straight into two names:

```python
for i, name in enumerate(names, start=1):
    print(i, name)
# 1 ada
# 2 lin
# 3 sam
```

That also works in a comprehension: `[f"{i}: {name}" for i, name in enumerate(names, start=1)]` gives `['1: ada', '2: lin', '3: sam']`.

### zip: walking two lists side by side

The built-in **`zip`** takes two lists and pairs them up, first with first, second with second, like the teeth of a [[zip fastener|zipper]]:

```python
names = ["ada", "lin", "sam"]
scores = [91, 78, 85]
print(list(zip(names, scores)))
# [('ada', 91), ('lin', 78), ('sam', 85)]
```

And `dict(...)` turns a list of pairs into a dictionary, with the first of each pair as the key:

```python
print(dict(zip(names, scores)))
# {'ada': 91, 'lin': 78, 'sam': 85}
```

**Watch out:** three mistakes people really make here.

- `for i in range(len(names)): print(names[i])` works, but it is clumsy and easy to get wrong. When you need the position, use `enumerate`.
- `zip` stops at the *shortest* list, silently. `zip([1, 2, 3], ["a", "b"])` gives only two pairs, and the `3` is lost without a word. If the lists should be the same length, write `zip(a, b, strict=True)`, which raises a `ValueError` when [[they are not|zip-strict]].
- A tuple with one value needs a comma after it: `(5,)`. Without the comma, `(5)` is the number 5 in brackets, not a tuple at all.

::: context tuple-word Where the name comes from
Maths has words for groups of a fixed size: a pair, a triple, a quadruple, a quintuple. A tuple is the general word, "a group of any fixed size". Some people say "tupple", to rhyme with "couple", and some say "toople". Both are fine. Python writes an empty tuple as `()`, and you can turn a list into a tuple with `tuple([1, 2])`, which gives `(1, 2)`.
:::

::: context immutable-tuple Why a value that cannot change is useful
If you hand a list to some other code, that code could add or remove items, and your list changes under you. A tuple cannot, so everyone can share it safely. There is a second reason: because a tuple never changes, Python can use it as a dict key or put it in a set, and a list cannot be used either way. So `{(0, 0): "start", (3, 4): "goal"}` is a dict keyed by map coordinates, which is a common way to store grids and maps.
:::

::: context swap Swapping without a spare cup
To swap the drinks in two cups, you need a third, empty cup: pour A into the spare, B into A, then the spare into B. Most languages swap two variables the same way, with a temporary variable. In Python, `a, b = b, a` first builds the tuple `(b, a)` from the old values, and only then unpacks it into `a` and `b`. The tuple is the spare cup, and Python handles it for you.
:::

::: context zipper Why it is called zip
A zip fastener on a jacket has two rows of teeth. As you pull the slider, it locks one tooth from the left with one tooth from the right, then the next pair, and so on. Python's `zip` does the same with two lists.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">names</text>
  <text x="20" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">scores</text>
  <rect x="90" y="12" width="60" height="28" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="170" y="12" width="60" height="28" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="250" y="12" width="60" height="28" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="120" y="31" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"ada"</text>
  <text x="200" y="31" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"lin"</text>
  <text x="280" y="31" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"sam"</text>
  <rect x="90" y="112" width="60" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="170" y="112" width="60" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="250" y="112" width="60" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="120" y="131" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">91</text>
  <text x="200" y="131" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">78</text>
  <text x="280" y="131" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">85</text>
  <rect x="85" y="58" width="70" height="36" rx="8" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="165" y="58" width="70" height="36" rx="8" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="245" y="58" width="70" height="36" rx="8" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="120" y="81" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">pair 1</text>
  <text x="200" y="81" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">pair 2</text>
  <text x="280" y="81" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">pair 3</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="40" x2="120" y2="58" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="200" y1="40" x2="200" y2="58" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="280" y1="40" x2="280" y2="58" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="94" x2="120" y2="112" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="200" y1="94" x2="200" y2="112" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="280" y1="94" x2="280" y2="112" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context zip-strict A quiet loss is the worst kind
The `strict=True` option was added in Python 3.10, because silent truncation caused real bugs. Picture two lists from a test stand: the times of each reading, and the readings. If one sensor dropped a sample, the lists differ in length by one. Plain `zip` would pair them up, stop early, and every later reading would sit next to the wrong time, with no error at all. Engineers prefer a program that stops loudly over one that carries on with wrong data, because a loud stop gets looked at and fixed.
:::
--- task
Write four functions.

- `min_max(nums)` returns a tuple `(smallest, largest)`. Use `min` and `max`, and return them with a comma between them. For example, `min_max([4, -2, 9, 0])` gives `(-2, 9)`.
- `numbered(lines)` returns a list of strings like `"1. first"`, `"2. second"`, numbering from 1. Use `enumerate(lines, start=1)` in a comprehension, and the f-string `f"{i}. {line}"`. For example, `numbered(["wake", "code", "sleep"])` gives `["1. wake", "2. code", "3. sleep"]`.
- `scoreboard(names, scores)` returns a dict mapping each name to its score. Use `dict(zip(...))`. For example, `scoreboard(["ada", "lin"], [91, 78])` gives `{"ada": 91, "lin": 78}`.
- `head_tail(items)` returns a tuple `(first_item, list_of_the_rest)`. Use star unpacking, `first, *rest = items`, then return `first, rest`. For example, `head_tail([10, 20, 30])` gives `(10, [20, 30])`.
--- starter
def min_max(nums):
    pass


def numbered(lines):
    pass


def scoreboard(names, scores):
    pass


def head_tail(items):
    pass
--- solution
def min_max(nums):
    return min(nums), max(nums)


def numbered(lines):
    return [f"{i}. {line}" for i, line in enumerate(lines, start=1)]


def scoreboard(names, scores):
    return dict(zip(names, scores))


def head_tail(items):
    first, *rest = items
    return first, rest
--- hint
`return a, b` returns the tuple `(a, b)`. So `min_max` is one line: `return min(nums), max(nums)`.
--- hint
`enumerate(lines, start=1)` gives `(1, line)`, `(2, line)` and so on. Unpack them in the comprehension, `for i, line in enumerate(lines, start=1)`, and put `f"{i}. {line}"` at the front.
--- hint
`scoreboard` is `return dict(zip(names, scores))`. In `head_tail`, `first, *rest = items` puts the first item in `first` and a list of the others in `rest`; then `return first, rest`.
--- check case | min_max returns a (smallest, largest) tuple
min_max([4, -2, 9, 0])
=> (-2, 9)
--- check case | min_max of one number
min_max([7])
=> (7, 7)
--- check case | numbered counts from 1
numbered(["wake", "code", "sleep"])
=> ["1. wake", "2. code", "3. sleep"]
--- check case | numbered of nothing is an empty list
numbered([])
=> []
--- check case | scoreboard pairs names with scores
scoreboard(["ada", "lin"], [91, 78])
=> {"ada": 91, "lin": 78}
--- check case | head_tail splits the first item from the rest
head_tail([10, 20, 30])
=> (10, [20, 30])
--- check case | head_tail of a single item has an empty rest
head_tail(["only"])
=> ("only", [])

+++ practice | Three numbers in one tuple
--- task
Write `summary(readings)`. It returns a tuple of three values: how many readings there are, their total, and their average (the total divided by how many). Return all three at once, with commas between them. You can assume `readings` is not empty.

For example, `summary([4, 8, 6])` gives `(3, 18, 6.0)`.
--- starter
def summary(readings):
    return [len(readings), sum(readings)]
--- solution
def summary(readings):
    count = len(readings)
    total = sum(readings)
    return count, total, total / count
--- hint
`return a, b, c` hands back the tuple `(a, b, c)`. A list, with square brackets, is not a tuple.
--- hint
Work out `count` with `len` and `total` with `sum`, then return `count, total, total / count`.
--- check case | Count, total and average
summary([4, 8, 6])
=> (3, 18, 6.0)
--- check case | One reading
summary([5])
=> (1, 5, 5.0)
--- check case | Negative readings
summary([-2, 2, -6])
=> (3, -6, -2.0)
--- check test | The answer is a tuple
type(summary([1, 2])) is tuple

+++ practice | How much did each change?
--- task
Write `changes(before, after)`. Both are lists of the same length: `before[0]` goes with `after[0]`, and so on. Return a list of how much each value changed, `after` minus `before`, in order. Walk the two lists side by side with `zip` in one comprehension, unpacking each pair into two names.

For example, `changes([10, 20, 30], [12, 15, 30])` gives `[2, -5, 0]`.
--- starter
def changes(before, after):
    return [b - a for a in before for b in after]
--- solution
def changes(before, after):
    return [b - a for a, b in zip(before, after)]
--- hint
Two `for`s pair every value with every other value, which is too many. `zip` pairs first with first, second with second.
--- hint
Loop with `for a, b in zip(before, after)` and put `b - a` at the front.
--- check case | Up, down and unchanged
changes([10, 20, 30], [12, 15, 30])
=> [2, -5, 0]
--- check case | One pair
changes([1], [4])
=> [3]
--- check case | Empty lists
changes([], [])
=> []

+++ practice | Where is each name?
--- task
Write `positions(names)`. It returns a dict from each name to its position in the list, counting from **1**. Use `enumerate` with `start=1` inside a single dict comprehension. If a name appears more than once, its **later** position wins, because a later key replaces an earlier one.

For example, `positions(["ada", "lin", "sam"])` gives `{"ada": 1, "lin": 2, "sam": 3}`.
--- starter
def positions(names):
    return {name: 0 for name in names}
--- solution
def positions(names):
    return {name: i for i, name in enumerate(names, start=1)}
--- hint
`enumerate(names, start=1)` hands out pairs `(1, "ada")`, `(2, "lin")` and so on. Unpack each pair into two names in the `for`.
--- hint
In the dict comprehension, the key is the name and the value is the position: `{name: i for i, name in enumerate(names, start=1)}`.
--- check case | Positions counted from 1
positions(["ada", "lin", "sam"])
=> {"ada": 1, "lin": 2, "sam": 3}
--- check case | A repeated name keeps its later position
positions(["x", "y", "x"])
=> {"x": 3, "y": 2}
--- check case | No names
positions([])
=> {}

+++ practice | The header and the rows
--- task
A table arrives as a list of lines: the first line is the header, and the rest are data rows. Write `split_table(lines)`. It returns a tuple `(header, rows)`, where `rows` is a list of the remaining lines. Use star unpacking.

There is one awkward case: if `lines` is empty, there is no header at all. Then return `(None, [])` instead of stopping with an error.

For example, `split_table(["name,score", "ada,91", "lin,78"])` gives `("name,score", ["ada,91", "lin,78"])`, and `split_table(["name,score"])` gives `("name,score", [])`.
--- starter
def split_table(lines):
    header, *rows = lines
    return header, rows
--- solution
def split_table(lines):
    if not lines:
        return None, []
    header, *rows = lines
    return header, rows
--- hint
Try the starter with an empty list. Unpacking needs at least one value for `header`, so it stops with a `ValueError`.
--- hint
Check for the empty list first. An empty list counts as false, so `if not lines:` catches it, and you can `return None, []` right there.
--- check case | Header and rows
split_table(["name,score", "ada,91", "lin,78"])
=> ("name,score", ["ada,91", "lin,78"])
--- check case | A header with no rows
split_table(["name,score"])
=> ("name,score", [])
--- check case | No lines at all
split_table([])
=> (None, [])

+++ practice | Debug: the swap that lost a value
--- task
`smaller_first(a, b)` should return a tuple of its two numbers with the smaller one first. `smaller_first(1, 9)` gives `(1, 9)`, which is right.

The bug: `smaller_first(5, 2)` gives `(2, 2)` instead of `(2, 5)`. The 5 disappears. Fix the swap.
--- starter
def smaller_first(a, b):
    if a > b:
        a = b
        b = a
    return a, b
--- solution
def smaller_first(a, b):
    if a > b:
        a, b = b, a
    return a, b
--- hint
Follow the values by hand with `a = 5` and `b = 2`. After the line `a = b`, what is left in `a`? What does `b = a` then copy?
--- hint
Python can swap two names in one line, with a tuple on the right-hand side, so neither value is lost halfway.
--- check case | Swaps when the first is bigger
smaller_first(5, 2)
=> (2, 5)
--- check case | Leaves them alone when already in order
smaller_first(1, 9)
=> (1, 9)
--- check case | Equal numbers
smaller_first(4, 4)
=> (4, 4)
--- check case | Negative numbers
smaller_first(0, -3)
=> (-3, 0)

+++ practice | The fastest lap
--- task
A test car's lap times, in seconds, are in a list, in the order they were driven. Laps are numbered from 1. Write `fastest_lap(times)`. It returns a tuple `(lap_number, time)` for the fastest lap, the smallest time. If two laps tie, return the **earlier** one. If there are no laps, return `None`.

Use `enumerate(times, start=1)` in a `for` loop. For example, `fastest_lap([62.5, 60.1, 61.0, 60.1])` gives `(2, 60.1)`.
--- starter
def fastest_lap(times):
    best = min(times)
    return 1, best
--- solution
def fastest_lap(times):
    best = None
    for lap, time in enumerate(times, start=1):
        if best is None or time < best[1]:
            best = (lap, time)
    return best
--- hint
Keep the best lap so far in a variable that starts as `None`. Walk the laps with `for lap, time in enumerate(times, start=1):`.
--- hint
Replace the best when there is no best yet, or when this time is smaller than the best time. Use `<`, not `<=`, so a tie keeps the earlier lap. The best can be stored as the tuple `(lap, time)`, and `best[1]` is its time.
--- check case | The fastest lap and its number
fastest_lap([62.5, 60.1, 61.0, 60.9])
=> (2, 60.1)
--- check case | A tie keeps the earlier lap
fastest_lap([62.5, 60.1, 61.0, 60.1])
=> (2, 60.1)
--- check case | The first lap can be the fastest
fastest_lap([59.0, 60.0])
=> (1, 59.0)
--- check case | One lap
fastest_lap([70.2])
=> (1, 70.2)
--- check case | No laps gives None
fastest_lap([])
=> None

=== py2-03 | String methods and f-string formatting
--- teach
Last lesson you used an f-string to build labels like `"1. wake"`. This lesson goes deeper into strings in two parts. First, the string methods you will reach for all the time, to cut text up and glue it back together. Second, how an f-string can *format* a value, not only insert it: two decimal places, a thousands comma, neat columns.

Think of a label maker and a pair of scissors. The scissors cut a long strip of text into pieces. The label maker prints text neatly, the same width every time, so a list of labels lines up.

## Part 1: string methods

You know `strip`, `lower`, `upper` and `split` from the basics. Here are the rest of the everyday toolkit, one at a time.

### split with a separator

`split()` with nothing in the brackets cuts at spaces. Put a string in the brackets, and it cuts at that string instead. The piece you cut at is the **separator**:

```python
print("a,b,c".split(","))       # ['a', 'b', 'c']
print("a,b,,c".split(","))      # ['a', 'b', '', 'c']
```

Two commas in a row leave an empty string `''` between them. And you can chain methods, left to right:

```python
s = "  Launch Window: 14:05  "
print(s.strip())                  # Launch Window: 14:05
print(s.strip().split(": "))      # ['Launch Window', '14:05']
```

First `strip()` removes the spaces at both ends, then `split(": ")` cuts at colon-space.

### join: the opposite of split

`sep.join(pieces)` glues a list of strings together, with `sep` between each pair. Read `"-".join(parts)` as "join the parts, with a dash between them". Notice that the method belongs to the *separator*, not to the list:

```python
print("-".join(["2026", "09", "25"]))    # 2026-09-25
print("".join(["a", "b", "c"]))          # abc
```

An empty separator `""` glues the pieces with nothing between them.

`join` accepts any [[iterable|iterable-join]] of strings, not only a list. That includes a comprehension written *without* its square brackets:

```python
print("".join(c.upper() for c in "abc"))    # ABC
```

That bracket-less form is called a **generator expression**. For now, treat it as a comprehension that `join` reads one item at a time; the advanced course explains what it really is.

### Asking questions about a string

Some methods answer yes or no, with `True` or `False`:

```python
print("report.csv".endswith(".csv"))   # True
print("abc123".isalnum())              # True
print("42".isdigit())                  # True
print("4.2".isdigit())                 # False
```

- `endswith(x)` asks "does the string end with x?"
- `isalnum()` asks "is every character a letter or a digit?" (alnum is short for [[alphanumeric|alphanumeric]]). On one character, `"a".isalnum()` is `True` and `"!".isalnum()` is `False`.
- `isdigit()` asks "is every character a digit 0 to 9?" The dot in `"4.2"` is not a digit, so the answer is `False`.

### Changing a string, the copy way

```python
print("hello".replace("l", "L"))       # heLLo
print("ada lovelace".title())          # Ada Lovelace
print("key=value=x".partition("="))    # ('key', '=', 'value=x')
```

- `replace(old, new)` swaps every `old` for `new`.
- `title()` gives each word a capital first letter.
- `partition(sep)` cuts at the *first* `sep` only, and gives back a tuple of three: the part before, the separator itself, and the part after.

Strings are **[[immutable|immutable-string]]**: they can never be changed. Every method gives back a *new* string. So `s.upper()` on a line of its own does nothing useful. You must keep the result: `s = s.upper()`.

## Part 2: formatting with f-strings

### The format spec

Inside an f-string's curly braces, after the value, a colon `:` starts a **format spec**: instructions for how to show the value. Read `{price:.2f}` as "price, shown with 2 decimal places":

```python
price = 1234.5
print(f"{price:.2f}")     # 1234.50
print(f"{price:,.2f}")    # 1,234.50
```

- `.2f` (read "point 2 f") means "a decimal number (`f` for [[fixed-point|rounding]]) with 2 places after the point".
- A comma `,` before it means "put a comma between each group of thousands".

### Width and alignment

A number before the rest is a **width**: the smallest number of characters to fill. Extra room is filled with spaces. The symbols `<`, `>` and `^` choose where the value sits in that room:

```python
print(f"{'ab':<5}|")    # ab   |
print(f"{'ab':>5}|")    #    ab|
print(f"{'ab':^6}|")    #   ab  |
```

The `|` is only there so you can see where the spaces end. `'ab'` is two letters, so width 5 leaves three spaces: after it, before it, or split around it. Read `<` as "push left", `>` as "push right" and `^` as "center". Numbers are right-aligned unless you say otherwise:

```python
print(f"{42:5d}|")      #    42|
print(f"{7:03d}")       # 007
```

- `d` means "a whole number" (d for decimal digits).
- `5d` is "whole number, width 5". 42 has two digits, so three spaces go in front.
- `03d` is "whole number, width 3, padded with zeros instead of spaces".

One more type. Inside a format spec, `%` does not mean remainder. It means "show as a percentage", and it multiplies by 100 for you:

```python
print(f"{0.256:.1%}")   # 25.6%
```

### Putting the spec in order

When you use several parts together, they go in this order: **align, width, comma, precision, type**. So `>12,.2f` reads "push right, width 12, with commas, 2 decimal places, as a decimal number":

```python
total = 2500.0
print(f"[{total:>12,.2f}]")    # [    2,500.00]
```

`2,500.00` is 8 characters, so 4 spaces go in front to make 12.

Lining values up in [[fixed-width columns|columns]] is how you make a text report readable:

```python
for name, qty in [("tea", 3), ("biscuits", 12)]:
    print(f"{name:<10}{qty:>4}")
# tea          3
# biscuits    12
```

Each name fills a column 10 wide, pushed left. Each quantity fills a column 4 wide, pushed right. So the numbers line up on their last digit.

**Watch out:** two mistakes people really make.

- `split()` with nothing in the brackets cuts at any run of spaces and drops empty pieces: `"a  b".split()` is `['a', 'b']`. `split(" ")` cuts at every single space and keeps the empty strings: `"a  b".split(" ")` is `['a', '', 'b']`.
- A width is a *minimum*, not a maximum. A value longer than the width is never cut short. `f"{'watermelons':<10}"` still shows all 11 letters, and the columns after it are pushed along by one.

::: context iterable-join Anything you can loop over
An iterable is anything a `for` loop can walk through one piece at a time: a list, a tuple, a string, a `range`, a set, a dict. `join` walks through what you give it and glues each piece in. The pieces must all be strings, though. `"-".join([1, 2])` stops with `TypeError`, because 1 and 2 are numbers. Convert them first: `"-".join(str(n) for n in [1, 2])` gives `"1-2"`.
:::

::: context alphanumeric Letters and numbers
Alphanumeric is "alphabet" plus "numeric": letters and digits, nothing else. Spaces, dots, dashes and punctuation are not alphanumeric. Python's idea of a letter is wider than A to Z: `"é".isalnum()` is `True` too, because Python strings are Unicode, a standard that covers the letters of nearly every written language. That matters in real programs, where names are not only in English.
:::

::: context immutable-string Why strings never change
Immutable means "cannot be changed". Because a string can never change, Python can safely share one copy of it between many variables, and it can use strings as dict keys and set items. The cost is that every "change" makes a new string. For everyday text that is fast enough. When a program builds a very long string from thousands of small pieces, the usual trick is to collect the pieces in a list and `join` them once at the end.
:::

::: context rounding Why 2.675 shows as 2.67
The `f` stands for fixed-point: a fixed number of digits after the point. Formatting rounds to that many places. But computers store decimals in binary, and most decimals cannot be stored exactly, the way 1/3 cannot be written exactly as 0.333... So `2.675` is really stored as a tiny bit less than 2.675, and `f"{2.675:.2f}"` gives `2.67`, not `2.68`. For money and for flight calculations, engineers keep this in mind and round only at the very end, for display.
:::

::: context columns Readouts that do not jump around
Fixed-width columns are everywhere in mission control. On a telemetry display, each value (a tank pressure, a temperature, a voltage) sits in its own column, right-aligned, with the same number of decimals. As the numbers update many times a second, the digits stay in place, and the eye can spot a change without re-reading every line.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="15" width="130" height="90" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <rect x="150" y="15" width="52" height="90" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="85" y="12" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">width 10, left</text>
  <text x="176" y="12" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">width 4</text>
  <text x="28" y="45" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44">tea</text>
  <text x="196" y="45" font-size="14" text-anchor="end" fill="#1d6fd1">3</text>
  <text x="28" y="80" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44">biscuits</text>
  <text x="196" y="80" font-size="14" text-anchor="end" fill="#1d6fd1">12</text>
  <text x="222" y="45" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">numbers pushed</text>
  <text x="222" y="62" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">right, so their</text>
  <text x="222" y="79" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">last digits line up</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write two functions.

`format_row(item, qty, price)` returns one line of a receipt, as a single f-string with three parts:

1. `item`, left-aligned in a column 10 wide: `{item:<10}`
2. `qty`, right-aligned in a column 4 wide: `{qty:>4}`
3. the line total `qty * price`, right-aligned in a column 12 wide, with a thousands comma and 2 decimal places: the spec `>12,.2f`

For example, `format_row("apple", 3, 0.5)` gives `"apple        3        1.50"`.

`slugify(title)` turns a title into a URL slug: all lowercase, words joined by single dashes `-`, with no dash at either end. Every character that is not a letter or a digit counts as a gap between words. For example, `slugify("  Hello, World!  ")` gives `"hello-world"`. Do it in two steps:

1. Build a string `kept` where each character `c` of `title.lower()` stays as it is if `c.isalnum()`, and becomes a space `" "` otherwise. Use `"".join(...)` with the conditional expression `c if c.isalnum() else " "` from lesson py2-01b.
2. Return `"-".join(kept.split())`.
--- starter
def format_row(item, qty, price):
    return f"{item} {qty} {qty * price}"


def slugify(title):
    return title.lower()
--- solution
def format_row(item, qty, price):
    return f"{item:<10}{qty:>4}{qty * price:>12,.2f}"


def slugify(title):
    kept = "".join(c if c.isalnum() else " " for c in title.lower())
    return "-".join(kept.split())
--- hint
`format_row` puts the three specs side by side with nothing between them: `f"{item:<10}{qty:>4}{...}"`. The spec for the total is `>12,.2f`: right-align, width 12, thousands comma, 2 decimals.
--- hint
For `slugify`, the first step replaces every character that is not `isalnum()` with a space: `c if c.isalnum() else " "` for each `c` in `title.lower()`, all inside `"".join( )`. Then the problem becomes "join the words with dashes".
--- hint
`"-".join(kept.split())` joins the words with dashes. `split()` with nothing in the brackets already ignores runs of spaces and spaces at the ends, so there are no empty words and no dash at either end.
--- check case | format_row lines up the three columns
format_row("apple", 3, 0.5)
=> "apple     " + "   3" + "        1.50"
--- check case | Large totals get a thousands separator
format_row("laptop", 2, 1250.0)
=> "laptop    " + "   2" + "    2,500.00"
--- check case | A long name is not cut short
format_row("watermelons", 1, 3.0)
=> "watermelons" + "   1" + "        3.00"
--- check case | slugify lowercases and joins words with hyphens
slugify("  Hello, World!  ")
=> "hello-world"
--- check case | Punctuation between words becomes one hyphen
slugify("Python 3.13 -- is out!")
=> "python-3-13-is-out"
--- check case | A title with no letters or digits gives an empty slug
slugify("!!! ---")
=> ""

+++ practice | Dollars and cents

Write `money(amount)`. It returns the amount as a string that starts with a dollar sign `$`, has a comma between each group of thousands, and always shows exactly 2 decimal places. Use one f-string with a format spec.

For example, `money(1234.5)` gives `"$1,234.50"`, and `money(0)` gives `"$0.00"`.
--- starter
def money(amount):
    return f"${amount}"
--- solution
def money(amount):
    return f"${amount:,.2f}"
--- hint
After the value inside the curly braces, a colon starts the format spec. The dollar sign goes outside the braces, as plain text.
--- hint
The spec is a comma for the thousands, then `.2f` for two decimal places: `{amount:,.2f}`.
--- check case | Thousands comma and two decimals
money(1234.5)
=> "$1,234.50"
--- check case | Zero still shows two decimals

+++ practice | A clock with leading zeros
--- task
Write `clock(hours, minutes, seconds)`. It returns the time as `HH:MM:SS`, each part exactly two digits wide, padded with a zero in front when needed. Use one f-string with the whole-number spec `02d` for each part.

For example, `clock(7, 5, 9)` gives `"07:05:09"`, and `clock(23, 59, 0)` gives `"23:59:00"`.
--- starter
def clock(hours, minutes, seconds):
    return f"{hours}:{minutes}:{seconds}"
--- solution
def clock(hours, minutes, seconds):
    return f"{hours:02d}:{minutes:02d}:{seconds:02d}"
--- hint
`03d` means "whole number, width 3, padded with zeros". What width do you need here?
--- hint
Put `:02d` after each of the three values inside their curly braces, and keep the colons between them as plain text.
--- check case | Single digits get a zero in front

=> "07:05:09"
--- check case | Two digits stay as they are

=> "23:59:00"
