+++ practice | Initials with dots
--- task
Write `initials(full_name)`. It returns the capital first letter of each word in the name, each followed by a dot, all glued together. Words are separated by spaces, and extra spaces must not matter. Use `split()`, `join` and the string index `word[0]` from the comprehensions lesson.

For example, `initials("ada lovelace")` gives `"A.L."`, `initials("  grace   brewster hopper ")` gives `"G.B.H."`, and `initials("")` gives `""`.
--- starter
def initials(full_name):
    return full_name[0].upper() + "."
--- solution
def initials(full_name):
    return "".join(word[0].upper() + "." for word in full_name.split())
--- hint
`split()` with nothing in the brackets gives the words and ignores extra spaces. For each word you want its first letter, in capitals, plus a dot.
--- hint
Glue the pieces with an empty separator: `"".join(... for word in full_name.split())`, with `word[0].upper() + "."` at the front.
--- check case | Two words
initials("ada lovelace")
=> "A.L."
--- check case | Extra spaces do not matter
initials("  grace   brewster hopper ")
=> "G.B.H."
--- check case | One word
initials("Linus")
=> "L."
--- check case | An empty name gives an empty string
initials("")
=> ""

+++ practice | A setting with an equals sign in it
--- task
A settings line looks like `key = value`. Write `parse_setting(line)`. It returns a tuple `(key, value)`, both with the spaces at their ends removed. Cut at the **first** `=` only, because a value may contain `=` itself. If the line has no `=` at all, return the stripped line as the key and `""` as the value. Use `partition`.

For example, `parse_setting(" mode = fast ")` gives `("mode", "fast")`, `parse_setting("query=a=b")` gives `("query", "a=b")`, and `parse_setting("verbose")` gives `("verbose", "")`.
--- starter
def parse_setting(line):
    key, value = line.split("=")
    return key.strip(), value.strip()
--- solution
def parse_setting(line):
    key, sep, value = line.partition("=")
    return key.strip(), value.strip()
--- hint
Try the starter on `"query=a=b"` and on `"verbose"`. `split("=")` makes three pieces in the first case and one in the second, so the unpacking fails.
--- hint
`partition("=")` always gives exactly three pieces: before the first `=`, the `=` itself (or `""` if there is none), and everything after. Unpack all three, and ignore the middle one.
--- check case | Spaces around the key and value are removed
parse_setting(" mode = fast ")
=> ("mode", "fast")
--- check case | Only the first = splits the line
parse_setting("query=a=b")
=> ("query", "a=b")
--- check case | No = at all
parse_setting("verbose")
=> ("verbose", "")
--- check case | An empty value
parse_setting("name=")
=> ("name", "")

+++ practice | Debug: counting words
--- task
`word_count(text)` should return how many words are in `text`, where words are separated by one or more spaces. `word_count("go for launch")` gives `3`, which is right.

The bug: `word_count("go  for launch")`, with two spaces, gives `4`, and `word_count("")` gives `1` instead of `0`. Fix it.
--- starter
def word_count(text):
    return len(text.split(" "))
--- solution
def word_count(text):
    return len(text.split())
--- hint
What does `"a  b".split(" ")` give? Look at the lesson's "Watch out" about the two kinds of `split`.
--- hint
`split()` with nothing in the brackets cuts at any run of spaces and never keeps empty pieces.
--- check case | Single spaces
word_count("go for launch")
=> 3
--- check case | Runs of spaces count as one gap
word_count("go  for   launch")
=> 3
--- check case | Spaces at the ends do not make words
word_count("  go  ")
=> 1
--- check case | Empty text has no words
word_count("")
=> 0

+++ practice | A fuel report in columns
--- task
`tanks` is a dict from each tank's name to the kilograms of fuel in it, like `{"LOX": 1500.0, "RP-1": 500.0}`. Write `fuel_report(tanks)`. It returns a **list** of strings, one row per tank, in the dict's order. Each row has three columns side by side:

1. the name, pushed left in a column 8 wide;
2. the kilograms, pushed right in a column 10 wide, with a thousands comma and 1 decimal place;
3. that tank's share of the total fuel, pushed right in a column 7 wide, as a percentage with 1 decimal place.

For example, `fuel_report({"LOX": 1500.0, "RP-1": 500.0})` gives:

```
["LOX        1,500.0  75.0%",
 "RP-1         500.0  25.0%"]
```

You can assume there is at least one tank and the total is more than zero.
--- starter
def fuel_report(tanks):
    return [f"{name} {kg}" for name, kg in tanks.items()]
--- solution
def fuel_report(tanks):
    total = sum(kg for name, kg in tanks.items())
    return [f"{name:<8}{kg:>10,.1f}{kg / total:>7.1%}" for name, kg in tanks.items()]
--- hint
Work out the total first. `sum` accepts a comprehension without its square brackets, like `join` does, so you can add up the kilograms while looping over `tanks.items()`.
--- hint
The three specs are `<8` for the name, `>10,.1f` for the kilograms and `>7.1%` for the share. The share is `kg / total`: the `%` type multiplies by 100 for you.
--- check case | Two tanks in columns
fuel_report({"LOX": 1500.0, "RP-1": 500.0})
=> ["LOX     " + "   1,500.0" + "  75.0%", "RP-1    " + "     500.0" + "  25.0%"]
--- check case | One tank holds all the fuel
fuel_report({"N2O4": 12345.67})
=> ["N2O4    " + "  12,345.7" + " 100.0%"]
--- check case | A small share
fuel_report({"main": 999.0, "vernier": 1.0})
=> ["main    " + "     999.0" + "  99.9%", "vernier " + "       1.0" + "   0.1%"]

=== py2-04 | Dicts in depth: get, setdefault, Counter and defaultdict
--- teach
Last lesson you cut strings up and lined them up in columns. This lesson goes back to dictionaries, for the two jobs programs do more than almost any other: **counting** things and **grouping** things. Python has a tool for each.

Think of sorting the post in an office. For grouping, you have a wall of pigeonholes, one per person, and you drop each letter in the right hole. For counting, you keep a tally sheet and add a mark next to a name each time a letter arrives. The awkward moment is the first letter for someone new: there is no pigeonhole yet, and no line on the tally sheet.

### get: reading with a fallback

You met `get` in the basics. `d[key]` stops with a [[KeyError|key-error]] when the key is missing. `d.get(key, default)` gives back `default` instead:

```python
stock = {"tea": 3}
print(stock.get("tea", 0))      # 3
print(stock.get("coffee", 0))   # 0
print(stock)                    # {'tea': 3}   (unchanged)
```

`get` only reads. It never adds the key.

### setdefault: read, and store if missing

`d.setdefault(key, default)` does a little more. If the key is missing, it *stores* the default under that key first. Then it gives back whatever is stored there:

```python
groups = {}
box = groups.setdefault("a", [])
print(box)       # []
print(groups)    # {'a': []}
```

That makes grouping short. Here each word goes into the list for its first letter:

```python
groups = {}
for word in ["apple", "avocado", "banana"]:
    groups.setdefault(word[0], []).append(word)
print(groups)    # {'a': ['apple', 'avocado'], 'b': ['banana']}
```

Step by step: for `"apple"`, the key `"a"` is missing, so `setdefault` stores a new empty list under `"a"` and gives it back, and `.append("apple")` puts the word in it. For `"avocado"`, the key `"a"` is already there, so `setdefault` gives back the same list, and the word is added to it.

### Importing tools from the standard library

Python comes with a large **[[standard library|standard-library]]**: a collection of ready-made tools that every Python install has. It is split into **modules**, one file of tools for each topic. The two tools for the rest of this lesson live in a module called `collections`.

To use a module you **import** it. There are two ways:

```python
import collections
c = collections.Counter("abc")

from collections import Counter
c = Counter("abc")
```

`import collections` brings in the whole module; you then write the module name, a dot, and the tool. `from collections import Counter` brings in that one name directly, so you can write `Counter` on its own. You can import several names at once, with commas: `from collections import Counter, defaultdict`. Imports go at the top of the file.

### defaultdict: a dict that fills in the gaps

A **`defaultdict`** does what `setdefault` does, without you having to say it every time. When you make one, you give it a [[function that makes the default|default-factory]]. The first time you use a missing key, it calls that function and stores the result:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

groups = defaultdict(list)
for word in ["apple", "avocado", "banana"]:
    groups[word[0]].append(word)
print(dict(groups))    # {'a': ['apple', 'avocado'], 'b': ['banana']}
```

`defaultdict(list)` means "when a key is missing, make a new empty list for it". Notice it is `list`, the name of the function, with no brackets. The defaultdict calls it for you later, each time it needs a fresh empty list.

A defaultdict is still a dict, but it [[prints differently|dict-family]]. `dict(groups)` turns it into a plain dict, which prints the normal way.

### Counter: a dict built for counting

A **`Counter`** counts things for you. Give it anything you can loop over, and it counts how many times each item appears:

```python
from collections import Counter

c = Counter("mississippi")
print(c["s"])    # 4
print(c["z"])    # 0   (missing keys count as zero, no KeyError)
```

Looping over a string gives its letters, so this counts letters. Give it a list of words and it counts words.

`most_common(n)` gives back the `n` most common items, as a list of `(item, count)` tuples, highest count first:

```python
print(c.most_common(2))    # [('i', 4), ('s', 4)]
```

When two items have the same count, they stay in the order in which they were [[first seen|insertion-order]]. In "mississippi", `i` appears before the first `s`, so `('i', 4)` comes before `('s', 4)`. That matters when you write checks that expect an exact order.

**Watch out:** three mistakes people really make.

- Reading a defaultdict with `groups[key]` *creates* the key, even if you only wanted to look. To ask "is it there?", use `key in groups`, which never creates anything.
- `d.setdefault(k, []).append(x)` works, but `d.get(k, []).append(x)` does nothing useful: `get` hands back a fresh list that is never stored in `d`, so the appended item is lost.
- A Counter or defaultdict is still a dict, but it prints differently. Convert with `dict(...)` when you return it to code that expects a plain dict.

::: context key-error The error for a missing key
`KeyError` is the exception Python raises when you ask a dict for a key it does not have. The message names the key: `stock["coffee"]` stops with `KeyError: 'coffee'`. In the basics you learned to catch exceptions with `try` and `except`. That works here too, but `get`, `setdefault`, `defaultdict` and `Counter` usually make the missing-key case so ordinary that there is no error to catch at all.
:::

::: context standard-library Batteries included
Python's makers describe it as "batteries included": a lot comes in the box. Besides `collections`, the standard library has `math` for square roots and trigonometry, `datetime` for dates and times, `json` for reading and writing data files, `csv` for spreadsheets, and many more. You will meet several of them later in this course. Before writing a tool yourself, it is worth checking whether the standard library already has one, because its tools are well tested and other programmers already know them.
:::

::: context default-factory Handing over a function
In Python a function is a value, like a number or a string. You can store it in a variable or pass it to another function without calling it. `list` with no brackets is the function itself; `list()` with brackets calls it and gives back `[]`. A defaultdict keeps the function and calls it each time it meets a new key. That way every key gets its *own* new list. The same trick works with other functions: `defaultdict(int)` gives `0` for a missing key, because `int()` gives back `0`, which makes a simple counter.
:::

::: context dict-family Same dict underneath
Print a defaultdict and you see `defaultdict(<class 'list'>, {'a': ['apple', 'avocado'], 'b': ['banana']})`, and a Counter prints as `Counter({'i': 4, 's': 4, 'p': 2, 'm': 1})`. Both are special kinds of dict: they do everything a dict does, plus a bit extra. Code that checks `type(x) is dict` will say no to them, though, which is why you convert with `dict(...)` before handing one back to code that wants a plain dict.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 140" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="120" y="15" width="120" height="36" rx="8" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="38" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">dict</text>
  <rect x="30" y="90" width="130" height="36" rx="8" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="95" y="113" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">defaultdict</text>
  <rect x="200" y="90" width="130" height="36" rx="8" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="265" y="113" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Counter</text>
  <line x1="95" y1="90" x2="160" y2="51" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="265" y1="90" x2="200" y2="51" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="95" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">+ fills missing keys</text>
  <text x="265" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">+ counts, most_common</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context insertion-order Dicts remember their order
Since Python 3.7, every dict keeps its keys in the order they were first added, and a Counter is a dict, so it does too. Python's documentation for `most_common` promises that items with equal counts come out in the order they were first met. So the tie between `i` and `s` is settled by which letter turned up first. Counting like this is common in engineering logs: a test team might count how often each error code appears in a night of telemetry, and look at the top few first.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. The starter already has the import line `from collections import Counter, defaultdict` at the top; keep it.

- `group_by_length(words)` returns a plain `dict` that maps each word length to the list of words of that length, in their original order. Use `groups = defaultdict(list)`, add each word with `groups[len(word)].append(word)`, and return `dict(groups)`. For example, `group_by_length(["hi", "yo", "hey", "a"])` gives `{2: ["hi", "yo"], 3: ["hey"], 1: ["a"]}`.
- `top_words(text, n)` returns the `n` most common words of `text`, compared in lowercase, as a list of `(word, count)` tuples. Make the words with `text.lower().split()`, count them with `Counter`, and use `most_common(n)`. For example, `top_words("the cat and The dog and THE end", 2)` gives `[("the", 3), ("and", 2)]`.
--- starter
from collections import Counter, defaultdict


def group_by_length(words):
    return {}


def top_words(text, n):
    return []
--- solution
from collections import Counter, defaultdict


def group_by_length(words):
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for word in words:
        groups[len(word)].append(word)
    return dict(groups)


def top_words(text, n):
    return Counter(text.lower().split()).most_common(n)
--- hint
`group_by_length` is the defaultdict grouping example, with `len(word)` as the key instead of `word[0]`. No `if` is needed: the defaultdict makes each new list for you.
--- hint
End `group_by_length` with `return dict(groups)` so the caller gets a plain dict, not a defaultdict.
--- hint
`Counter(some_list).most_common(n)` already returns a list of `(item, count)` tuples. So `top_words` is one line: `return Counter(text.lower().split()).most_common(n)`.
--- check case | group_by_length groups words by their length
group_by_length(["hi", "yo", "hey", "a"])
=> {2: ["hi", "yo"], 3: ["hey"], 1: ["a"]}
--- check test | group_by_length returns a plain dict
type(group_by_length(["a"])) is dict
?? Wrap the defaultdict in dict(...) before returning it.
--- check case | No words, no groups
group_by_length([])
=> {}
--- check case | top_words counts case-insensitively
top_words("the cat and The dog and THE end", 2)
=> [("the", 3), ("and", 2)]
--- check case | Ties keep the order the words first appeared
top_words("b a b a c", 3)
=> [("b", 2), ("a", 2), ("c", 1)]
--- check case | top_words of empty text is empty
top_words("", 5)
=> []

+++ practice | Count the fault codes
--- task
A night of telemetry produced a list of fault codes, like `["E7", "E2", "E7"]`. Write `code_counts(codes)`. It returns a **plain dict** from each code to how many times it appears. Use `Counter` from `collections` (import it at the top), and turn the result into a plain dict before returning it.

For example, `code_counts(["E7", "E2", "E7"])` gives `{"E7": 2, "E2": 1}`, and `code_counts([])` gives `{}`.
--- starter
def code_counts(codes):
    return {}
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def code_counts(codes):
    return dict(Counter(codes))
--- hint
Start the file with `from collections import Counter`. `Counter(codes)` counts every item of the list.
--- hint
A Counter prints differently from a plain dict. Wrap it in `dict(...)` before you return it.

+++ practice | Files by owner
--- task
`files` is a dict from each file name to the person who owns it, like `{"a.txt": "ada", "b.txt": "lin", "c.txt": "ada"}`. Write `by_owner(files)`. It returns a dict from each owner to the **list** of their file names, in the order the files appear in `files`. Use `setdefault` to make each owner's list the first time you meet them.

For that example it gives `{"ada": ["a.txt", "c.txt"], "lin": ["b.txt"]}`.
--- starter
def by_owner(files):
    groups = {}
    for name, owner in files.items():
        groups[owner] = [name]
    return groups
--- solution
def by_owner(files):
    groups = {}
    for name, owner in files.items():
        groups.setdefault(owner, []).append(name)
    return groups
--- hint
The starter replaces an owner's list every time, so only their last file is kept. You want to add to the list if it exists, and make it if it does not.
--- hint
`groups.setdefault(owner, [])` hands back the owner's list, making an empty one first if needed. Call `.append(name)` on what it hands back.
--- check case | Groups the files by owner, in order
by_owner({"a.txt": "ada", "b.txt": "lin", "c.txt": "ada"})
=> {"ada": ["a.txt", "c.txt"], "lin": ["b.txt"]}
--- check case | One owner with everything
by_owner({"x": "sam", "y": "sam", "z": "sam"})
=> {"sam": ["x", "y", "z"]}
--- check case | No files
by_owner({})
=> {}

+++ practice | The most common email domains
--- task
Write `top_domains(emails, n)`. For each email address, the **domain** is the part after the `@`, compared in lowercase. Return the `n` most common domains as a list of `(domain, count)` tuples, highest count first, with ties in the order the domains were first seen. Use `partition` from the string lesson and `Counter`.

For example, `top_domains(["ada@Orbit.org", "lin@mail.com", "sam@orbit.org"], 1)` gives `[("orbit.org", 2)]`.
--- starter
from collections import Counter


def top_domains(emails, n):
    return Counter(emails).most_common(n)
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def top_domains(emails, n):
    domains = [email.partition("@")[2].lower() for email in emails]
    return Counter(domains).most_common(n)
--- hint
First make a list of just the domains, then count that list.
--- hint
`email.partition("@")` gives a tuple of three pieces; the domain is the last one, at index 2. Add `.lower()` so `Orbit.org` and `orbit.org` count as the same.
--- check case | Counts domains, ignoring capitals
top_domains(["ada@Orbit.org", "lin@mail.com", "sam@orbit.org"], 1)
=> [("orbit.org", 2)]
--- check case | Ties keep the order they were first seen
top_domains(["a@x.io", "b@y.io", "c@y.io", "d@x.io", "e@z.io"], 3)
=> [("x.io", 2), ("y.io", 2), ("z.io", 1)]
--- check case | No emails
top_domains([], 3)
=> []

+++ practice | What is still missing?
--- task
A launch checklist says how many of each part are **needed**, and the store room says how many are **in stock**. Both are dicts from part name to a number. A part that is not in the stock dict at all counts as zero in stock.

Write `shortfall(needed, stock)`. It returns a dict from each part that is short to **how many more** are needed. Parts with enough stock are left out. Use `get` so a missing part never causes a `KeyError`.

For example, `shortfall({"bolt": 10, "valve": 2, "seal": 4}, {"bolt": 7, "seal": 9})` gives `{"bolt": 3, "valve": 2}`.
--- starter
def shortfall(needed, stock):
    return {part: n - stock[part] for part, n in needed.items() if stock[part] < n}
--- solution
def shortfall(needed, stock):
    return {part: n - stock.get(part, 0) for part, n in needed.items() if stock.get(part, 0) < n}
--- hint
Try the starter with a part that is not in `stock`, such as `"valve"`. `stock[part]` stops with `KeyError`.
--- hint
Replace both `stock[part]` with `stock.get(part, 0)`, so a missing part counts as zero.
--- check case | Short parts and how many more are needed
shortfall({"bolt": 10, "valve": 2, "seal": 4}, {"bolt": 7, "seal": 9})
=> {"bolt": 3, "valve": 2}
--- check case | A part with exactly enough is not short
shortfall({"bolt": 5}, {"bolt": 5})
=> {}
--- check case | A part stocked at zero
shortfall({"fuse": 3}, {"fuse": 0})
=> {"fuse": 3}
--- check case | Nothing needed
shortfall({}, {"bolt": 7})
=> {}

+++ practice | Debug: the groups that vanished
--- task
`group_by_initial(names)` should return a dict from each first letter to the list of names that start with it, in order. For `["ada", "alan", "lin"]` it should give `{"a": ["ada", "alan"], "l": ["lin"]}`.

The bug: it always returns `{}`, however many names you give it. Fix the one line that loses the names.
--- starter
def group_by_initial(names):
    groups = {}
    for name in names:
        groups.get(name[0], []).append(name)
    return groups
--- solution
def group_by_initial(names):
    groups = {}
    for name in names:
        groups.setdefault(name[0], []).append(name)
    return groups
--- hint
Does `get` ever store anything in the dict? Look at the lesson's "Watch out".
--- hint
`get` hands back a fresh empty list that is never put into `groups`, so the name is appended to a list that is thrown away. Use the method that stores the default before handing it back.
--- check case | Groups names by first letter
group_by_initial(["ada", "alan", "lin"])
=> {"a": ["ada", "alan"], "l": ["lin"]}
--- check case | Order within a group is kept
group_by_initial(["bo", "al", "bea", "bix"])

+++ practice | The faults that matter
--- task
Each line of a flight log has a level, a fault code and then a message, separated by spaces, like `"ERROR E42 valve stuck"`. The level is one of `INFO`, `WARN`, `ERROR` or `FATAL`.

Write `top_faults(lines, n)`. It counts the fault codes, but only on lines whose level is `ERROR` or `FATAL`, and returns the `n` most common as a list of `(code, count)` tuples, highest first, ties in the order first seen. Skip empty lines. Use `split()` and `Counter`.

For example:

```
top_faults(["ERROR E42 valve stuck", "INFO E01 all good",
            "FATAL E42 valve failed", "ERROR E07 low volts"], 2)
```

gives `[("E42", 2), ("E07", 1)]`. The `INFO` line is not counted.
--- starter
from collections import Counter


def top_faults(lines, n):
    return Counter(line.split()[1] for line in lines).most_common(n)
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def top_faults(lines, n):
    counts = Counter()
    for line in lines:
        words = line.split()
        if words and words[0] in ("ERROR", "FATAL"):
            counts[words[1]] += 1
    return counts.most_common(n)
--- hint
Walk the lines with a `for` loop. Split each line into words; the level is `words[0]` and the code is `words[1]`.
--- hint
An empty line splits into an empty list, which counts as false, so `if words and ...` skips it before you ever look at `words[0]`. To test the level, ask whether it is `in` a tuple of the two levels you want.
--- hint
A `Counter()` made with nothing in the brackets starts empty, and a missing key counts as zero, so `counts[code] += 1` works even the first time you see a code. End with `counts.most_common(n)`.
--- check case | Only ERROR and FATAL lines count
top_faults(["ERROR E42 valve stuck", "INFO E01 all good", "FATAL E42 valve failed", "ERROR E07 low volts"], 2)
=> [("E42", 2), ("E07", 1)]
--- check case | Empty lines are skipped
top_faults(["", "ERROR E1 a", "", "WARN E1 b"], 5)
=> [("E1", 1)]
--- check case | Ties keep the order first seen
top_faults(["FATAL B2 x", "ERROR A1 y", "ERROR A1 z", "FATAL B2 w"], 2)
=> [("B2", 2), ("A1", 2)]
--- check case | Nothing serious
top_faults(["INFO E1 fine", "WARN E2 hmm"], 3)
=> []

=== py2-05 | Sorting with key= and lambda
--- teach
Last lesson, `most_common` handed you counts that were already sorted, highest first. This lesson puts you in charge of sorting. You will choose *what* to sort by, and which way round.

Picture a box of library cards. You could line them up by title, by author, or by number of pages. The cards stay the same. Only the thing you look at on each card changes. In Python, that "thing you look at" is called the **key**.

## sorted and .sort

You met `sorted` in the basics. It gives back a **new** sorted list and leaves the old one alone. Lists also have a method, `.sort()`, that sorts the list **in place**: it rearranges the list itself and hands back `None`.

```python
temps = [18, 25, 11]
sorted(temps)      # [11, 18, 25]   a new list
temps              # [18, 25, 11]   unchanged

temps.sort()       # hands back None
temps              # [11, 18, 25]   the list itself changed
```

Both take two optional extra arguments that do most of the real work: `key` and `reverse`. You pass them by name, like `reverse=True`. An argument passed by name is called a **[[keyword argument|keyword-argument]]**: the parameter's name, an `=` sign, then the value.

## reverse=True: biggest first

`reverse=True` flips the order, so the biggest comes first:

```python
sorted([18, 25, 11], reverse=True)   # [25, 18, 11]
```

## key: what to sort by

Plain `sorted` on words compares them letter by letter, and every capital letter comes before every small letter. That surprises people:

```python
words = ["banana", "Kiwi", "apple"]
sorted(words)                    # ['Kiwi', 'apple', 'banana']  (capitals sort first)
```

The reason is that Python compares the [[number behind each character|char-codes]], and capitals have smaller numbers.

The **key** is a function Python calls on each item to get *the value to sort by*. The items themselves do not change. Python only uses the key's answers to decide the order, then hands back the original items in that order.

```python
sorted(words, key=str.lower)     # ['apple', 'banana', 'Kiwi']
sorted(words, key=len)           # ['Kiwi', 'apple', 'banana']
```

With `key=str.lower`, Python works out `"banana"`, `"kiwi"` and `"apple"`, sorts by those, and gives back the words as they were, capital K and all. `str` is the name of the string type, and `str.lower` is its `lower` method: `str.lower("Kiwi")` does the same as `"Kiwi".lower()`. With `key=len`, Python sorts by length: 4, 5, 6.

Notice there are no brackets after `len` or `str.lower`. You are handing over the [[function itself|functions-as-values]], for `sorted` to call on each item later. You are not calling it yourself.

## lambda: a small function with no name

Sometimes the key you want is not a function that exists yet. Say each person is a dict and you want to sort by age. You could write a whole `def` for it. For a one-off key there is a shorter way: a **lambda**, a small function with no name, written in one line.

```python
lambda p: p["age"]
```

Read it as "given `p`, hand back `p["age"]`". The word [[lambda|lambda-word]] starts it. The name before the colon is the parameter. The part after the colon is the answer. There is no `return` word: the answer is whatever comes after the colon. It does exactly the same job as this:

```python
def get_age(p):
    return p["age"]
```

Now sort with it:

```python
people = [{"name": "Lin", "age": 30}, {"name": "Ada", "age": 36}, {"name": "Sam", "age": 30}]
sorted(people, key=lambda p: p["age"])
# Lin 30, Sam 30, Ada 36
```

Python calls the lambda on each dict, gets 30, 36 and 30, and sorts by those numbers.

## Sorting by two things at once

What if two people have the same age? Make the key return a **tuple** (from py2-02). Tuples [[compare item by item|tuple-compare]]: they compare their first items, and only look at the second items when the first are equal.

```python
sorted(people, key=lambda p: (p["age"], p["name"]))
# Lin 30, Sam 30, Ada 36   (youngest first, then by name)
```

To sort one part the other way round, put a minus sign in front of it, as long as it is a number. `-p["age"]` reads "minus the age". Turning the ages negative flips their order: -36 is smaller than -30, so the oldest person now comes first.

```python
sorted(people, key=lambda p: (-p["age"], p["name"]))
# Ada 36, Lin 30, Sam 30   (oldest first, then by name)
```

The keys here are `(-30, "Lin")`, `(-36, "Ada")` and `(-30, "Sam")`. The smallest first item is -36, so Ada leads. Lin and Sam tie on -30, so their names decide: L before S.

## Ties keep their order

Python's sort is **stable**: items that compare equal keep the order they started in. In the age-only sort above, Lin and Sam are both 30. Lin came first in the list, so Lin stays ahead of Sam. That is true with `reverse=True` too:

```python
sorted(["bb", "a", "cc"], key=len, reverse=True)   # ['bb', 'cc', 'a']
```

`"bb"` and `"cc"` have the same length, so they stay in their starting order. This is a [[promise you can build on|stable-sort]]. For example, you can sort twice, and the second sort keeps the first sort's order inside every tie:

```python
by_name = sorted(people, key=lambda p: p["name"], reverse=True)   # Sam, Lin, Ada
sorted(by_name, key=lambda p: p["age"])
# Sam 30, Lin 30, Ada 36   (youngest first, names Z to A)
```

## The same key works on min and max

`min` and `max` take a `key=` too. They hand back the whole item whose key is smallest or largest:

```python
max(people, key=lambda p: p["age"])            # {'name': 'Ada', 'age': 36}
max(people, key=lambda p: p["age"])["name"]    # 'Ada'
```

That is the oldest person, not the "largest dict". Adding `["name"]` on the end picks the name out of the dict that came back.

**Watch out:**

- `names = names.sort()` sets `names` to `None`, because `.sort()` hands back `None`. Either write `names.sort()` on its own line, or write `names = sorted(names)`.
- `key=len()` calls `len` with nothing inside the brackets, and Python stops with an error. Pass the function itself: `key=len`.
- The minus trick only works for numbers: `-"Ada"` is an error. To sort strings Z to A inside a tuple key, sort twice (stability makes that correct, as shown above) or use `reverse=True` on the whole thing.

::: context keyword-argument Passing by name
So far you have passed arguments by position: `greet("Lin", "?")` puts the first value in the first parameter and the second in the second. A keyword argument says which parameter it is for: `greet("Lin", punctuation="?")`. The order of keyword arguments does not matter, and the call reads like a label on each value. You already used one in py2-02: `enumerate(names, start=1)`. The next lesson looks at keyword arguments in depth.
:::

::: context char-codes Every letter is a number
Inside the computer, every character is stored as a number, called its code point. Capitals A to Z are 65 to 90. Small letters a to z are 97 to 122. When Python compares two strings, it compares these numbers, first character first. So `"Kiwi"` (K is 75) comes before `"apple"` (a is 97). You can see the number with the built-in `ord`: `ord("K")` is `75`.


:::

::: context functions-as-values Handing over a function
In Python a function is a value, like a number or a list. You can store it in a variable or pass it to another function. `len` names the function; `len("abc")` runs it. So `key=len` gives `sorted` the tool, and `sorted` decides when to use it: once on each item. Engineers call a function that is handed over to be used later a *callback*. You will meet callbacks again whenever code needs to say "do this to each item" or "do this when something happens".
:::

::: context lambda-word Why the odd name
The name comes from a Greek letter, lambda (written λ). In the 1930s the mathematician Alonzo Church used it in a system for writing down functions without naming them, now called the lambda calculus. Several programming languages borrowed the word for their small nameless functions. In Python a lambda can hold only one expression, the answer, so anything that needs several lines, loops or an `if` statement belongs in a normal `def`.
:::

::: context tuple-compare Like words in a dictionary
Tuples compare the way a dictionary orders words. Look at the first letter; only if the first letters match do you look at the second. For tuples, the first item decides unless it is a tie.

```svg

  <rect x="20" y="22" width="50" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>

  <text x="170" y="41" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">vs</text>
  <rect x="208" y="22" width="50" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="233" y="41" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">-30</text>
  <rect x="264" y="22" width="56" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="292" y="41" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"Lin"</text>

  <rect x="76" y="88" width="56" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="104" y="107" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"Lin"</text>
  <text x="170" y="107" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">vs</text>
  <rect x="208" y="88" width="50" height="28" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="233" y="107" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">-30</text>
  <rect x="264" y="88" width="56" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
```
