:::

::: context stable-sort A promise, not luck
Python's documentation guarantees that its sort is stable, so you can rely on it. The method inside is called Timsort, written for Python by Tim Peters in 2002. Stability matters with real data. Imagine a list of sensor readings, already in the order they arrived, that you sort by which sensor sent them. Because the sort is stable, each sensor's readings stay in arrival order, with no extra work from you.
:::
--- task
Each person is a dict with the keys `"name"` and `"age"`, like `{"name": "Ada", "age": 36}`. Write three functions:

1. `by_age_then_name(people)` returns a **new** list (use `sorted`, not `.sort()`), oldest first, with people of the same age in name order A to Z. Use a lambda key that returns a tuple: minus the age, then the name.
2. `longest_first(words)` returns the words sorted by length, longest first. Words of the same length must keep their original order. Use `key=len` and `reverse=True`.
3. `youngest(people)` returns the **name** of the youngest person. Use `min` with a lambda key, then take `["name"]` from the dict it hands back.

For example, `longest_first(["bb", "a", "ccc"])` returns `["ccc", "bb", "a"]`, and for the three people Lin (30), Ada (36) and Sam (30), `youngest` returns `"Lin"`.
--- starter
def by_age_then_name(people):
    return people


def longest_first(words):
    return sorted(words)


def youngest(people):
    return min(people)
--- solution
def by_age_then_name(people):
    return sorted(people, key=lambda p: (-p["age"], p["name"]))


def longest_first(words):
    return sorted(words, key=len, reverse=True)


def youngest(people):
    return min(people, key=lambda p: p["age"])["name"]
--- hint
All three are one `return` line. The first two use `sorted` with a `key=`, and the third uses `min` with a `key=`.
--- hint
A key can return a tuple: `lambda p: (-p["age"], p["name"])` sorts by age, oldest first, then by name A to Z. For `longest_first`, the key is `len` with no brackets, plus `reverse=True`. Stability keeps equal lengths in order.
--- hint
`min(people, key=lambda p: p["age"])` hands back the whole dict of the youngest person. Put `["name"]` right after the closing bracket to take the name out of it.
--- check case | Oldest first, then alphabetical
[p["name"] for p in by_age_then_name([{"name": "Lin", "age": 30}, {"name": "Ada", "age": 36}, {"name": "Cy", "age": 30}])]
=> ["Ada", "Cy", "Lin"]
--- check test | by_age_then_name does not change the list you pass in
(lambda ps: (by_age_then_name(ps), [p["name"] for p in ps])[1] == ["B", "A"])([{"name": "B", "age": 1}, {"name": "A", "age": 2}])
?? Use sorted(...), which returns a new list, rather than .sort().
--- check case | longest_first keeps ties in their original order
longest_first(["bb", "a", "ccc", "dd", "e"])
=> ["ccc", "bb", "dd", "a", "e"]
--- check case | youngest returns a name
youngest([{"name": "Ada", "age": 36}, {"name": "Tim", "age": 9}, {"name": "Lin", "age": 30}])
=> "Tim"

+++ practice | Cheapest first
--- task
Each item in a shop is a tuple `(name, price)`, like `("tea", 2.5)`. Write `cheapest_first(items)`. It returns a **new** list of the items sorted by price, cheapest first. Items with the same price keep their original order. Use `sorted` with a lambda key.

For example, `cheapest_first([("jam", 4.0), ("tea", 2.5), ("bun", 1.2)])` gives `[("bun", 1.2), ("tea", 2.5), ("jam", 4.0)]`.
--- starter
def cheapest_first(items):
    return sorted(items)
--- solution
def cheapest_first(items):
    return sorted(items, key=lambda item: item[1])
--- hint
Plain `sorted` on tuples compares the first items, the names. You want Python to look at the price instead.
--- hint
The price is at position 1 of each tuple, so the key is a lambda that hands back `item[1]`.
--- check case | Sorted by price
cheapest_first([("jam", 4.0), ("tea", 2.5), ("bun", 1.2)])
=> [("bun", 1.2), ("tea", 2.5), ("jam", 4.0)]
--- check case | Equal prices keep their order
cheapest_first([("b", 1.0), ("a", 1.0), ("c", 0.5)])
=> [("c", 0.5), ("b", 1.0), ("a", 1.0)]
--- check case | No items
cheapest_first([])
=> []
--- check test | The list you pass in is not changed
(lambda xs: (cheapest_first(xs), xs)[1] == [("x", 2), ("y", 1)])([("x", 2), ("y", 1)])

+++ practice | The closest value
--- task
Write `closest(values, target)`. It returns the value in `values` that is closest to `target`, meaning the difference between them is smallest. If two values are equally close, return the one that comes **first** in the list. Use `min` with a lambda key.

One trick: the difference can be negative (3 is 2 below 5, which is -2), and you want 2 and -2 to count as equally far. Squaring the difference makes both the same: `(v - target) ** 2`.

For example, `closest([10, 4, 7, 12], 8)` gives `7`.
--- starter
def closest(values, target):
    return min(values)
--- solution
def closest(values, target):
    return min(values, key=lambda v: (v - target) ** 2)
--- hint
`min` takes a `key=`, just like `sorted`. It hands back the whole item whose key is smallest.
--- hint
The key is how far each value is from the target, squared, so that being below or above counts the same: `lambda v: (v - target) ** 2`. `min` keeps the first of equal keys.
--- check case | The nearest value
closest([10, 4, 7, 12], 8)
=> 7
--- check case | A tie goes to the value that comes first
closest([6, 10, 3], 8)
=> 6
--- check case | The target itself
closest([1, 5, 9], 5)
=> 5
--- check case | Negative numbers
closest([-10, -3, 4], -2)
=> -3

+++ practice | Words by how often they appear
--- task
Write `by_frequency(words)`. It returns a list of the **different** words in `words`, the most frequent first. Words that appear equally often are in A to Z order. Count with `Counter`, then sort its `(word, count)` pairs from `.items()` with a lambda key that returns a tuple, and keep only the words.

For example, `by_frequency(["b", "a", "c", "a", "b", "a"])` gives `["a", "b", "c"]`, and `by_frequency(["y", "x"])` gives `["x", "y"]`.
--- starter
from collections import Counter


def by_frequency(words):
    return list(Counter(words))
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def by_frequency(words):
    pairs = sorted(Counter(words).items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return [word for word, count in pairs]
--- hint
`Counter(words).items()` gives pairs like `("a", 3)`. Sort those pairs, then take the word out of each one with a comprehension.
--- hint
To sort by count, biggest first, and then by word A to Z, the key returns a tuple: minus the count, then the word. In a pair, the count is `pair[1]` and the word is `pair[0]`.
--- check case | Most frequent first
by_frequency(["b", "a", "c", "a", "b", "a"])
=> ["a", "b", "c"]
--- check case | Ties are in A to Z order, not first-seen order
by_frequency(["y", "x"])
=> ["x", "y"]
--- check case | A mix of counts and ties
by_frequency(["pear", "fig", "kiwi", "fig", "pear", "date"])
=> ["fig", "pear", "date", "kiwi"]
--- check case | No words
by_frequency([])
=> []

+++ practice | Names A to Z, ignoring capitals
--- task
Write `sort_names(names)`. It returns a **new** list of the names in A to Z order, ignoring capitals, so `"bob"` and `"Bob"` count as the same name. Names that are the same apart from capitals must stay in the order they started in. The names themselves are not changed: `"Bob"` keeps its capital B.

For example, `sort_names(["carol", "Bob", "alice", "bob"])` gives `["alice", "Bob", "bob", "carol"]`.
--- starter
def sort_names(names):
    return sorted(names)
--- solution
def sort_names(names):
    return sorted(names, key=str.lower)
--- hint
Plain `sorted` puts every capital letter before every small letter. Give it a key that compares the lowercase form of each name.
--- hint
`key=str.lower` sorts by the lowercase text but hands back the original names. The sort is stable, so names that tie keep their starting order.
--- check case | Ignores capitals
sort_names(["carol", "Bob", "alice", "bob"])
=> ["alice", "Bob", "bob", "carol"]
--- check case | Ties keep their starting order
sort_names(["bob", "BOB", "Bob"])
=> ["bob", "BOB", "Bob"]
--- check case | No names
sort_names([])
=> []
--- check test | The list you pass in is not changed
(lambda xs: (sort_names(xs), xs)[1] == ["b", "A"])(["b", "A"])

+++ practice | Debug: the sorted list that was None
--- task
`alphabetical(names)` should return a **new** list of the names in A to Z order, and leave the caller's list exactly as it was.

The bug: it returns `None`, and it also rearranges the caller's list. Fix it.
--- starter
def alphabetical(names):
    names = names.sort()
    return names
--- solution
def alphabetical(names):
    return sorted(names)
--- hint
What does `.sort()` hand back? Look at the lesson's "Watch out".
--- hint
`.sort()` changes the list itself and hands back `None`. `sorted(...)` builds a new list and leaves the old one alone, which is exactly what this function promises.
--- check case | Returns the names in order
alphabetical(["lin", "ada", "sam"])
=> ["ada", "lin", "sam"]
--- check test | The caller's list is not rearranged
(lambda xs: (alphabetical(xs), xs)[1] == ["b", "a"])(["b", "a"])
--- check case | An empty list gives an empty list
alphabetical([])
=> []

+++ practice | The launch schedule
--- task
Each mission is a dict like `{"name": "Kestrel", "day": 12, "priority": 2}`. Write `schedule(missions, today)`. It returns the **names** of the missions whose `day` is `today` or later, in the order they should fly:

1. earliest day first;
2. on the same day, higher `priority` first;
3. on the same day and priority, names in A to Z order.

Missions before `today` are left out. Use a comprehension to filter and `sorted` with a lambda key that returns a tuple.

For example, with Kestrel (day 12, priority 2), Osprey (day 10, priority 1), Heron (day 12, priority 5) and Wren (day 3, priority 9), `schedule(..., 5)` gives `["Osprey", "Heron", "Kestrel"]`.
--- starter
def schedule(missions, today):
    return [m["name"] for m in missions]
--- solution
def schedule(missions, today):
    upcoming = [m for m in missions if m["day"] >= today]
    ordered = sorted(upcoming, key=lambda m: (m["day"], -m["priority"], m["name"]))
    return [m["name"] for m in ordered]
--- hint
Break it into three steps: keep the upcoming missions, sort them, then take out the names.
--- hint
A tuple key can have three parts. The day goes first as it is (smallest first), the priority goes second with a minus sign (biggest first), and the name goes last.
--- check case | Day, then priority, then name
schedule([{"name": "Kestrel", "day": 12, "priority": 2}, {"name": "Osprey", "day": 10, "priority": 1}, {"name": "Heron", "day": 12, "priority": 5}, {"name": "Wren", "day": 3, "priority": 9}], 5)
=> ["Osprey", "Heron", "Kestrel"]
--- check case | Same day and priority go A to Z
schedule([{"name": "Tern", "day": 1, "priority": 1}, {"name": "Auk", "day": 1, "priority": 1}], 1)
=> ["Auk", "Tern"]
--- check case | A mission today still flies
schedule([{"name": "Now", "day": 7, "priority": 0}], 7)
=> ["Now"]
--- check case | Everything is in the past
schedule([{"name": "Old", "day": 1, "priority": 3}], 2)
=> []

=== py2-06 | *args, **kwargs and default arguments
--- teach
Last lesson you passed `key=len` and `reverse=True` to `sorted` by name. You have also seen that `max(3, 7, 1)` works, and so does `max(3, 7, 1, 9, 4)`. How can one function take three arguments, or five, or none at all? This lesson shows how to write functions like that.

Picture a shopping bag at the till. The cashier does not know how many things you will buy. Whatever comes down the belt goes into the bag. A Python function can have a bag like that for its extra arguments.

## Two ways to pass an argument

A quick reminder first. There are two ways to hand an argument to a function:

- A **positional argument** goes in by its place: first value to the first parameter, second to the second.
- A **keyword argument** goes in by name: the parameter's name, `=`, then the value.

```python
def greet(name, punctuation="!"):
    return f"Hello, {name}{punctuation}"

greet("Lin", "?")                     # 'Hello, Lin?'   both positional
greet("Lin", punctuation="?")         # 'Hello, Lin?'   the second by keyword
greet("Ada")                          # 'Hello, Ada!'   punctuation uses its default
```

As in py-08b, a parameter with `=` has a **default**: the value it uses when the call leaves it out.

## *args: any number of positional arguments

Put a star `*` in front of a parameter's name, and that parameter becomes the bag. Read `*nums` as "star nums". It collects every extra positional argument into a [[tuple|args-tuple]].

```python
def collect(*nums):
    return nums

collect(1, 2, 3)     # (1, 2, 3)
collect(5)           # (5,)
collect()            # ()
```

Inside the function, `nums` is an ordinary tuple, so you can loop over it, take its `len`, or hand it to `sum`:

```python
def average(*nums):
    return sum(nums) / len(nums)

average(4, 6, 8)     # 6.0
```

## **kwargs: any number of keyword arguments

Two stars, `**`, read "double star", make a bag for keyword arguments. It collects them into a **dict**: each name becomes a key (as a string), and each value becomes that key's value.

```python
def settings(**options):
    return options

settings(colour="red", size=3)   # {'colour': 'red', 'size': 3}
settings()                       # {}
```

The dict keeps the names [[in the order the caller wrote them|kwargs-order]].

## Both bags at once

A function can have both. The usual names are `*args` (short for arguments) and `**kwargs` (short for keyword arguments). Those names are only a [[convention|naming-convention]]. The stars do the work.

```python
def show(*args, **kwargs):
    return args, kwargs

show(1, 2, colour="red")   # ((1, 2), {'colour': 'red'})
```

`1` and `2` came in by position, so they went into the tuple. `colour="red"` came in by name, so it went into the dict.

## Normal parameters first, then the bags

A function can also have ordinary parameters before its bags. The first positional arguments fill the ordinary parameters. Only the extras go into the bag.

```python
def label(title, *words, **extras):
    return title, words, extras

label("Crew", "Ada", "Lin", ship="Orbit")
# ('Crew', ('Ada', 'Lin'), {'ship': 'Orbit'})
```

`"Crew"` filled `title`. `"Ada"` and `"Lin"` were left over, so they went into `words`. `ship="Orbit"` went into `extras`.

## Turning the bags into text

Your task builds a piece of [[HTML|html-tags]], the language web pages are written in. Two tools from py2-03 do it. To glue the words of a tuple together with nothing in between, use `"".join(...)`:

```python
words = ("x", "y")
"".join(words)                   # 'xy'
```

To turn a dict into text, loop over `.items()`, which gives `(key, value)` pairs, and build one small string for each pair:

```python
extras = {"ship": "Orbit", "crew": 2}
"".join(f" {key}={value}" for key, value in extras.items())
# ' ship=Orbit crew=2'
```

Each piece starts with a space, so the pieces stay apart when they are glued together.

**Watch out:** a function with `*nums` wants the numbers one by one, not a list. `total(1, 2, 3)` puts three numbers in the bag. `total([1, 2, 3])` puts *one* thing in the bag, a list, so `nums` is `([1, 2, 3],)`, and `sum(nums)` stops with a `TypeError` because it cannot add a list to a number.

::: context args-tuple Why a tuple and not a list
The extra arguments arrive as a tuple, which cannot be changed after it is made. That fits: the arguments are what the caller sent, and the function has no business editing that record. If you do want to change them, make a list first: `items = list(args)`. A one-item bag prints as `(5,)`, with a trailing comma. That comma is how Python writes a tuple with one item, as you saw in py2-02.
:::

::: context kwargs-order Order is kept
Since Python 3.6, the dict made by `**kwargs` keeps the names in the order the caller wrote them. From Python 3.7 on, every ordinary dict keeps the order its keys were added in, too. That is why looping over `kwargs.items()` gives the names back in the same order, and why the HTML in your task comes out with its attributes in the order they were given.
:::

::: context naming-convention A habit everyone shares
A convention is a habit that everyone agrees to follow, though nothing forces it. `def f(*things, **extras)` works exactly like `def f(*args, **kwargs)`. Most Python programmers write `args` and `kwargs` when the extras have no better name, so a reader spots them at once. When a better name exists, use it: `*nums` in a function that adds numbers tells the reader more than `*args`.
:::

::: context html-tags How a tag is built
A web page is written in HTML, as a nest of tags. A tag has a name, then optional attributes (extra settings, written as `name="value"`), then its children (what goes inside), then a closing tag with a slash. The browser reads `<a href="/">home</a>` as "a link, going to `/`, showing the word home".

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="14" y="30" width="44" height="32" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="36" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;a</text>
  <rect x="64" y="30" width="96" height="32" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="100" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">href</text>
  <text x="100" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="start" fill="#1f2a44">="/"</text>
  <rect x="166" y="30" width="26" height="32" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="179" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&gt;</text>
  <rect x="198" y="30" width="72" height="32" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="234" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">home</text>
  <rect x="276" y="30" width="70" height="32" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="311" y="51" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;/a&gt;</text>
  <text x="36" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">name</text>
  <text x="112" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">attribute</text>
  <text x="234" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">child</text>
  <text x="311" y="84" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">closing tag</text>
  <text x="180" y="108" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">attributes from **attrs, children from *children</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write two functions.

1. `total(*nums)` returns the sum of any number of numbers. `total(1, 2, 3, 4)` is `10`, and `total()` is `0`. (The starter has `nums` with no star. Add the star.)
2. `tag(name, *children, **attrs)` returns a piece of HTML as a string:
   - it starts with `<`, the tag name, then each attribute as a space followed by `key="value"`, in the order they were given, then `>`;
   - then the children, joined with nothing in between;
   - then `</`, the tag name and `>`.

   So `tag("a", "home", href="/")` is `'<a href="/">home</a>'`, `tag("p")` is `"<p></p>"`, and `tag("b", "x", "y")` is `"<b>xy</b>"`.
--- starter
def total(nums):
    return sum(nums)


def tag(name, children, attrs):
    return ""
--- solution
def total(*nums):
    return sum(nums)


def tag(name, *children, **attrs):
    attr_text = "".join(f' {key}="{value}"' for key, value in attrs.items())
    return f"<{name}{attr_text}>{''.join(children)}</{name}>"
--- hint
In both `def` lines, put stars in front of the bag parameters: `*nums`, `*children`, `**attrs`. Inside `total`, `nums` is then a tuple, and `sum(nums)` adds it up with no other change.
--- hint
In `tag`, build the attributes as one string first. Each one is `f' {key}="{value}"'` (note the leading space and the double quotes around the value), glued together with `"".join(...)` while looping over `attrs.items()`.
--- hint
Then return `f"<{name}{attr_text}>{''.join(children)}</{name}>"`. Inside the f-string the empty string is written with single quotes, `''`, so it does not clash with the double quotes around the f-string.
--- check case | total adds any number of numbers
total(1, 2, 3, 4)
=> 10
--- check case | total() is 0
total()
=> 0
--- check case | tag with an attribute and a child
tag("a", "home", href="/")
=> '<a href="/">home</a>'
--- check case | tag with nothing inside
tag("p")
=> "<p></p>"
--- check case | Several children are joined
tag("b", "x", "y")
=> "<b>xy</b>"
--- check case | Attributes keep the order they were given in
tag("img", src="a.png", alt="A")
=> '<img src="a.png" alt="A"></img>'

+++ practice | The largest of any number of values
--- task
Write `largest(*values)`. It takes any number of numbers, one by one, and returns the biggest. Called with no numbers at all, it returns `None` instead of stopping with an error.

For example, `largest(3, 9, 4)` gives `9`, `largest(-5)` gives `-5`, and `largest()` gives `None`.
--- starter
def largest(values):
    return max(values)
--- solution
def largest(*values):
    return max(values) if values else None
--- hint
A star in front of the parameter's name turns it into a bag: every number passed in lands in one tuple.
--- hint
`max` of an empty tuple stops with an error. An empty tuple counts as false, so a conditional expression can choose between `max(values)` and `None`.
--- check case | The biggest of three
largest(3, 9, 4)
=> 9
--- check case | One number
largest(-5)
=> -5
--- check case | No numbers gives None
largest()
=> None
--- check case | All negative
largest(-8, -2, -30)
=> -2

+++ practice | Describe anything by name
--- task
Write `describe(**specs)`. It takes any number of keyword arguments and returns them as text: each one written `name: value`, joined with a comma and a space, in the order they were given. With no keyword arguments at all, it returns `"nothing"`.

For example, `describe(mass=12, colour="red")` gives `"mass: 12, colour: red"`, and `describe()` gives `"nothing"`.
--- starter
def describe(specs):
    return str(specs)
--- solution
def describe(**specs):
    if not specs:
        return "nothing"
    return ", ".join(f"{name}: {value}" for name, value in specs.items())
--- hint
Two stars in front of the parameter's name collect the keyword arguments into a dict. An empty dict counts as false.
--- hint
Loop over `specs.items()` to get each name and value, build `f"{name}: {value}"` for each, and glue them with `", ".join(...)`.
--- check case | Two specs, in order
describe(mass=12, colour="red")
=> "mass: 12, colour: red"
--- check case | One spec
describe(stage=2)
=> "stage: 2"
--- check case | Nothing given
describe()
=> "nothing"

+++ practice | Merge any number of tallies
--- task
Each tally is a dict from an item to a count, like `{"bolt": 3}`. Write `merge_counts(*tallies)`. It takes any number of tallies and returns one new dict with every item's counts added together, in the order the items are first met. It must not change any of the tallies passed in. Use `get` to add to a count that might not exist yet.

For example, `merge_counts({"bolt": 3, "nut": 1}, {"nut": 4, "seal": 2})` gives `{"bolt": 3, "nut": 5, "seal": 2}`, and `merge_counts()` gives `{}`.
--- starter
def merge_counts(*tallies):
    return tallies[0]
--- solution
def merge_counts(*tallies):
    total = {}
    for tally in tallies:
        for item, count in tally.items():
            total[item] = total.get(item, 0) + count
    return total
--- hint
Start with a new empty dict. Loop over the tallies, and inside that, over each tally's `items()`.
--- hint
Add each count with `total[item] = total.get(item, 0) + count`, the counting line from the basics.
--- check case | Counts are added across tallies
merge_counts({"bolt": 3, "nut": 1}, {"nut": 4, "seal": 2})
=> {"bolt": 3, "nut": 5, "seal": 2}
--- check case | Three tallies
merge_counts({"a": 1}, {"a": 1}, {"a": 1})
=> {"a": 3}
--- check case | No tallies at all
merge_counts()
=> {}
--- check test | The tallies passed in are not changed
(lambda t: (merge_counts(t, {"x": 5}), t)[1] == {"x": 1})({"x": 1})

+++ practice | An average that never divides by zero
--- task
Write `mean(*nums)`. It returns the average of any number of numbers, always as a float. Called with no numbers, it returns `0.0` instead of stopping with a `ZeroDivisionError`.

For example, `mean(2, 4, 9)` gives `5.0`, `mean(7)` gives `7.0`, and `mean()` gives `0.0`.
--- starter
def mean(*nums):
    return sum(nums) / len(nums)
--- solution
def mean(*nums):
    if not nums:
        return 0.0
    return sum(nums) / len(nums)
--- hint
Call the starter with no arguments. `len(nums)` is 0, and dividing by 0 stops the program.
--- hint
Check for the empty tuple first: `if not nums:` then return `0.0`. Dividing with `/` already gives a float in every other case.
--- check case | The average of three
mean(2, 4, 9)
=> 5.0
--- check case | One number is its own average, as a float
mean(7)
=> 7.0
--- check case | No numbers gives 0.0
mean()
=> 0.0
--- check case | Negative numbers
mean(-3, 3, -6)
=> -2.0

+++ practice | Debug: a banner with optional style
--- task
`banner(text, **style)` puts a border character on each side of the text: `banner("GO", border="*")` gives `"* GO *"`. If the caller passes `upper=True`, the text is shown in capitals: `banner("go", border="#", upper=True)` gives `"# GO #"`.

Both style options are optional. With no `border`, it should use `"="`, and with no `upper`, the text should stay as it is. The bug: `banner("go")` stops with `KeyError: 'border'`. Fix it so a missing option falls back to its default.
--- starter
def banner(text, **style):
    border = style["border"]
    if style["upper"]:
        text = text.upper()
    return f"{border} {text} {border}"
--- solution
def banner(text, **style):
    border = style.get("border", "=")
    if style.get("upper", False):
        text = text.upper()
    return f"{border} {text} {border}"
--- hint
`style` is an ordinary dict of whatever keyword arguments the caller passed. What happens when you ask a dict for a key it does not have?
--- hint
Read each option with `get` and a default: `"="` for the border and `False` for upper.
--- check case | No options uses the defaults
banner("go")
=> "= go ="
--- check case | Only a border
banner("GO", border="*")
=> "* GO *"
--- check case | Only upper
banner("hold", upper=True)
=> "= HOLD ="
--- check case | Both options
banner("go", border="#", upper=True)
=> "# GO #"

+++ practice | A log line from any parts
--- task
Write `log_line(level, *parts, **fields)`. It returns one line of text made of, in this order, with single spaces between them:

1. the level in capitals, inside square brackets, like `[WARN]`;
2. each of the `parts`, turned into text;
3. each of the `fields`, written `name=value`, in the order given.

For example, `log_line("warn", "valve", 3, "slow", ms=120, retry=True)` gives `"[WARN] valve 3 slow ms=120 retry=True"`, and `log_line("info")` gives `"[INFO]"`.
--- starter
def log_line(level, *parts, **fields):
    return f"[{level}] {parts} {fields}"
--- solution
def log_line(level, *parts, **fields):
    line = f"[{level.upper()}]"
    for part in parts:
        line += f" {part}"
    for name, value in fields.items():
        line += f" {name}={value}"
    return line
--- hint
Start with the bracketed level as a string, then add each piece to the end of it with `+=`. Give each piece its own space in front, so there is never a space left over when `parts` or `fields` is empty.
--- hint
Loop over `parts` and add `f" {part}"` for each one (an f-string turns a number into text for you). Then loop over `fields.items()` and add `f" {name}={value}"`.
--- check case | Level, parts and fields
log_line("warn", "valve", 3, "slow", ms=120, retry=True)
=> "[WARN] valve 3 slow ms=120 retry=True"
--- check case | Only the level
log_line("info")
=> "[INFO]"
--- check case | Parts but no fields
log_line("error", "disk", "full")
=> "[ERROR] disk full"
--- check case | Fields but no parts
log_line("debug", step=4)
=> "[DEBUG] step=4"

=== py2-06b | Spreading arguments and keyword-only parameters
--- teach
Last lesson a star in a `def` line made a bag that *collects* arguments. This lesson turns the stars around: in a call, they *spread* a list or dict out into separate arguments. Then you will see how to make some parameters accept a value only by name.

Picture a bag of groceries tipped out onto the counter. Before, you had one bag. After, you have separate things, one by one.

## One star in a call: spread a list

Put `*` in front of a list when you call a function, and Python passes its items one by one, as separate positional arguments:

```python
nums = [3, 7, 1]
max(*nums)          # same as max(3, 7, 1)  ->  7
print(*nums)        # same as print(3, 7, 1), prints: 3 7 1
print(nums)         # prints the list itself: [3, 7, 1]
```

Compare the last two lines. `print(nums)` gets *one* argument, a list. `print(*nums)` gets *three* numbers. This [[spreading|spread-picture]] is sometimes called unpacking, like the unpacking of tuples in py2-02.

## Two stars in a call: spread a dict

`**` in front of a dict passes each key and value as a keyword argument. `print` has a keyword parameter called `sep`, short for separator: what goes between the items. Normally it is a space.

```python
opts = {"sep": "-"}
print("a", "b", **opts)    # same as print("a", "b", sep="-"), prints: a-b
```

The keys of the dict must match parameter names, or Python stops with a `TypeError`.

## Keyword-only parameters

Look at a function with two numbers that are easy to mix up:

```python
def connect(host, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} (timeout {timeout}s)"

connect("example.org", 5, 8080)   # 'example.org:5 (timeout 8080s)'  oops
```

The caller meant a timeout of 5 and port 8080, but put them the wrong way round. Nothing warned them.

A bare `*` in the `def` line fixes this. Everything after the bare `*` is **keyword-only**: it can only be passed by name.

```python
def connect(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} (timeout {timeout}s)"

connect("example.org")                 # 'example.org:443 (timeout 5.0s)'
connect("example.org", port=8080)      # 'example.org:8080 (timeout 5.0s)'
# connect("example.org", 8080)  ->  TypeError: connect() takes 1 positional argument but 2 were given
```

Here the `*` has no name after it. It collects nothing. It only marks the line: "from here on, by name only". Now a mix-up is impossible, because a wrong call [[stops with an error|type-error]] instead of quietly doing the wrong thing.

Parameters that come after `*args` are keyword-only too, because the bag swallows every extra positional argument:

```python
def log(*parts, sep=" "):
    return sep.join(parts)

log("T", "minus", "ten")             # 'T minus ten'
log("T", "minus", "ten", sep="-")    # 'T-minus-ten'
```

## The full order

When a function uses everything, the parts of its [[signature|signature-word]] always come in this order:

1. normal parameters,
2. `*args` (or a bare `*`),
3. keyword-only parameters,
4. `**kwargs`.

```python
def launch(vehicle, *payloads, dry_run=False, **extra):
    return vehicle, payloads, dry_run, extra

launch("Orbit-1", "cubesat", "camera", dry_run=True, pad=39)
# ('Orbit-1', ('cubesat', 'camera'), True, {'pad': 39})
```

**Watch out:** the same star does opposite jobs in different places. In a `def` line, `*` and `**` *collect* arguments into a bag. In a call, they *spread* a list or dict out into separate arguments. Look at which side of the function you are on.

One more thing about defaults, to carry into the next lesson. A default is [[worked out once|default-once]], when the `def` line runs, not on every call. For numbers and strings that never matters. For lists and dicts it matters a lot.

::: context spread-picture Tipping out the bag

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="14" y="36" width="96" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="62" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">[3, 7, 1]</text>
  <text x="62" y="24" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">one list</text>
  <line x1="116" y1="53" x2="150" y2="53" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="158,53 148,48 148,58" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="137" y="44" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">*</text>
  <text x="192" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">max(</text>
  <rect x="216" y="40" width="26" height="26" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="229" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <rect x="250" y="40" width="26" height="26" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="263" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">7</text>
  <rect x="284" y="40" width="26" height="26" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="297" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="320" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">)</text>
  <text x="263" y="92" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">three separate arguments</text>
</svg>
```

The star opens the list and lays its items side by side in the call. Some programmers nickname it the "splat" operator.
:::

::: context type-error Loud beats quiet
A `TypeError` is the exception Python raises when a value or a call has the wrong shape: too many positional arguments, a keyword the function does not have, or adding a list to a number. An error that stops the program feels bad, but it is far better than a program that runs with the wrong numbers. Engineers aim for mistakes to fail loudly and early, while someone is watching, rather than quietly, later, in flight. Python's own `sorted` makes its `key` and `reverse` keyword-only: `sorted([3, 1], len)` raises `TypeError`.
:::

::: context signature-word The function's outline
A function's signature is its name plus its list of parameters: everything in the `def` line between `def` and the colon. It tells a caller what the function accepts, the way a form's blank boxes tell you what to fill in. In code editors, hovering over a function name usually shows its signature, which is often the fastest way to learn how to call it.
:::

::: context default-once Made once, kept forever
When Python reaches a `def` line, it builds the function, and at that moment it works out each default value and stores it with the function. Every later call that leaves the argument out reuses that same stored value. A number or a string can never be changed, so reusing it is safe. A list can be changed in place, as you saw with `append`. The next lesson is a bug hunt that shows exactly what goes wrong.
:::
--- task
Practice both new ideas. In the starter:

1. Make `port` and `timeout` in `connect` **keyword-only**, by putting a bare `*` after `host`: `def connect(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):`. Leave the body as it is.
2. Fix `connect_with(host, settings)`. `settings` is a dict like `{"port": 22, "timeout": 1.5}`. It must return `connect(host, **settings)`, spreading the dict out as keyword arguments. So `connect_with("example.org", {"port": 22, "timeout": 1.5})` returns `"example.org:22 (timeout 1.5s)"`.
3. Change the last line so it spreads `parts` into `print`, with `"-"` as the separator: `print(*parts, sep="-")`. The program must print:

```
T-minus-10
```
--- starter
def connect(host, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} (timeout {timeout}s)"


def connect_with(host, settings):
    return connect(host)


parts = ["T", "minus", 10]
print(parts)
--- solution
def connect(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} (timeout {timeout}s)"


def connect_with(host, settings):
    return connect(host, **settings)


parts = ["T", "minus", 10]
print(*parts, sep="-")
--- hint
Three small edits: a bare `*` in the `def` line of `connect`, two stars in the call inside `connect_with`, and one star in the `print` call.
--- hint
`**settings` inside a call turns `{"port": 22}` into `port=22`. `*parts` inside a call turns the list into three separate arguments.
--- hint
The lines are `def connect(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):`, then `return connect(host, **settings)`, then `print(*parts, sep="-")`.
--- check output | Spreads the list into print with - between the items
T-minus-10
--- check case | connect uses its defaults
connect("example.org")
=> "example.org:443 (timeout 5.0s)"
--- check case | port can be given by name
connect("example.org", port=8080)
=> "example.org:8080 (timeout 5.0s)"
--- check test | port cannot be given by position
raises(TypeError, lambda: connect("example.org", 8080))
?? Put a bare * after host in the def line, so everything after it is keyword-only.
--- check case | connect_with spreads the settings dict
connect_with("example.org", {"port": 22, "timeout": 1.5})
=> "example.org:22 (timeout 1.5s)"
--- check case | An empty settings dict keeps the defaults
connect_with("a", {})
=> "a:443 (timeout 5.0s)"

+++ practice | Box volume from a list
--- task
The starter has `volume(length, width, height)`, which multiplies three numbers. Measurements arrive as a list of three numbers, like `[2, 3, 4]`. Write `volume_of(dims)`, which returns `volume(...)` called with the three numbers of `dims` spread out as separate arguments. Keep `volume` as it is.

For example, `volume_of([2, 3, 4])` gives `24`.
--- starter
def volume(length, width, height):
    return length * width * height


def volume_of(dims):
    return volume(dims)
--- solution
def volume(length, width, height):
    return length * width * height


def volume_of(dims):
    return volume(*dims)
--- hint
`volume(dims)` passes one argument, a list, but `volume` wants three.
--- hint
One star in front of a list, inside a call, passes its items one by one.
--- check case | Spreads the three measurements
volume_of([2, 3, 4])
=> 24
--- check case | Works with a tuple too
volume_of((1.5, 2, 10))
=> 30.0
--- check case | A zero side gives zero volume
volume_of([0, 5, 5])
=> 0
--- check case | volume itself is unchanged
volume(1, 2, 3)
=> 6

+++ practice | Scale by name only
--- task
Write `scale(values, *, factor=1, offset=0)`. It returns a new list with `v * factor + offset` for each value `v`. `factor` and `offset` must be **keyword-only**, so a call like `scale([1, 2], 10)` stops with a `TypeError` instead of guessing what 10 means.

For example, `scale([1, 2, 3], factor=10)` gives `[10, 20, 30]`, `scale([1, 2], offset=5)` gives `[6, 7]`, and `scale([4])` gives `[4]`.
--- starter
def scale(values, factor=1, offset=0):
    return [v * factor + offset for v in values]
--- solution
def scale(values, *, factor=1, offset=0):
    return [v * factor + offset for v in values]
--- hint
The body is already right. The change is in the `def` line.
--- hint
A bare `*` in the `def` line makes every parameter after it keyword-only.
--- check case | factor by name
scale([1, 2, 3], factor=10)
=> [10, 20, 30]
--- check case | offset by name
scale([1, 2], offset=5)
=> [6, 7]
--- check case | Both defaults
scale([4])
=> [4]
--- check test | factor cannot be given by position
raises(TypeError, lambda: scale([1, 2], 10))
?? Put a bare * after values in the def line.

+++ practice | Rows into columns
--- task
A small table is a list of rows, each row a list of the same length, like `[[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]`. Write `columns(rows)`. It returns the table's columns: a list of lists, where the first list holds every row's first item, the second list every row's second item, and so on.

Do it with `zip` and one star: spread `rows` into `zip`, so each row becomes a separate argument, and `zip` pairs up first items with first items. Turn each tuple that `zip` makes into a list with `list(...)`.

For example, `columns([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]])` gives `[[1, 4], [2, 5], [3, 6]]`.
--- starter
def columns(rows):
    return [list(col) for col in zip(rows)]
--- solution
def columns(rows):
    return [list(col) for col in zip(*rows)]
--- hint
`zip(rows)` gets one argument, the whole table. `zip(row_a, row_b)` is what you want: each row as its own argument.
--- hint
A star in front of `rows` inside the call spreads the rows out, so `zip(*rows)` is the same as `zip(rows[0], rows[1], ...)`.
--- check case | Two rows become three columns
columns([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]])
=> [[1, 4], [2, 5], [3, 6]]
--- check case | Three rows of two
columns([["a", "b"], ["c", "d"], ["e", "f"]])
=> [["a", "c", "e"], ["b", "d", "f"]]
--- check case | One row becomes columns of one
columns([[7, 8]])
=> [[7], [8]]

+++ practice | Divide safely
--- task
Write `safe_divide(a, b, *, default=None)`. It returns `a / b`, or `default` when `b` is zero. `default` is keyword-only.

For example, `safe_divide(9, 3)` gives `3.0`, `safe_divide(1, 0)` gives `None`, and `safe_divide(1, 0, default=0.0)` gives `0.0`. Dividing zero by something is fine: `safe_divide(0, 5)` gives `0.0`.
--- starter
def safe_divide(a, b, default=None):
    return a / b
--- solution
def safe_divide(a, b, *, default=None):
    if b == 0:
        return default
    return a / b
--- hint
Two changes: a bare `*` in the `def` line before `default`, and a check for `b == 0` before you divide.
--- hint
Only the divisor matters. `0 / 5` is a normal division that gives `0.0`, so test `b`, not `a`.
--- check case | Ordinary division
safe_divide(9, 3)
=> 3.0
--- check case | Dividing by zero gives the default, None
safe_divide(1, 0)
=> None
--- check case | A default given by name
safe_divide(1, 0, default=0.0)
=> 0.0
--- check case | Zero divided by something is zero
safe_divide(0, 5)
=> 0.0
--- check test | default cannot be given by position
raises(TypeError, lambda: safe_divide(1, 0, 99))

+++ practice | Debug: the wrong kind of star
--- task
`launch(vehicle, *, pad, crew=0)` returns a sentence like `"Orbit-1 from pad 39 with 3 crew"`. Its `pad` and `crew` are keyword-only. `launch_from(vehicle, options)` is meant to call it with a dict of options, like `{"pad": 39, "crew": 3}`, spread out as keyword arguments.

The bug: `launch_from("Orbit-1", {"pad": 39, "crew": 3})` stops with a `TypeError` about positional arguments. Fix `launch_from`. Leave `launch` as it is.
--- starter
def launch(vehicle, *, pad, crew=0):
    return f"{vehicle} from pad {pad} with {crew} crew"


def launch_from(vehicle, options):
    return launch(vehicle, *options)
--- solution
def launch(vehicle, *, pad, crew=0):
    return f"{vehicle} from pad {pad} with {crew} crew"


def launch_from(vehicle, options):
    return launch(vehicle, **options)
--- hint
What does one star do to a dict in a call? Looping over a dict gives its keys, so `*options` passes the key names as positional arguments.
--- hint
Two stars spread a dict as keyword arguments: each key becomes a parameter name.
--- check case | Spreads the options by name
launch_from("Orbit-1", {"pad": 39, "crew": 3})
=> "Orbit-1 from pad 39 with 3 crew"
--- check case | crew falls back to its default
launch_from("Probe", {"pad": 17})
=> "Probe from pad 17 with 0 crew"
--- check test | pad is still keyword-only
raises(TypeError, lambda: launch("X", 39))

+++ practice | Layers of settings
--- task
A connection's settings come in layers: built-in defaults, then a team's file, then one person's choices. Later layers win. The starter has `link(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0)`, which returns a description like `"example.org:22 (5.0s)"`. Keep it, and write two functions:

1. `merge(*layers, **overrides)` takes any number of dicts and any keyword arguments. It returns **one new dict** with every key from every layer, where a key in a later layer replaces the same key from an earlier one, and the keyword arguments replace everything. It must not change any layer passed in.
2. `open_link(host, *layers, **overrides)` returns `link(host, ...)` called with the merged settings spread out as keyword arguments. It must pass its own `layers` and `overrides` on to `merge` by spreading them too.

For example, `merge({"port": 80, "timeout": 1.0}, {"port": 22})` gives `{"port": 22, "timeout": 1.0}`, and `open_link("example.org", {"port": 80}, timeout=2.5)` gives `"example.org:80 (2.5s)"`.
--- starter
def link(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} ({timeout}s)"


def merge(*layers, **overrides):
    return overrides


def open_link(host, *layers, **overrides):
    return link(host)
--- solution
def link(host, *, port=443, timeout=5.0):
    return f"{host}:{port} ({timeout}s)"


def merge(*layers, **overrides):
    settings = {}
    for layer in layers:
        for key, value in layer.items():
            settings[key] = value
    for key, value in overrides.items():
        settings[key] = value
    return settings


def open_link(host, *layers, **overrides):
    return link(host, **merge(*layers, **overrides))
--- hint
In `merge`, start with a new empty dict and copy the keys in, layer by layer, then the overrides last. Setting a key that is already there replaces its value, which is exactly "later wins".
--- hint
Inside `open_link`, `layers` is a tuple and `overrides` is a dict. To hand them on to `merge` as separate arguments again, spread them: one star for the tuple, two for the dict.
--- hint
The result of `merge(...)` is a dict of settings, so spread it into `link` with two stars: `link(host, **merge(*layers, **overrides))`.
--- check case | A later layer wins
merge({"port": 80, "timeout": 1.0}, {"port": 22})
=> {"port": 22, "timeout": 1.0}
--- check case | Keyword overrides beat every layer
merge({"port": 80}, {"port": 22}, port=8080)
=> {"port": 8080}
--- check case | Nothing to merge
merge()
=> {}
--- check test | The layers passed in are not changed
(lambda a: (merge(a, {"port": 1}), a)[1] == {"port": 80})({"port": 80})
--- check case | open_link spreads everything through
open_link("example.org", {"port": 80}, timeout=2.5)
=> "example.org:80 (2.5s)"
--- check case | open_link with no settings uses link's defaults
open_link("a.io")
=> "a.io:443 (5.0s)"

=== py2-07 | Debugging: the mutable default argument
--- teach
Last lesson ended with a warning: a default value is worked out once, when the `def` line runs. This lesson is a bug hunt that shows why that matters. First, though, the **method**, because the method is what you keep. You will use it on every bug you ever meet.

Picture a detective. She does not guess who did it. She collects facts, checks each story, and only then names the culprit. Debugging works the same way.

## The debugging method

1. **Reproduce it.** Find the [[smallest set of calls|minimal-repro]] that shows the problem, every time.
2. **State what you expected and what you got.** Be precise: "the second call returned `['tea', 'milk']`, I expected `['milk']`".
3. **Check your assumptions.** The bug lives in something you believe that is not true. List what you are [[assuming|rubber-duck]] and test each one.
4. **Inspect.** Print values, or print `id(x)`, which is the identity of an object. Two names with the same `id` are the *same* object.
5. **Fix the cause, not the symptom.** A patch that clears the list after each call hides the problem. Understanding *why* the list is shared removes it.

## Step 1 and 2: the bug, reproduced

```python
def add_item(item, cart=[]):
    cart.append(item)
    return cart

add_item("tea")      # ['tea']
add_item("milk")     # ['tea', 'milk']   <- a new customer got the old cart
```

Expected: the second call returns `['milk']`. Got: `['tea', 'milk']`.

## Step 3: the assumption

The assumption that fails is: "`cart=[]` gives me a new empty list on each call". It does not. The default is created **once, when the `def` line runs**, and stored on the function. Every call that leaves `cart` out gets *that same list*, and `append` changes it in place.

## Step 4: inspect

Every value in Python is an [[object|object-word]]: a thing stored in the computer's memory. Two names can point at the same object. To tell "equal" apart from "the very same", Python has two tools:

- **`id(x)`** gives a number that identifies the object `x` points at. Two names with the same `id` point at the same object.
- **`is`** asks "are these the very same object?" It is different from `==`, which asks "do these hold equal values?"

```python
a = [1, 2]
b = a              # b is a second name for the same list
c = [1, 2]         # c is a different list with the same contents

a == c             # True    same contents
a is c             # False   two different lists
a is b             # True    one list, two names
id(a) == id(b)     # True
```

Now [[point these tools at the bug|shared-list]]. Start fresh by running the `def` again, then keep what each call hands back:

```python
first = add_item("tea")
second = add_item("milk")
first is second          # True: both calls handed back the same list
id(first) == id(second)  # True
first                    # ['tea', 'milk']  the first customer's cart changed too
```

A function is an object as well. It keeps its defaults in a [[special attribute|dunder]] called `__defaults__`. (An **attribute** is a value stored on an object, reached with a dot.) `__defaults__` is a tuple of the default values, and you can look inside it:

```python
add_item.__defaults__    # (['tea', 'milk'],)
```

There is the culprit: the stored default is no longer empty.

## Step 5: fix the cause

The fix is to use a default that cannot be changed, usually `None`, and make the new list *inside* the function. The body runs on every call, so every call gets a fresh list:

```python
def add_item(item, cart=None):
    if cart is None:
        cart = []
    cart.append(item)
    return cart

add_item("tea")      # ['tea']
add_item("milk")     # ['milk']
```

Read `if cart is None:` as "if the caller did not give a cart". There is only one `None` in a whole program, so `is None` is the [[right way to test for it|is-none]].

**Watch out:** use `is None`, not `if not cart`. An empty list counts as false in an `if`, the same "empty means false" rule you met in py2-01. So an empty list the caller passes in *on purpose* is [[falsy|falsy-word]] too, and `if not cart` would swap it for a new list. You must still add to *that* list, the caller's own.

## The same trap everywhere

The same trap catches `{}`, `set()`, and any object you create in a default. It is so common that [[linters|linter]] flag it on sight.

::: context minimal-repro Shrink it first
Engineers call this a minimal reproducible example: the fewest lines that still show the bug. Shrinking is powerful because every line you remove without losing the bug is a line that is not guilty. Often the cause becomes plain while you shrink. It is also what other people ask for when you want help: a question with ten lines that reproduce a bug gets answered far faster than one with a whole program.
:::

::: context rubber-duck Say it out loud
A well-known trick for step 3 is rubber duck debugging, from the book *The Pragmatic Programmer*: explain your code, line by line, to a rubber duck on your desk. Saying "and here `cart` is a new empty list" out loud forces you to notice you have never checked it. Writing your assumptions down as a list does the same job.
:::

::: context object-word What an object is
An object is one value living in the computer's memory: a number, a string, a list, a dict, even a function. A variable is a name that points at an object, like a label tied to a box. Two labels can be tied to the same box. In the usual Python, `id` is the object's memory address, and no two objects that exist at the same time share an `id`.
:::

::: context shared-list One list, three labels

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 160" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="14" y="16" width="130" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="79" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">__defaults__</text>
  <rect x="14" y="66" width="130" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="79" y="86" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">first</text>
  <rect x="14" y="116" width="130" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="79" y="136" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">second</text>
  <line x1="144" y1="31" x2="222" y2="74" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="144" y1="81" x2="222" y2="81" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="144" y1="131" x2="222" y2="88" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="226" y="60" width="122" height="42" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="287" y="86" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">['tea', 'milk']</text>
  <text x="287" y="122" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">one list in memory</text>
</svg>
```

Three names, one object. A change made through any name shows up through all of them, which is why the first customer's cart grew when the second customer shopped.
:::

::: context dunder Double underscores
Names with two underscores on each side, like `__defaults__`, belong to Python itself. Programmers say them "dunder defaults", short for "double underscore". They hold the machinery that makes objects work. You rarely change them, but reading one is a good way to look under the hood, as here. You will meet more of them, such as `__repr__` and `__eq__`, when you write your own classes.
:::

::: context is-none Why is and not ==
`None` is a single object: every `None` in a program is the same one. So `is None` asks exactly the right question, "is this that one object?", and it is quick. `== None` usually gives the same answer, but a class can change what `==` means for its objects, so style guides for Python say to write `is None`.
:::

::: context falsy-word Falsy and truthy
In an `if`, Python treats some values as false even though they are not the value `False`: `0`, `""`, `[]`, `{}`, `set()` and `None`. Programmers call these values falsy, and every other value truthy. That is handy for "is this list empty?", but it lumps together "the caller gave me an empty list" and "the caller gave me nothing". When those two mean different things, test for `None` directly.
:::

::: context linter A spell-checker for code
A linter is a program that reads your code without running it and points out likely mistakes. The popular Python linters all catch this one: Pylint calls it `dangerous-default-value` (W0102), and flake8-bugbear and Ruff call it B006. Flight software teams lean hard on tools like these. NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory has a well-known set of ten coding rules for safety-critical code, and one of them says data should live in the smallest scope possible, which is the same instinct as "make the list inside the call".
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** in the ordering system, the second customer's basket already contains the first customer's items. And the word tally for one document includes words from a previous document.

1. **Reproduce both bugs.** Add lines at the bottom that call `add_to_basket("tea")` and then `add_to_basket("milk")`, and print what each returns. Do the same with `tally(["a"])` and then `tally(["b"])`. You can also print `add_to_basket.__defaults__`.
2. **Fix the cause** in both functions. Make each default `None`, and inside the function, `if` it `is None`, make a new empty list (for `basket`) or dict (for `counts`).

Both functions must still add to a basket or dict that the caller passes in, even an empty one, and return that same object.
--- starter
def add_to_basket(item, basket=[]):
    basket.append(item)
    return basket


def tally(words, counts={}):
    for word in words:
        counts[word] = counts.get(word, 0) + 1
    return counts
--- solution
def add_to_basket(item, basket=None):
    if basket is None:
        basket = []
    basket.append(item)
    return basket


def tally(words, counts=None):
    if counts is None:
        counts = {}
    for word in words:
        counts[word] = counts.get(word, 0) + 1
    return counts
--- hint
Reproduce first: call `add_to_basket` twice without a basket, then print `add_to_basket.__defaults__`. What do you see inside the tuple?
--- hint
The shared list is made once, in the `def` line. Change the default to `None` (`basket=None`, `counts=None`) and make the empty list or dict inside the function, where it runs on every call.
--- hint
The first lines of the body are `if basket is None:` and, indented under it, `basket = []`. In `tally` it is `if counts is None:` and `counts = {}`. Use `is None` so an empty basket the caller passes in is still used.
--- check test | Two new baskets do not share items
add_to_basket("tea") == ["tea"] and add_to_basket("milk") == ["milk"]
?? Each call without a basket must start from an empty list made during that call.
--- check test | A basket you pass in is added to and returned
(lambda b: add_to_basket("jam", b) is b and b == ["bread", "jam"])(["bread"])
--- check test | An empty basket you pass in is still the one used
(lambda b: add_to_basket("egg", b) is b)([])
?? Check `is None`, not `if not basket`: an empty list is falsy too.
--- check test | Two new tallies are independent
tally(["a", "b", "a"]) == {"a": 2, "b": 1} and tally(["c"]) == {"c": 1}
--- check test | A dict you pass in keeps counting
(lambda d: tally(["x", "x"], d) is d and d == {"x": 3})({"x": 1})

+++ practice | A roster that remembers too much
--- task
`register(name, roster={})` gives `name` the next badge number (1 for the first person, 2 for the second, and so on) and returns the roster, a dict from name to badge number.

**Bug report:** each new event is meant to start a new roster, but the second call `register("lin")` returns `{"ada": 1, "lin": 2}` instead of `{"lin": 1}`.

Fix the cause: make the default `None`, and make a new empty dict inside the function when the caller gave no roster. A roster the caller passes in, even an empty one, must still be the one that is added to and returned.
--- starter
def register(name, roster={}):
    roster[name] = len(roster) + 1
    return roster
--- solution
def register(name, roster=None):
    if roster is None:
        roster = {}
    roster[name] = len(roster) + 1
    return roster
--- hint
Reproduce it: call `register("ada")` and then `register("lin")`, and print `register.__defaults__`. The dict inside it is the one every call shares.
--- hint
The default dict is made once, when the `def` line runs. Default to `None` instead, and create the dict in the body with `if roster is None:`, so it is made on every call that needs it.
--- check test | Two new rosters do not share names
register("ada") == {"ada": 1} and register("lin") == {"lin": 1}
--- check test | A roster you pass in is added to and returned
(lambda r: register("sam", r) is r and r == {"ada": 1, "sam": 2})({"ada": 1})
--- check test | An empty roster you pass in is the one used
(lambda r: register("zoe", r) is r and r == {"zoe": 1})({})
?? Test with `is None`, not `if not roster`: an empty dict is falsy too.

+++ practice | The crew that shared one list
--- task
The same trap works in a class, because `__init__` is a function too.

**Bug report:** every `Crew` made without a list of members shares the same list. After `a = Crew()` and `b = Crew()`, calling `a.join("Ada")` makes `b.members` show `["Ada"]` too.

Fix `__init__` so that each `Crew()` made without members gets its own new empty list. A list the caller passes in, even an empty one, must be the one stored in `self.members`.
--- starter
class Crew:
    def __init__(self, members=[]):
        self.members = members

    def join(self, name):
        self.members.append(name)
        return len(self.members)
--- solution
class Crew:
    def __init__(self, members=None):
        if members is None:
            members = []
        self.members = members

    def join(self, name):
        self.members.append(name)
        return len(self.members)
--- hint
The default list in `def __init__(self, members=[])` is made once, when the class is built, and every `Crew()` without members points at it.
--- hint
Default to `None`, and inside `__init__`, `if members is None:` make a new empty list before storing it on `self`.
--- check test | Two new crews are independent
(lambda a, b: (a.join("Ada"), b.members)[1] == [])(Crew(), Crew())
--- check test | Each new crew counts from 1
Crew().join("Lin") == 1 and Crew().join("Sam") == 1
--- check test | A list you pass in is the one stored
(lambda xs: Crew(xs).members is xs)(["Ada"])
--- check test | An empty list you pass in is the one stored
(lambda xs: (Crew(xs).join("Tim"), xs)[1] == ["Tim"])([])

+++ practice | Collect into a list, or a new one
--- task
Write `collect(*items, into=None)`. It adds every item to the end of the list `into`, in order, and returns that list. If no list is given, it makes a new empty list for this call. `into` comes after `*items`, so it can only be given by name.

For example, `collect(1, 2)` gives `[1, 2]`, and a second `collect(3)` gives `[3]`, not `[1, 2, 3]`. With `box = ["a"]`, `collect("b", "c", into=box)` changes `box` to `["a", "b", "c"]` and returns `box` itself.
--- starter
def collect(*items, into=[]):
    for item in items:
        into.append(item)
    return into
--- solution
def collect(*items, into=None):
    if into is None:
        into = []
    for item in items:
        into.append(item)
    return into
--- hint
The starter has the mutable default trap. What default can never be changed?
--- hint
Default `into` to `None`, and make the new list inside the function with `if into is None:`. Keep the loop that appends.
--- check test | Two calls without a list are independent
collect(1, 2) == [1, 2] and collect(3) == [3]
--- check test | A list you give is added to and returned
(lambda box: collect("b", "c", into=box) is box and box == ["a", "b", "c"])(["a"])
--- check test | An empty list you give is still the one used
(lambda box: collect(9, into=box) is box and box == [9])([])
--- check case | No items gives an empty list
collect()
=> []

+++ practice | Add an item without touching the original
--- task
Write `with_item(items, item)`. It returns a **new** list: everything in `items`, in order, then `item` at the end. The list passed in must not change, because other code is still using it.

For example, with `stores = ["fuel", "food"]`, `with_item(stores, "water")` gives `["fuel", "food", "water"]`, and afterwards `stores` is still `["fuel", "food"]`.
--- starter
def with_item(items, item):
    items.append(item)
    return items
--- solution
def with_item(items, item):
    result = list(items)
    result.append(item)
    return result
--- hint
The starter's `items` is a second name for the caller's own list, so `append` changes the caller's list. You need a separate list to add to.
--- hint
`list(items)` makes a new list with the same contents. Append to that one and return it.
--- check case | The item is added at the end
with_item(["fuel", "food"], "water")
=> ["fuel", "food", "water"]
--- check test | The original list is not changed
(lambda xs: (with_item(xs, "water"), xs)[1] == ["fuel", "food"])(["fuel", "food"])
--- check test | The answer is a different list, even from an empty one
(lambda xs: with_item(xs, 1) is not xs)([])
--- check case | Starting from an empty list
with_item([], "first")
=> ["first"]

+++ practice | Debug: the empty notebook that was thrown away
--- task
Someone already fixed the mutable default in `add_note`, but the fix has a new bug.

`add_note(note, notes=None)` should add `note`, with the spaces at its ends removed, to the list `notes` and return that list, or start a new list when none is given. That part works. **The bug:** when the caller passes an **empty** list on purpose, like `page = []` and then `add_note("hi", page)`, the note is not added to `page`: `page` stays `[]`. Fix it.
--- starter
def add_note(note, notes=None):
    if not notes:
        notes = []
    notes.append(note.strip())
    return notes
--- solution
def add_note(note, notes=None):
    if notes is None:
        notes = []
    notes.append(note.strip())
    return notes
--- hint
Which values count as false in an `if`? An empty list is one of them.
--- hint
`if not notes:` is true for `None` and also for the caller's empty list, which then gets swapped for a new one. Ask exactly the question you mean: `is None`.
--- check test | An empty list you pass in gets the note
(lambda page: (add_note(" hi ", page), page)[1] == ["hi"])([])
--- check test | The same list comes back
(lambda page: add_note("x", page) is page)([])
--- check test | Two calls without a list are independent
add_note("a") == ["a"] and add_note("b") == ["b"]
--- check test | A list with notes in it is added to
(lambda page: (add_note("c", page), page)[1] == ["a", "c"])(["a"])

+++ practice | A mission calendar
--- task
A calendar is a dict from a day (a number) to the list of tasks on that day. Write two functions:

1. `plan(task, day, calendar=None)` adds `task` to the end of that day's list and returns the calendar. With no calendar given, it starts a **new** one for this call. A calendar the caller passes in, even an empty one, is the one added to. Use `setdefault` for the day's list.
2. `tasks_on(calendar, day)` returns a **new** list of that day's tasks, or `[]` if the day has none. It must not add the day to the calendar, and changing the list it returns must not change the calendar.

For example, `plan("fuel", 3)` gives `{3: ["fuel"]}`, and a second `plan("test", 5)` gives `{5: ["test"]}`.
--- starter
def plan(task, day, calendar={}):
    calendar.setdefault(day, []).append(task)
    return calendar


def tasks_on(calendar, day):
    return calendar.setdefault(day, [])
--- solution
def plan(task, day, calendar=None):
    if calendar is None:
        calendar = {}
    calendar.setdefault(day, []).append(task)
    return calendar


def tasks_on(calendar, day):
    return list(calendar.get(day, []))
--- hint
`plan` has the mutable default trap. Fix it the usual way, with `None` and `is None`.
--- hint
In `tasks_on`, `setdefault` stores a new empty list under a missing day, which changes the calendar. `get` only reads.
--- hint
Even `get` hands back the calendar's own list when the day exists. Wrap it in `list(...)` so the caller gets a copy.
--- check test | Two new calendars are independent
plan("fuel", 3) == {3: ["fuel"]} and plan("test", 5) == {5: ["test"]}
--- check test | A calendar you pass in is added to
(lambda c: plan("check", 1, c) is c and c == {1: ["brief", "check"]})({1: ["brief"]})
--- check test | An empty calendar you pass in is the one used
(lambda c: plan("go", 2, c) is c and c == {2: ["go"]})({})
--- check test | tasks_on does not add a missing day
(lambda c: tasks_on(c, 9) == [] and c == {1: ["a"]})({1: ["a"]})
--- check test | Changing what tasks_on returns does not change the calendar
(lambda c: (tasks_on(c, 1).append("x"), c)[1] == {1: ["a"]})({1: ["a"]})

=== py2-08 | Exceptions you design
--- teach
Last lesson you hunted a bug by asking "what am I assuming that is not true?". In the basics you also met exceptions: `try` / `except ValueError:` to catch one, and `raise ValueError(...)` to send one yourself. This lesson finishes that story. You will learn the rest of the `try` statement, and then how to invent exceptions of your own, with their own names and their own data.

Think of a hospital. A small clinic sees you first. If it cannot fix the problem, it sends you on to a bigger hospital, with a note saying exactly what is wrong. The note has a clear name for the problem ("broken wrist", not "something hurts"), so the next doctor knows at once whether they can handle it. Exceptions work the same way: a function that cannot do its job sends a named problem, with details, to whoever called it.

### A quick reminder

```python
try:
    n = int("forty-two")
except ValueError:
    n = 0
print(n)          # 0
```

Python tries the indented lines under `try`. If one of them raises a `ValueError`, it jumps to the `except` block. If nothing goes wrong, the `except` block is skipped.

### Getting hold of the error: `as err`

An exception is not only a name. It is a real value, an [[object you can look at|error-object]], and it carries the message. Add `as` and a name to the `except` line, and Python puts the exception in that variable:

```python
try:
    n = int("forty-two")
except ValueError as err:
    print("not a number:", err)
# not a number: invalid literal for int() with base 10: 'forty-two'
```

Read `except ValueError as err:` as "if a `ValueError` happens, call it `err`". Printing `err`, or putting it in an f-string as `f"{err}"`, gives its message. `str(err)` gives the same message as a string. The name `err` is only a habit; `e` and `error` are common too.

### `else`: the success path

A `try` can have an `else` block after its `except` blocks. `else` runs only if **no** exception happened in the `try`:

```python
text = "42"
try:
    value = int(text)          # the line that might fail
except ValueError:
    print("not a number")      # runs only if that failed
else:
    print("got", value)        # runs only if it worked
# got 42
```

Why not put `print("got", value)` inside the `try`, under the `int` line? Because then a mistake in *that* line would also be caught by `except ValueError`, and you would print "not a number" for a problem that had nothing to do with the number. The rule is: **only the line that can fail goes in `try`**. The code that should run on success goes in `else`, where the `except` cannot swallow its errors by accident.

### `finally`: clean-up that always runs

A `finally` block goes last. It runs **every time**: after the `try` worked, after an `except` handled an error, and even when an error was not caught at all.

```python
text = "abc"
try:
    value = int(text)
except ValueError:
    print("not a number")
else:
    print("got", value)
finally:
    print("done")
# not a number
# done
```

With `text = "42"` it would print `got 42` and then `done`. `finally` is for [[clean-up|cleanup]] that must happen whatever occurs, such as closing a file you opened or handing back something you borrowed.

So the full shape, in order, is: `try`, then one or more `except`, then an optional `else`, then an optional `finally`.

### More than one `except`

One `try` can have several `except` blocks, one for each kind of problem. Python checks them from top to bottom and runs the **first one that matches**:

```python
def describe(text):
    try:
        share = 100 / int(text)
    except ZeroDivisionError:
        return "cannot share among zero"
    except ValueError:
        return "not a number"
    else:
        return f"each gets {share}"

print(describe("4"))     # each gets 25.0
print(describe("0"))     # cannot share among zero
print(describe("four"))  # not a number
```

### Your own kind of exception

`ValueError` is a very general name. In a bigger program you want names that say exactly what went wrong in *your* program, so the code that calls yours can catch exactly that. An exception type is a class, so you make a new one with `class`:

```python
class ShippingError(Exception):
    """Base class for everything that can go wrong with shipping."""
```

Two new things here.

- The `(Exception)` in brackets after the name means "this class is a kind of `Exception`". You will see what that gives you in a moment.
- The line in three double quote marks, `"""..."""`, is a **[[docstring|docstring]]**: a string on the first line of a class (or a function) that says what it is for. A class body needs at least one line, and a docstring is enough. `pass`, as in the basics, would also work.

Now you can raise it and catch it like any other exception. You raise an **instance** of it, meaning one object made from the class, written with brackets and a message:

```python
try:
    raise ShippingError("no address")
except ShippingError as err:
    print("shipping problem:", err)
# shipping problem: no address
```

### A family of exceptions

Classes can have parents. Write another class with `ShippingError` in its brackets:

```python
class TooHeavy(ShippingError):
    pass
```

Now `TooHeavy` is a **subclass** of `ShippingError`: a more specific kind of it. `ShippingError` is its **parent class**. `TooHeavy` **[[inherits|inherit-word]]** from its parent, meaning it gets everything the parent has, for free, plus anything it adds. A `TooHeavy` *is* a `ShippingError`, in the same way a sparrow is a bird. And since `ShippingError` inherits from `Exception`, a `TooHeavy` is an `Exception` too.

This is what makes a family useful. An `except` for the parent also catches every child:

```python
try:
    raise TooHeavy("32 kg")
except ShippingError as err:     # catches TooHeavy too: it IS a ShippingError
    print("shipping problem:", err)
# shipping problem: 32 kg
```

So callers get a choice. Catch `TooHeavy` to handle only that case, or catch `ShippingError` to handle anything that can go wrong with shipping.

Because `except` blocks are tried from top to bottom and the first match wins, put the **most specific** classes first. If `except ShippingError:` came first, it would catch a `TooHeavy` and an `except TooHeavy:` below it would never run.

### Carrying data

An exception can carry more than a message. Give it an `__init__`, like any class, and store the details on `self`:

```python
class TooHeavy(ShippingError):
    def __init__(self, weight, limit):
        super().__init__(f"{weight} kg is over the {limit} kg limit")
        self.weight = weight
        self.limit = limit
```

The new word is `super`. `super()` means "the parent class", so `super().__init__(message)` runs the **parent's** `__init__` with your message. That is [[the step that sets the message|super-call]] you see when the exception is printed. Then `self.weight = weight` and `self.limit = limit` store the details, as in any class.

Now the code that catches it can use both the message and the numbers:

```python
try:
    raise TooHeavy(32, 30)
except ShippingError as err:
    print(err)            # 32 kg is over the 30 kg limit
    print(err.weight)     # 32
```

**Watch out:** two mistakes are very common here.

- `raise TooHeavy` with no brackets fails. Python tries to make a `TooHeavy` with no arguments, but `__init__` needs a weight and a limit, so you get a `TypeError` instead of your exception. Always raise an instance with its values: `raise TooHeavy(32, 30)`.
- `except Exception:` wrapped around a big block of code catches almost everything, including bugs you never expected, and hides them. Catch only the exceptions you can really deal with, [[in the place that can deal with them|handle-where]].

::: context error-object The exception is a value
When Python raises a `ValueError`, it makes an object of the class `ValueError`, the same way `Account("ada")` made an object of your `Account` class. `as err` gives that object a name, so you can look at it. `type(err)` tells you its class, and `err.args` is a tuple holding what was passed in when it was made, such as `("whole must not be zero",)`. Printing it shows the message. Because it is an ordinary object, your own exception classes can store extra attributes on it, which is what this lesson builds up to.
:::

::: context cleanup The last one out turns off the lights
Some jobs have a "put it back" step that must happen even when things go wrong: close the file, hang up the connection, unlock the door. `finally` is the place for it. It runs even when the `try` block leaves early with `return`:

```python
def f():
    try:
        return 1
    finally:
        print("fin")
```

Calling `f()` prints `fin` and then hands back `1`. Python also has a `with` statement that runs this kind of clean-up for you, which is how files are usually opened.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">

  <line x1="95" y1="116" x2="150" y2="147" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#cu-arrow)"/>
```
