```svg
  <text x="180" y="138" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">always</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context docstring Notes the code carries with it
A docstring is a string that sits on the first line inside a class or a function. Python keeps it and attaches it to that class or function, so tools can show it. Try `help(ShippingError)` in Python: it prints the class name and your docstring. Ordinary `#` comments are thrown away when the program runs; docstrings are kept. Engineers use them to say what a function expects and what it returns, so the next person does not have to read every line to find out.
:::

::: context inherit-word A family tree of errors
"Inherit" means to receive something from a parent, like eye color or a grandmother's clock. Python's own exceptions already form a family tree. `ZeroDivisionError` is a child of `ArithmeticError`, which is a child of `Exception`. `KeyError` is a child of `LookupError`. So `except ArithmeticError:` would catch a division by zero too. Your own classes join the same tree:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="120" y="10" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="32" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Exception</text>
  <rect x="20" y="70" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="80" y="92" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ValueError</text>
  <rect x="220" y="70" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="280" y="92" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ShippingError</text>
  <rect x="220" y="128" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="280" y="150" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">TooHeavy</text>
  <line x1="160" y1="44" x2="95" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="200" y1="44" x2="265" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="280" y1="104" x2="280" y2="128" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="80" y="140" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">parent above, child below</text>
</svg>
```

An `except` for any box catches everything in the boxes below it.
:::

::: context super-call Why call the parent's __init__
`Exception` has its own `__init__`, and it stores what you pass it as the message. When you write your own `__init__`, yours runs instead of the parent's. `super().__init__(...)` is how you still run the parent's one, with the message you want. Leave it out and Python falls back to showing the raw values that were passed in: `str(TooHeavy(32, 30))` would be `(32, 30)` instead of a helpful sentence. You will see `super()` again whenever a child class needs to do everything its parent does, plus a little more.
:::

::: context handle-where Let the right level decide
An exception travels up the chain of calls, from the function that raised it to the one that called it, and so on, until an `except` catches it. The low-level code that notices a problem often cannot know what to do about it; the code higher up can. Spacecraft are built on the same idea. A sensor driver reports that a reading is bad, and a higher-level fault manager decides what to do: switch to a backup sensor, retry, or put the spacecraft into safe mode, where it points its solar panels at the Sun and waits for instructions from the ground. Named, specific errors are what make those decisions possible.
:::
--- task
Build a small withdrawal system for a bank account. You need these four pieces.

- `class LedgerError(Exception)`, the base class for the family. The starter already has it.
- `class InsufficientFunds(LedgerError)`, created as `InsufficientFunds(shortfall)`, where `shortfall` is how much money is missing. Its `__init__` calls `super().__init__(...)` with the message `f"short by {shortfall}"`, and stores `self.shortfall`.
- `withdraw(balance, amount)` returns the new balance, `balance - amount`. Before that, it raises `ValueError` (with any message) if `amount` is 0 or less, and raises `InsufficientFunds(amount - balance)` if `amount` is more than `balance`.
- `attempt(balance, amount)` calls `withdraw(balance, amount)` inside a `try` and returns a string. If that raised `InsufficientFunds`, return `"refused: short by <shortfall>"` (use `except InsufficientFunds as err:` and put `err` in an f-string). If it raised `ValueError`, return `"invalid"`. Otherwise, in an `else` block, return `"ok: <new balance>"`.

So `attempt(100, 40)` gives `"ok: 60"`, `attempt(20, 50)` gives `"refused: short by 30"`, and `attempt(20, -1)` gives `"invalid"`.
--- starter
class LedgerError(Exception):
    pass


def withdraw(balance, amount):
    return balance - amount


def attempt(balance, amount):
    return "ok: " + str(withdraw(balance, amount))
--- solution
class LedgerError(Exception):
    pass


class InsufficientFunds(LedgerError):
    def __init__(self, shortfall):
        super().__init__(f"short by {shortfall}")
        self.shortfall = shortfall


def withdraw(balance, amount):
    if amount <= 0:
        raise ValueError("amount must be positive")
    if amount > balance:
        raise InsufficientFunds(amount - balance)
    return balance - amount


def attempt(balance, amount):
    try:
        new_balance = withdraw(balance, amount)
    except InsufficientFunds as err:
        return f"refused: {err}"
    except ValueError:
        return "invalid"
    else:
        return f"ok: {new_balance}"
--- hint
Copy the shape of `TooHeavy` for `InsufficientFunds`: the parent's name in brackets, an `__init__(self, shortfall)` that calls `super().__init__(f"short by {shortfall}")`, then `self.shortfall = shortfall`.
--- hint
In `withdraw`, check the bad cases first with `if`, and raise an instance: `raise ValueError("amount must be positive")` when `amount <= 0` ("less than or equal to 0"), and `raise InsufficientFunds(amount - balance)` when `amount > balance`. Only then `return balance - amount`.
--- hint
In `attempt`, put only `new_balance = withdraw(balance, amount)` inside `try`. Then `except InsufficientFunds as err: return f"refused: {err}"`, then `except ValueError: return "invalid"`, then `else: return f"ok: {new_balance}"`.
--- check case | A valid withdrawal returns the new balance
withdraw(100, 30)
=> 70
--- check case | Withdrawing everything is allowed
withdraw(50, 50)
=> 0
--- check test | Overdrawing raises InsufficientFunds (a LedgerError)
raises(InsufficientFunds, lambda: withdraw(20, 50)) and raises(LedgerError, lambda: withdraw(0, 1))
--- check test | InsufficientFunds is a LedgerError and carries the shortfall
issubclass(InsufficientFunds, LedgerError) and InsufficientFunds(30).shortfall == 30 and str(InsufficientFunds(30)) == "short by 30"
--- check test | Zero or negative amounts raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: withdraw(10, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: withdraw(10, -5))
--- check case | attempt reports success
attempt(100, 40)
=> "ok: 60"
--- check case | attempt reports a refusal with the shortfall
attempt(20, 50)
=> "refused: short by 30"
--- check case | attempt reports an invalid amount
attempt(20, -1)
=> "invalid"

+++ practice | A percentage that survives a zero
--- task
Write `percent(part, whole)`. It returns `part / whole * 100`. If `whole` is zero, the division raises `ZeroDivisionError`: catch it with `try` / `except` and return `None` instead. Put only the division in the `try`, and return the answer from an `else` block.

For example, `percent(1, 4)` gives `25.0`, and `percent(3, 0)` gives `None`.
--- starter
def percent(part, whole):
    return part / whole * 100
--- solution
def percent(part, whole):
    try:
        share = part / whole
    except ZeroDivisionError:
        return None
    else:
        return share * 100
--- hint
The line that can fail is the division. Put it alone under `try:` and store its result in a variable.
--- hint
Under `except ZeroDivisionError:` return `None`. Under `else:`, which runs only when the division worked, return the result times 100.
--- check case | A quarter is 25 percent
percent(1, 4)
=> 25.0
--- check case | Dividing by zero gives None
percent(3, 0)
=> None
--- check case | Nothing of something is zero percent
percent(0, 8)
=> 0.0
--- check case | More than the whole
percent(3, 2)
=> 150.0

+++ practice | An exception that knows the range
--- task
Write an exception class `OutOfRange` that is a kind of `Exception`. It is created as `OutOfRange(value, low, high)`. Its `__init__` calls `super().__init__(...)` with the message `f"{value} is not between {low} and {high}"`, then stores `self.value`, `self.low` and `self.high`.

Then write `check_range(value, low, high)`. It returns `value` if `low <= value <= high` (both ends allowed), and otherwise raises `OutOfRange(value, low, high)`.

For example, `check_range(5, 1, 10)` gives `5`, and `check_range(12, 1, 10)` raises `OutOfRange` with the message `"12 is not between 1 and 10"`.
--- starter
class OutOfRange(Exception):
    pass


def check_range(value, low, high):
    return value
--- solution
class OutOfRange(Exception):
    def __init__(self, value, low, high):
        super().__init__(f"{value} is not between {low} and {high}")
        self.value = value
        self.low = low
        self.high = high


def check_range(value, low, high):
    if value < low or value > high:
        raise OutOfRange(value, low, high)
    return value
--- hint
Follow the shape of `TooHeavy` in the lesson: an `__init__` with the three values, a `super().__init__(...)` call with the message, then one `self.` line for each value.
--- hint
In `check_range`, raise an instance, with its three values, when `value < low or value > high`. Otherwise return `value`.
--- check case | A value inside the range comes back
check_range(5, 1, 10)
=> 5
--- check case | Both ends of the range are allowed
check_range(1, 1, 10) + check_range(10, 1, 10)
=> 11
--- check test | A value outside raises OutOfRange
raises(OutOfRange, lambda: check_range(12, 1, 10)) and raises(OutOfRange, lambda: check_range(0, 1, 10))
--- check test | The exception carries its message and data
(lambda e: str(e) == "12 is not between 1 and 10" and e.value == 12 and e.low == 1 and e.high == 10)(OutOfRange(12, 1, 10))
--- check test | OutOfRange is a kind of Exception
issubclass(OutOfRange, Exception)

+++ practice | Run a step and keep a log
--- task
Write `run_step(step, log)`. `step` is a function that takes no arguments, and `log` is a list. `run_step` calls `step()` inside a `try` and adds lines to the end of `log`:

- if `step()` raised a `ValueError`, add `"bad value: <message>"` (use `as err` and put `err` in an f-string) and return `False`;
- if it raised a `ZeroDivisionError`, add `"divided by zero"` and return `False`;
- otherwise, in an `else` block, add `"ok: <result>"` and return `True`;
- in **every** case, add `"done"` last, from a `finally` block.

For example, `run_step(lambda: 6 * 7, log)` adds `"ok: 42"` then `"done"`, and returns `True`.
--- starter
def run_step(step, log):
    result = step()
    log.append(f"ok: {result}")
    return True
--- solution
def run_step(step, log):
    try:
        result = step()
    except ValueError as err:
        log.append(f"bad value: {err}")
        return False
    except ZeroDivisionError:
        log.append("divided by zero")
        return False
    else:
        log.append(f"ok: {result}")
        return True
    finally:
        log.append("done")
--- hint
The full shape is `try`, then the two `except` blocks, then `else`, then `finally`. Only `result = step()` goes under `try`.
--- hint
A `finally` block runs even when the block before it has already said `return`, so `log.append("done")` in `finally` always happens, and always last.
--- check test | A step that works
(lambda log: run_step(lambda: 6 * 7, log) is True and log == ["ok: 42", "done"])([])
--- check test | A step that raises ValueError
(lambda log: run_step(lambda: int("abc"), log) is False and log == ["bad value: invalid literal for int() with base 10: 'abc'", "done"])([])
--- check test | A step that divides by zero
(lambda log: run_step(lambda: 1 / 0, log) is False and log == ["divided by zero", "done"])([])
--- check test | Lines are added after what the log already holds
(lambda log: (run_step(lambda: "x", log), log)[1] == ["start", "ok: x", "done"])(["start"])

+++ practice | Durations that can go wrong
--- task
A mission timer takes durations written as a number, a space, and a unit: `"90 s"`, `"5 min"` or `"2 h"`. Write:

1. `class DurationError(ValueError)`: a kind of `ValueError`, so code that already catches `ValueError` still catches it. It needs nothing more than a docstring or `pass`.
2. `seconds(text)`, which returns the duration in seconds as an `int`: `s` means seconds, `min` means minutes (60 seconds) and `h` means hours (3600 seconds). It raises `DurationError` for every bad input:
   - not exactly two parts after `split()`, such as `""`, `"5"` or `"5 min now"`;
   - a unit that is not `s`, `min` or `h`;
   - a number part that `int` cannot read, such as `"five s"`;
   - a negative number, such as `"-3 s"`.

For example, `seconds("5 min")` gives `300`, and `seconds("0 h")` gives `0`.
--- starter
class DurationError(ValueError):
    pass


def seconds(text):
    number, unit = text.split()
    if unit == "min":
        return int(number) * 60
    if unit == "h":
        return int(number) * 3600
    return int(number)
--- solution
class DurationError(ValueError):
    pass


UNITS = {"s": 1, "min": 60, "h": 3600}


def seconds(text):
    parts = text.split()
    if len(parts) != 2:
        raise DurationError(f"expected a number and a unit: {text}")
    number, unit = parts
    if unit not in UNITS:
        raise DurationError(f"unknown unit: {unit}")
    try:
        amount = int(number)
    except ValueError:
        raise DurationError(f"not a number: {number}")
    if amount < 0:
        raise DurationError(f"negative duration: {amount}")
    return amount * UNITS[unit]
--- hint
Check each way it can go wrong, one at a time, before you do the sum. A dict from each unit to how many seconds it is, like `{"s": 1, "min": 60, "h": 3600}`, makes the unit check and the sum one line each.
--- hint
`int("five")` raises a `ValueError`. Catch that with `try` / `except ValueError:` and raise a `DurationError` instead, so every bad input gives the same, named error.
--- hint
Split first and check `len(parts) != 2` before unpacking, so `""` and `"5"` raise `DurationError` rather than a plain unpacking `ValueError`.
--- check case | Minutes
seconds("5 min")
=> 300
--- check case | Hours and seconds
seconds("2 h") + seconds("90 s")
=> 7290
--- check case | Zero is allowed
seconds("0 h")
=> 0
--- check test | Wrong number of parts raises DurationError
raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("")) and raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("5")) and raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("5 min now"))
--- check test | An unknown unit raises DurationError
raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("3 days"))
--- check test | A bad number raises DurationError
raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("five s"))
--- check test | A negative duration raises DurationError
raises(DurationError, lambda: seconds("-3 s"))
--- check test | DurationError is still a ValueError
issubclass(DurationError, ValueError)

+++ practice | Debug: the timeout that looked like a fault
--- task
A sensor can fail in two ways. `SensorTimeout` is a kind of `SensorError`, meaning the sensor did not answer in time and it is worth asking again. Any other `SensorError` means the sensor is broken.

`read_status(read)` calls `read()` and should return:

- `"timed out, retry"` if it raised `SensorTimeout`;
- `"sensor fault"` if it raised any other `SensorError`;
- `"ok: <value>"` if it worked.

The starter has two test functions, `slow` and `broken`. **The bug:** `read_status(slow)` returns `"sensor fault"` instead of `"timed out, retry"`. Fix `read_status`.
--- starter
class SensorError(Exception):
    pass


class SensorTimeout(SensorError):
    pass


def slow():
    raise SensorTimeout("no reply in 2 s")


def broken():
    raise SensorError("short circuit")


def read_status(read):
    try:
        value = read()
    except SensorError:
        return "sensor fault"
    except SensorTimeout:
        return "timed out, retry"
    else:
        return f"ok: {value}"
--- solution
class SensorError(Exception):
    pass


class SensorTimeout(SensorError):
    pass


def slow():
    raise SensorTimeout("no reply in 2 s")


def broken():
    raise SensorError("short circuit")


def read_status(read):
    try:
        value = read()
    except SensorTimeout:
        return "timed out, retry"
    except SensorError:
        return "sensor fault"
    else:
        return f"ok: {value}"
--- hint
Python tries the `except` blocks from top to bottom and runs the first one that matches. Does `except SensorError:` match a `SensorTimeout`?
--- hint
A child class is caught by its parent's `except`. Put the most specific class first.
--- check case | A timeout asks for a retry
read_status(slow)
=> "timed out, retry"
--- check case | Any other sensor error is a fault
read_status(broken)
=> "sensor fault"
--- check case | A good reading
read_status(lambda: 21.5)
=> "ok: 21.5"

+++ practice | Fuel transfers
--- task
Fuel is moved from a source tank to a destination tank that holds at most `capacity` kilograms. Write:

1. `class TransferError(Exception)`, the base class.
2. `class Overflow(TransferError)`, created as `Overflow(excess)`, with the message `f"overflow by {excess}"` and the attribute `self.excess`.
3. `class Empty(TransferError)`, created as `Empty(shortfall)`, with the message `f"short by {shortfall}"` and the attribute `self.shortfall`.
4. `transfer(src, dst, amount, capacity)`, which returns the new levels as a tuple `(src - amount, dst + amount)`. First it raises `ValueError` if `amount` is 0 or less, then `Empty(amount - src)` if the source has less than `amount`, then `Overflow(dst + amount - capacity)` if the destination would go over `capacity`.
5. `run_plan(src, dst, amounts, capacity)`, which tries each amount in order, keeping the new levels after each one that works and skipping each one that fails. It returns a list with one line per amount: `"moved <amount>"`, or the failed transfer's message for a `TransferError`, or `"invalid"` for a `ValueError`.

For example, `run_plan(100, 0, [30, 80, 50, 0], 60)` gives `["moved 30", "short by 10", "overflow by 20", "invalid"]`.
--- starter
class TransferError(Exception):
    pass


def transfer(src, dst, amount, capacity):
    return src - amount, dst + amount


def run_plan(src, dst, amounts, capacity):
    return [f"moved {amount}" for amount in amounts]
--- solution
class TransferError(Exception):
    pass


class Overflow(TransferError):
    def __init__(self, excess):
        super().__init__(f"overflow by {excess}")
        self.excess = excess


class Empty(TransferError):
    def __init__(self, shortfall):
        super().__init__(f"short by {shortfall}")
        self.shortfall = shortfall


def transfer(src, dst, amount, capacity):
    if amount <= 0:
        raise ValueError("amount must be positive")
    if amount > src:
        raise Empty(amount - src)
    if dst + amount > capacity:
        raise Overflow(dst + amount - capacity)
    return src - amount, dst + amount


def run_plan(src, dst, amounts, capacity):
    lines = []
    for amount in amounts:
        try:
            src, dst = transfer(src, dst, amount, capacity)
        except TransferError as err:
            lines.append(str(err))
        except ValueError:
            lines.append("invalid")
        else:
            lines.append(f"moved {amount}")
    return lines
--- hint
Build the two child classes the way the lesson built `TooHeavy`: `super().__init__(message)`, then store the number on `self`.
--- hint
In `transfer`, check the three bad cases in the order the task gives, each with `if` and `raise`, and only then return the tuple.
--- hint
In `run_plan`, put just `src, dst = transfer(...)` inside the `try`, so the levels only change when the transfer worked. `str(err)` gives the exception's message.
--- check case | A plan with every outcome
run_plan(100, 0, [30, 80, 50, 0], 60)
=> ["moved 30", "short by 10", "overflow by 20", "invalid"]
--- check case | A transfer that works
transfer(100, 10, 40, 60)
=> (60, 50)
--- check case | Filling the destination exactly is allowed
transfer(50, 10, 50, 60)
=> (0, 60)
--- check test | Too little fuel raises Empty with the shortfall
raises(Empty, lambda: transfer(20, 0, 50, 100)) and Empty(30).shortfall == 30 and str(Empty(30)) == "short by 30"
--- check test | Too much for the destination raises Overflow with the excess
raises(Overflow, lambda: transfer(100, 50, 20, 60)) and Overflow(10).excess == 10
--- check test | Both are TransferErrors
issubclass(Overflow, TransferError) and issubclass(Empty, TransferError)
--- check test | A zero or negative amount raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: transfer(10, 0, 0, 60)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: transfer(10, 0, -5, 60))
--- check case | Failed transfers leave the levels alone
run_plan(10, 0, [20, 10], 50)
=> ["short by 10", "moved 10"]

=== py2-09 | Debugging: reading a traceback
--- teach
Last lesson you designed exceptions for other code to catch. This lesson is about the exceptions nobody catches: the ones that stop your program and fill the screen with red text. That text is called a **traceback**, and it is a precise report of what went wrong. People new to Python see a wall of red and panic. Experienced people read it in ten seconds. By the end of this lesson, so will you.

Think of a message passed down a line of people. The boss asks a manager, the manager asks a clerk, the clerk asks an intern, and the intern trips. The report that comes back lists everyone in the chain, in order, ending with the person who tripped. A traceback is that report.

### The program

Here is a small program that works out the total cost of an order. Each order line is a string like `"tea, 3, 2.50"`: a name, a quantity (how many), and a price. The line numbers on the left are there so you can match them to the traceback; they are not part of the code.

```python fragment
 1  ORDERS = ["tea, 3, 2.50", "biscuits, 2, 1.25", "jam, 1, 7.50"]
 2
 3
 4  def parse_line(line):
 5      name, qty, price = line.split(",")
 6      return {"name": name, "qty": qty, "price": float(price)}
 7
 8
 9  def line_total(item):
10      return item["qty"] * item["price"]
11
12
13  def order_total(lines):
14      return sum(line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines)
15
16
17  print(order_total(ORDERS))
```

`parse_line` cuts one line into three pieces at the commas and puts them in a dict. `line_total` multiplies quantity by price. `order_total` adds up every line. Running it prints this:

```
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "main.py", line 17, in <module>
    print(order_total(ORDERS))
  File "main.py", line 14, in order_total
    return sum(line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines)
  File "main.py", line 14, in <genexpr>
    return sum(line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines)
  File "main.py", line 10, in line_total
    return item["qty"] * item["price"]
TypeError: can't multiply sequence by non-int of type 'float'
```

(Newer versions of Python also draw [[little arrows under the code|carets]] on some of these lines. Ignore them for now.)

### Step 1: read the last line first

**Read a traceback from the bottom up.** The last line is the error itself: its type, then a colon, then its message.

```
TypeError: can't multiply sequence by non-int of type 'float'
```

A `TypeError` means "this operation does not work on this type of value". The message says Python tried to multiply a **[[sequence|sequence]]** (a string, list or tuple: something made of items in order) by a `float`. Since the only multiplication here is quantity times price, one of those is a string.

### Step 2: the block right above is where it happened

Each pair of lines above the error is one **[[frame|frame]]**: one function that was running at the moment of the crash. The first line of a frame says the file, the line number, and the function's name. The second line shows the code on that line.

The frame right above the error is where Python actually stopped:

```
  File "main.py", line 10, in line_total
    return item["qty"] * item["price"]
```

So the crash happened in `line_total`, on line 10.

### Step 3: the blocks above that are how you got there

Keep reading upwards and you walk back along the chain of calls, out to the start of the program:

- line 17, in `<module>`: the top level of the file called `order_total`. `<module>` is [[the label Python uses|module-name]] for code that is not inside any function.
- line 14, in `order_total`: that function ran `sum(...)`.
- line 14, in `<genexpr>`: the bracket-less comprehension inside `sum(...)`. You met this shape in the string lesson: it is a [[generator expression|genexpr]], and it runs as a frame of its own.
- line 10, in `line_total`: where it crashed.

"Most recent call last" at the top of the traceback means exactly this: the outermost call is at the top, and the newest one, where the crash happened, is at the bottom.

### Step 4: where it crashed is not always where the bug is

This is the most important idea in the lesson. Look at line 10 again:

```python fragment
return item["qty"] * item["price"]
```

Multiplying a quantity by a price is **correct code**. The line is fine. It crashed because it was *given* a bad value: `item["qty"]` is the string `" 3"` (with a space in front, left over from `"tea, 3, 2.50"`), not the number `3`.

So the real question is not "what is wrong with line 10?" but **"who made that value?"** Walk back along the path the data traveled. The `item` dict came from `parse_line`. Look at line 6: it converts the price with `float(price)`, but it puts `qty` in the dict as it is, still a string. That is the bug.

### Checking your theory with `repr`

Before changing anything, prove the theory with one line of code:

```python
item = parse_line("tea,3,2.50")
print(repr(item["qty"]), type(item["qty"]))
# '3' <class 'str'>
```

**`repr(x)`** gives the text of a value the way Python would write it in code. For a string, that means **with its quotes**. A plain `print` hides the quotes, so `print("3")` and `print(3)` look exactly the same on screen. `repr` shows you [[which one you really have|repr-vs-print]]. And `type(...)`, from the basics, confirms it: a `str`, not an `int`.

### Fix the cause, not the symptom

You could write `int(item["qty"])` inside `line_total`. That would stop *this* crash. But every other piece of code that uses `parse_line` would still get a string for the quantity, and would trip over it somewhere else. That is fixing the **symptom** (the crash you can see) instead of the **cause** (the function that makes bad data).

The better fix is in `parse_line`, the place the bad value is made. Once it hands out clean data, everything after it can trust that data. Engineers call this [[fixing it at the source|fix-at-source]].

Two helpers from earlier lessons do the job. `int(" 3")` turns text into a whole number, and it ignores spaces around the digits. `" tea ".strip()` removes the spaces at both ends of a string.

```python
print(int(" 3"))            # 3
print(repr(" tea ".strip())) # 'tea'
```

**Watch out:** do not stop reading at the line where the program crashed and start "fixing" it. First ask whether that line is wrong, or whether it was handed a wrong value. Most of the time in real programs, it is the second.

::: context carets Arrows that point at the problem
Since Python 3.11, many traceback lines have a row of `^` or `~` marks underneath. They underline the exact part of the line that was running when the error happened. For line 10 of this program, the `~` marks sit under the two values and a `^` sits under the `*`, pointing straight at the multiplication. They carry no new information that the rest of the traceback does not, but they save you from guessing which part of a long line failed.
:::

::: context sequence Why a string can be multiplied at all
The message is so specific because a string *can* be multiplied, by a whole number. `"ab" * 3` repeats it three times and gives `"ababab"`. Lists work the same way: `[0] * 4` is `[0, 0, 0, 0]`. What Python cannot do is repeat something 2.5 times. So it says "can't multiply sequence by non-int": a sequence can only be multiplied by an `int`. If the price had been a whole number, `"3" * 2` would have quietly given `"33"`, with no crash at all. That is a far nastier bug, and a good reason to convert data as soon as it arrives.
:::

::: context frame A stack of calls
When a function calls another function, Python keeps the first one waiting, with all its variables, until the second one returns. Each waiting function is a frame, and the frames pile up like plates, one on top of the other. This pile is called the call stack. A traceback is a printout of the call stack at the moment of the crash:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 180" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="40" y="10" width="200" height="34" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="140" y="32" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;module&gt;  line 17</text>
  <rect x="40" y="50" width="200" height="34" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="140" y="72" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">order_total  line 14</text>
  <rect x="40" y="90" width="200" height="34" rx="5" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="140" y="112" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;genexpr&gt;  line 14</text>
  <rect x="40" y="130" width="200" height="34" rx="5" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="140" y="152" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">line_total  line 10</text>
  <text x="252" y="32" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">called first</text>
  <text x="252" y="152" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">crashed here</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context module-name The file itself is a module
In Python, every `.py` file is a module. Code written at the left margin of the file, outside every `def` and `class`, belongs to no function, so the traceback labels it `<module>`. The angle brackets show that it is not a real name you wrote, but a label Python made up. You will see it at the top of almost every traceback, because every program starts running at the top level of a file.
:::

::: context genexpr A comprehension without the brackets
`line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines`, written straight inside `sum(...)` with no square brackets, is a generator expression. It works like a list comprehension, but it hands `sum` one value at a time instead of building a whole list first. Behind the scenes, Python runs it as a small hidden function, which is why it gets its own frame, labeled `<genexpr>`, in the traceback. The advanced course shows how it works inside.
:::

::: context repr-vs-print Two ways to show a value
Every value has two text forms. `str(x)` is the friendly one, for people using the program; `print` uses it. `repr(x)` is the exact one, for programmers; it writes the value the way you would type it in code. For numbers they are the same. For strings they are not: `str("3")` is `3`, but `repr("3")` is `'3'`, with quotes. When you are hunting a bug, `repr` is almost always what you want, because it shows the quotes, and spaces at the ends of a string become visible inside them: `repr(" 3")` is `' 3'`. In a later lesson you will write your own `__repr__` so your classes show up well too.
:::

::: context fix-at-source Bad data made far from the crash
On 23 September 1999, NASA lost the Mars Climate Orbiter as it arrived at Mars. Ground software built by one team reported thruster results in pound-force seconds, an American unit. The navigation software that used those numbers expected newton-seconds, the metric unit. Each number was about 4.45 times too small, and for months the small errors added up, until the spacecraft flew far too close to Mars and was lost. The code that did the navigation maths was not where the mistake started; the data was wrong where it was made. Checking and converting data at the point where it enters a system is how engineers stop this.
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** running the order program crashes with the traceback above. It should print the order total, `17.5`.

Find the cause and fix it at the source, in `parse_line`. Leave `line_total` as it is. After your fix, `parse_line` must return a dict with:

- `"name"`: the name with the spaces at its ends removed (use `.strip()`),
- `"qty"`: the quantity as an `int` (use `int(...)`),
- `"price"`: the price as a `float`, as it already is.

It must work even when there are spaces around the commas, so `parse_line(" tea , 3 , 2.50")` gives `{"name": "tea", "qty": 3, "price": 2.5}`.
--- starter
ORDERS = ["tea, 3, 2.50", "biscuits, 2, 1.25", "jam, 1, 7.50"]


def parse_line(line):
    name, qty, price = line.split(",")
    return {"name": name, "qty": qty, "price": float(price)}


def line_total(item):
    return item["qty"] * item["price"]


def order_total(lines):
    return sum(line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines)


print(order_total(ORDERS))
--- solution
ORDERS = ["tea, 3, 2.50", "biscuits, 2, 1.25", "jam, 1, 7.50"]


def parse_line(line):
    name, qty, price = line.split(",")
    return {"name": name.strip(), "qty": int(qty), "price": float(price)}


def line_total(item):
    return item["qty"] * item["price"]


def order_total(lines):
    return sum(line_total(parse_line(line)) for line in lines)


print(order_total(ORDERS))
--- hint
The traceback ends in `line_total`, but that line is correct code. What type is `item["qty"]`? Try `print(repr(parse_line("tea, 3, 2.50")))`.
--- hint
The quantity is still a string. Which function created that dict? Look at how it treats `price`, and do the same kind of thing for `qty`.
--- hint
Change the `return` line of `parse_line` so the dict holds `name.strip()` and `int(qty)` instead of `name` and `qty`. `int` copes with the spaces around the digits by itself.
--- check output | The program prints the total
17.5
--- check case | parse_line converts and trims every field
parse_line(" tea , 3 , 2.50")
=> {"name": "tea", "qty": 3, "price": 2.5}
--- check test | The quantity is a real int
type(parse_line("jam,1,7.5")["qty"]) is int
--- check case | order_total of no lines is 0
order_total([])
=> 0
--- check source absent | The fix is not a patch inside line_total
int\(\s*item\[

+++ practice | The wrong thing passed in
--- task
`word_counts(lines)` should return a list with the number of words on each line. Running it on `["go for launch", "hold"]` should give `[3, 1]`, but it crashes:

```
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "main.py", line 9, in <module>
    print(word_counts(["go for launch", "hold"]))
  File "main.py", line 6, in word_counts
    return [len(words_in(lines)) for line in lines]
  File "main.py", line 6, in <listcomp>
    return [len(words_in(lines)) for line in lines]
  File "main.py", line 2, in words_in
    return line.split()
AttributeError: 'list' object has no attribute 'split'
```

The crash is inside `words_in`, but `words_in` is correct: it splits one line of text. Read the frames above it (`<listcomp>` is the list comprehension, running as a frame of its own, like `<genexpr>` in the lesson), find which call handed it a list, and fix that call. Leave `words_in` as it is.
--- starter
def words_in(line):
    return line.split()


def word_counts(lines):
    return [len(words_in(lines)) for line in lines]


print(word_counts(["go for launch", "hold"]))
--- solution
def words_in(line):
    return line.split()


def word_counts(lines):
    return [len(words_in(line)) for line in lines]


print(word_counts(["go for launch", "hold"]))
--- hint
The last line says a **list** has no `split`. `words_in` expects one string. Which variable in `word_counts` holds the whole list, and which holds one line?
--- hint
The comprehension loops with `for line in lines`, but it passes `lines`, the whole list, to `words_in`. Pass the loop variable instead.
--- check output | Prints the word counts
[3, 1]
--- check case | Counts the words on each line
word_counts(["a b", "c", "d e f g"])
=> [2, 1, 4]
--- check case | No lines
word_counts([])
=> []
--- check case | words_in still splits one line
words_in("x y")
=> ["x", "y"]

+++ practice | What exactly did you get?
--- task
When a test fails, a plain `print` can hide the difference: `3` and `"3"` look the same on screen. Write `mismatch(expected, got)`. It returns `"same"` if the two are equal. Otherwise it returns `"expected <e>, got <g>"`, where `<e>` and `<g>` are the `repr` of each value, so strings show their quotes.

For example, `mismatch(3, "3")` gives `"expected 3, got '3'"`, and `mismatch("ok", "ok ")` gives `"expected 'ok', got 'ok '"`.
--- starter
def mismatch(expected, got):
    if expected == got:
        return "same"
    return f"expected {expected}, got {got}"
--- solution
def mismatch(expected, got):
    if expected == got:
        return "same"
    return f"expected {repr(expected)}, got {repr(got)}"
--- hint
Inside an f-string, `{value}` shows the friendly form, the one `print` uses. You want the exact form instead.
--- hint
Call `repr(...)` on each value inside the curly braces.
--- check case | A number and a string that look alike
mismatch(3, "3")
=> "expected 3, got '3'"
--- check case | A hidden space at the end
mismatch("ok", "ok ")
=> "expected 'ok', got 'ok '"
--- check case | Equal values
mismatch([1, 2], [1, 2])
=> "same"
--- check case | None against an empty string
mismatch(None, "")
=> "expected None, got ''"

+++ practice | The key with a space in it
--- task
A settings file arrives as a list of lines like `"timeout = 30"`. `parse_config(lines)` turns them into a dict, and `timeout_of(lines)` reads the timeout as a number. Running `print(timeout_of(["timeout = 30", "retries=3"]))` should print `30`, but it crashes:

```
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "main.py", line 10, in <module>
    print(timeout_of(["timeout = 30", "retries=3"]))
  File "main.py", line 7, in timeout_of
    return int(parse_config(lines)["timeout"])
KeyError: 'timeout'
```

The key **is** in the file, so find out what `parse_config` really stores (try printing `repr` of its result), and fix it at the source: `parse_config` must return keys and values with the spaces at their ends removed. Leave `timeout_of` as it is.
--- starter
def parse_config(lines):
    pairs = [line.partition("=") for line in lines]
    return {key: value for key, sep, value in pairs}


def timeout_of(lines):
    return int(parse_config(lines)["timeout"])


print(timeout_of(["timeout = 30", "retries=3"]))
--- solution
def parse_config(lines):
    pairs = [line.partition("=") for line in lines]
    return {key.strip(): value.strip() for key, sep, value in pairs}


def timeout_of(lines):
    return int(parse_config(lines)["timeout"])


print(timeout_of(["timeout = 30", "retries=3"]))
--- hint
Print `repr(parse_config(["timeout = 30"]))`. Look closely at the key inside the quotes.
--- hint
`partition("=")` cuts at the `=` and keeps the spaces around it, so the key is `"timeout "` with a space at the end. Strip the key and the value where the dict is built.
--- check output | Prints the timeout
30
--- check case | Keys and values are trimmed
parse_config(["timeout = 30", " retries=3 "])
=> {"timeout": "30", "retries": "3"}
--- check case | No lines, no settings
parse_config([])
=> {}
--- check source absent | The fix is in parse_config, not in timeout_of
\["timeout "\]

+++ practice | Lines too short to have a level
--- task
Each log line should look like `"12:00:01 SYS ERROR disk full"`: a time, a system, a level, then a message. The level is the **third** word. Write two functions:

1. `level_of(line)` returns the third word of `line`, or `None` if the line has fewer than three words.
2. `count_levels(lines)` returns a dict from each level to how many lines have it, skipping lines where `level_of` gives `None`.

The starter crashes with `IndexError: list index out of range` on a blank line or a short line like `"12:00:02 SYS"`. Handle those edge cases.
--- starter
def level_of(line):
    return line.split()[2]


def count_levels(lines):
    counts = {}
    for line in lines:
        level = level_of(line)
        counts[level] = counts.get(level, 0) + 1
    return counts
--- solution
def level_of(line):
    words = line.split()
    if len(words) < 3:
        return None
    return words[2]


def count_levels(lines):
    counts = {}
    for line in lines:
        level = level_of(line)
        if level is None:
            continue
        counts[level] = counts.get(level, 0) + 1
    return counts
--- hint
`IndexError: list index out of range` means the list has no item at that position. How many words does a blank line have?
--- hint
In `level_of`, split once, and if `len(words) < 3`, return `None` before you ever touch `words[2]`.
--- hint
In `count_levels`, check `if level is None:` and `continue` before counting.
--- check case | The third word is the level
level_of("12:00:01 SYS ERROR disk full")
=> "ERROR"
--- check test | Short and blank lines give None
level_of("") is None and level_of("   ") is None and level_of("12:00:02 SYS") is None
--- check case | Exactly three words is enough
level_of("t s INFO")
=> "INFO"
--- check case | count_levels skips the lines with no level
count_levels(["1 A INFO up", "", "2 B", "3 A INFO ok", "4 C WARN hot"])
=> {"INFO": 2, "WARN": 1}
--- check case | Nothing but blank lines
count_levels(["", " "])
=> {}

+++ practice | Debug: the unit nobody handled
--- task
`total_kg(items)` adds up masses given as `(value, unit)` tuples, where the unit is `"kg"`, `"g"` or `"t"` (tonnes: 1 t is 1000 kg). `total_kg([(2, "kg"), (500, "g")])` gives `2.5`, which is right. But with a tonne in the list it crashes:

```
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "main.py", line 12, in <module>
    print(total_kg([(2, "kg"), (1, "t")]))
  File "main.py", line 9, in total_kg
    return sum(to_kg(value, unit) for value, unit in items)
TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for +: 'int' and 'NoneType'
```

`sum` is not the problem: it was handed a `None`. Find which function made that `None`, and fix it at the source. `to_kg` must handle `"t"`, and for any other unit it must raise `ValueError(f"unknown unit: {unit}")` instead of quietly giving `None`.
--- starter
def to_kg(value, unit):
    if unit == "kg":
        return value
    if unit == "g":
        return value / 1000


def total_kg(items):
    return sum(to_kg(value, unit) for value, unit in items)


print(total_kg([(2, "kg"), (1, "t")]))
--- solution
def to_kg(value, unit):
    if unit == "kg":
        return value
    if unit == "g":
        return value / 1000
    if unit == "t":
        return value * 1000
    raise ValueError(f"unknown unit: {unit}")


def total_kg(items):
    return sum(to_kg(value, unit) for value, unit in items)


print(total_kg([(2, "kg"), (1, "t")]))
--- hint
What does a function hand back when it reaches its end without a `return`? Try `print(repr(to_kg(1, "t")))`.
--- hint
Add a branch for `"t"`, and make the last line of `to_kg` a `raise`, so a unit nobody handled stops loudly at the place it was made.
--- check output | Prints the total with a tonne in it
1002
--- check case | Grams and kilograms
total_kg([(2, "kg"), (500, "g")])
=> 2.5
--- check case | Tonnes
to_kg(3, "t")
=> 3000
--- check test | An unknown unit raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: to_kg(5, "lb"))
--- check case | Nothing to add
total_kg([])
=> 0

+++ practice | Sensor averages, fixed at the source
--- task
Each line of a test-stand log looks like `"temp = 21.5"`: a sensor name, `=`, a reading. `averages(lines)` should return a dict from each sensor to the average of its readings, in the order the sensors first appear. It crashes:

```
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "main.py", line 18, in <module>
    print(averages(["temp = 21.5", "volts = 28", "temp = 22.5"]))
  File "main.py", line 15, in averages
    return {s: sum(vs) / len(vs) for s, vs in by_sensor(lines).items()}
  File "main.py", line 15, in <dictcomp>
    return {s: sum(vs) / len(vs) for s, vs in by_sensor(lines).items()}
TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for +: 'int' and 'str'
```

(`<dictcomp>` is the dict comprehension's own frame.) Fix it at the source, in `parse_reading`, which must return `(name, reading)` with the name stripped and the reading as a `float`. While you are there, make `parse_reading` raise `ValueError(f"bad line: {repr(line)}")` for a line with no `=` in it, so a bad line is reported where it is read. Leave `by_sensor` and `averages` as they are.
--- starter
def parse_reading(line):
    name, value = line.split("=")
    return name.strip(), value.strip()


def by_sensor(lines):
    table = {}
    for line in lines:
        name, value = parse_reading(line)
        table.setdefault(name, []).append(value)
    return table


def averages(lines):
    return {s: sum(vs) / len(vs) for s, vs in by_sensor(lines).items()}


print(averages(["temp = 21.5", "volts = 28", "temp = 22.5"]))
--- solution
def parse_reading(line):
    name, sep, value = line.partition("=")
    if not sep:
        raise ValueError(f"bad line: {repr(line)}")
    return name.strip(), float(value)


def by_sensor(lines):
    table = {}
    for line in lines:
        name, value = parse_reading(line)
        table.setdefault(name, []).append(value)
    return table


def averages(lines):
    return {s: sum(vs) / len(vs) for s, vs in by_sensor(lines).items()}


print(averages(["temp = 21.5", "volts = 28", "temp = 22.5"]))
--- hint
The traceback ends in `averages`, but adding up numbers is correct code. What type are the readings in each list? Walk back to the function that made them.
--- hint
Convert the reading with `float(...)` in `parse_reading`; `float` copes with the spaces around the number by itself.
--- hint
`partition("=")` gives an empty middle piece when there is no `=`, and an empty string counts as false. That is a neat way to spot a bad line before you raise.
--- check output | Prints the averages
{'temp': 22.0, 'volts': 28.0}
--- check case | parse_reading gives a name and a float
parse_reading(" pressure = 101.3 ")
=> ("pressure", 101.3)
--- check test | A line with no = raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_reading("temp 21"))
--- check test | The error message shows the bad line with its quotes
(lambda d: (exec("try:\n    parse_reading('temp 21')\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", globals(), d), d.get("msg"))[1])({}) == "bad line: 'temp 21'"
--- check case | One sensor, several readings
averages(["a=1", "a=2", "a=6"])
=> {"a": 3.0}
--- check case | No lines
averages([])
=> {}

=== py2-10 | Classes that behave: __repr__, __eq__ and @property
--- teach
Last lesson you used `repr` to see exactly what a value was while hunting a bug. In the basics you wrote your own classes, with `__init__` and methods. This lesson joins the two: it shows how to make your own objects print their data, compare sensibly with `==`, and check their own data, so they behave as nicely as Python's built-in values.

Think of a new gadget straight out of the box. It works, but it has no labels, and two identical ones look like strangers to each other. A few small additions make it pleasant to use. For a class, those additions are a few special methods.

### The problem

Here is a class with only `__init__`:

```python
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

p = Point(1, 2)
print(p)                     # <__main__.Point object at 0x7f3a2c1b9d50>
print(Point(1, 2) == Point(1, 2))   # False
```

Two problems. Printing it shows the class name and a strange number (a [[memory address|memory-address]]), not the data inside. And two points with the same `x` and `y` are not equal. Both can be fixed by writing **special methods**: methods with two underscores on each side of the name (the "dunder" names you met with `__init__`), which Python calls by itself at the right moment.

### `__repr__`: how the object shows itself

**`__repr__`** returns the text Python shows for your object. Python calls it when you use `repr(p)`, when the object sits inside a list, in error messages, and when you `print` it (if the class has no other way to show itself). A good `__repr__` returns a string that looks like the code that would build the object:

```python
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Point({self.x}, {self.y})"

print(Point(1, 2))           # Point(1, 2)
print([Point(1, 2), Point(3, 4)])   # [Point(1, 2), Point(3, 4)]
```

`__repr__` takes only `self`, and it must **return** a string, not print it. You never call `p.__repr__()` yourself; you write `repr(p)` or `print(p)`, and [[Python finds your method|print-uses-repr]].

### `__eq__`: what `==` means

Without help, `==` on your objects asks the same question as `is`, which you met in the bug-hunting lesson: "is this the very same object?". Two separate `Point(1, 2)` objects are two objects, so the answer is `False`.

**`__eq__`** lets you decide what `==` means. When you write `a == b`, Python calls `a.__eq__(b)`: `self` is the left side and the parameter, usually called `other`, is the right side. It should return `True` or `False`.

Before we write it, one helper. **`isinstance(value, SomeClass)`** is `True` when the value is an object of that class, or of [[a subclass of it|isinstance-word]]:

```python
print(isinstance(Point(1, 2), Point))   # True
print(isinstance("hello", Point))       # False
print(isinstance(5, int))               # True
```

Now `__eq__`:

```python fragment
    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.x, self.y) == (other.x, other.y)
```

Read it line by line. If `other` is not a `Point` at all, return the special value `NotImplemented`, which means "I do not know how to compare with that". Otherwise, pack both points' data into tuples and compare the tuples: equal if `x` matches `x` and `y` matches `y`.

```python
print(Point(1, 2) == Point(1, 2))   # True
print(Point(1, 2) == Point(9, 9))   # False
print(Point(1, 2) != Point(9, 9))   # True
print(Point(1, 2) == "hello")       # False
```

Notice two things. `!=`, "is not equal to", works for free: Python flips the answer of your `__eq__`. And comparing with a string did not crash. Returning `NotImplemented` let Python [[ask the other side and then give up politely|not-implemented]] with `False`.

### `@property`: a method that looks like data

Sometimes a value can be worked out from the others. A rectangle's area is width times height. You could write a method `area()`, but then callers must remember the brackets. A **property** is a method that callers use as if it were a plain attribute, with no brackets:

```python
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        self.width = width
        self.height = height

    @property
    def area(self):
        return self.width * self.height

r = Rectangle(3, 4)
print(r.area)     # 12   (no brackets: it reads like data)
r.width = 10
print(r.area)     # 40   (worked out again, so always up to date)
```

The line `@property`, read "at property", sits right above the `def`. A line starting with the at sign `@` is a **[[decorator|decorator]]**: it wraps the function below it and changes how it behaves. `@property` turns the method `area` into something you read with `r.area`. Each time you read it, Python runs the method and hands you the answer.

### A setter: checking a value before it is stored

A property can also run code when someone **sets** it, with `=`. That code is called a **setter**, and its usual job is to check the new value first. The real value is kept in an attribute whose name starts with an underscore, `_balance`, which [[means "private, for this class only"|underscore]]:

```python
class Account:
    def __init__(self, balance):
        self.balance = balance          # goes through the setter below

    @property
    def balance(self):
        return self._balance

    @balance.setter
    def balance(self, value):
        if value < 0:
            raise ValueError("balance cannot be negative")
        self._balance = value
```

Take it slowly. There are two methods, both named `balance`.

- The first, under `@property`, is the **getter**. It runs when you *read* `acct.balance`, and returns the stored `self._balance`.
- The second, under `@balance.setter`, is the **setter**. It runs when you *assign* `acct.balance = something`. It gets the new value as `value`, raises a `ValueError` if it is below zero, and otherwise stores it in `self._balance`.

Using it looks like ordinary attributes:

```python
acct = Account(50)
print(acct.balance)     # 50
acct.balance = 80
print(acct.balance)     # 80
acct.balance = -10      # ValueError: balance cannot be negative
```

Look at `__init__` again: `self.balance = balance` is an assignment to `balance`, so it goes through the setter too. That means even `Account(-5)` is refused. The check happens in one place, and nothing can get past it.

**Watch out:** inside the getter, never write `return self.balance`. Reading `self.balance` runs the getter, which reads `self.balance`, which runs the getter again, forever, until Python gives up with a [[RecursionError|recursion-error]]. The same happens in the setter with `self.balance = value`. The getter and setter always use the underscore name, `self._balance`.

::: context memory-address The number is where the object lives
Every object sits somewhere in the computer's memory, and `0x7f3a2c1b9d50` is roughly that location. The `0x` at the front means the number is written in hexadecimal, a way of writing numbers with sixteen digits, `0` to `9` and then `a` to `f`. The number is different every time you run the program. In the usual version of Python it is the same number that `id(p)` gives, the identity you printed in the bug-hunting lesson, written in hexadecimal. It tells you *which* object you have, but nothing about what is inside it, which is why a `__repr__` is so much more useful.
:::

::: context print-uses-repr Two text forms, one fallback
Python has two special methods for turning an object into text. `__str__` is the friendly form, for people using the program, and `print` and `str()` use it. `__repr__` is the exact form, for programmers. If a class has no `__str__`, Python uses `__repr__` for both. That is why writing only `__repr__` is the usual choice: one method, and your object shows up well everywhere, from `print` to an error message. Add a `__str__` only when you want a different, friendlier text for users.
:::

::: context isinstance-word Is it one of these?
`isinstance` asks "is this value a kind of that class?", and it follows the family tree from the exceptions lesson. `isinstance(True, int)` is even `True`, because `bool` is a subclass of `int`. That is why `__eq__` uses `isinstance(other, Point)` rather than `type(other) is Point`: a subclass of `Point` is still a point, and should still compare. You can also pass a tuple of classes, as in `isinstance(x, (int, float))`, to accept any one of them.
:::

::: context not-implemented Asking the other side
When `a.__eq__(b)` returns `NotImplemented`, Python does not give up at once. It turns the question around and tries `b.__eq__(a)`, in case the right-hand side knows how to compare. If that also says `NotImplemented`, Python falls back to "are they the same object?", which for a point and a string is `False`. Note that `NotImplemented` is returned, not raised. It is a special value, not an exception.

