=> ""

+++ practice | Summarize a sensor CSV
--- task
A test stand writes CSV with the columns `sensor` and `value`. Some rows have an empty value, where a reading was lost. Write `summarize(csv_text)`. It returns new CSV text, with `\n` line endings, that has:

- a header row: `sensor,count,mean`;
- then one row per sensor, in the order the sensors first appear: the sensor name, how many readings it has, and the mean of those readings written with exactly 2 decimal places (use the format spec `.2f`).

Rows with an empty value are skipped. A sensor whose readings were all lost does not appear. Use `csv.DictReader` to read, a `defaultdict(list)` to group, and `csv.writer` to write.

For example, `summarize("sensor,value\ntemp,20\nvolts,28.5\ntemp,\ntemp,23\n")` gives `"sensor,count,mean\ntemp,2,21.50\nvolts,1,28.50\n"`.
--- starter
import csv
import io
from collections import defaultdict


def summarize(csv_text):
    return csv_text
--- solution
import csv
import io
from collections import defaultdict


def summarize(csv_text):
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for row in csv.DictReader(io.StringIO(csv_text)):
        if row["value"]:
            groups[row["sensor"]].append(float(row["value"]))
    out = io.StringIO()
    writer = csv.writer(out, lineterminator="\n")
    writer.writerow(["sensor", "count", "mean"])
    for sensor, values in groups.items():
        writer.writerow([sensor, len(values), f"{sum(values) / len(values):.2f}"])
    return out.getvalue()
--- hint
Work in two halves. First read: group the readings by sensor, as floats, skipping empty values (an empty string counts as false). Then write: a header row, and one row per group.
--- hint
A `defaultdict(list)` keeps its keys in the order they were first added, so looping over `groups.items()` gives the sensors in first-seen order. It never gets a key for a sensor whose values were all empty, because you only touch `groups[...]` when there is a value.
--- hint
For each sensor, write `[sensor, len(values), f"{mean:.2f}"]` with `writerow`. The writer turns the count into text for you.
--- check case | Groups, counts and means
summarize("sensor,value\ntemp,20\nvolts,28.5\ntemp,\ntemp,23\n")
=> "sensor,count,mean\ntemp,2,21.50\nvolts,1,28.50\n"
--- check case | A sensor with only lost readings is left out
summarize("sensor,value\na,\nb,1\na,\n")
=> "sensor,count,mean\nb,1,1.00\n"
--- check case | No rows gives just the header
summarize("sensor,value\n")
=> "sensor,count,mean\n"
--- check case | Means are rounded to two places
summarize("sensor,value\nx,1\nx,2\nx,2\n")
=> "sensor,count,mean\nx,3,1.67\n"

=== py2-13 | Problem solving: two sum
--- teach
The last two lessons handed you ready-made tools. This one is different: it is about *how to solve a problem you have not seen before*, when there is no tool with the answer in it. You will learn a method that works on almost any puzzle, and use it on a famous one.

Here is the puzzle as a shopping trip. You have a gift card worth exactly 9 dollars, and a shelf of things with prices 2, 7, 11 and 15. Which *two* things can you buy that use up the card exactly? You can probably see it: 2 and 7. The question is how to make a computer see it, fast, when the shelf has a hundred thousand things on it.

### A method for new problems

1. **Restate the problem** in your own words, including what to give back when there is no answer.
2. **Work small examples by hand**, including awkward ones: an empty list, the same number twice, negative numbers.
3. **Write the [[brute force|brute-force]] first**, in your head or on paper: the plain, try-everything way. It is usually easy to see and usually slow, but it proves you understand the problem.
4. **Find what the brute force repeats**, and remember it instead of working it out again.
5. **Check the awkward examples again** against the faster version.

### Step 1: restate it

Given a list `nums` and a number `target`, find two *different* positions `i` and `j`, with `i` before `j` (`i < j`, "i is less than j"), so that `nums[i] + nums[j] == target`. Give back the tuple `(i, j)`. If there is no such pair, give back `None`.

Remember that a position is an **index**, counted from 0. The answer is about positions, not prices.

### Step 2: small examples by hand

- `[2, 7, 11, 15]` with target 9: `nums[0]` is 2 and `nums[1]` is 7, and 2 + 7 = 9. The answer is `(0, 1)`.
- `[3]` with target 6: 3 + 3 is 6, but there is only one 3. A number cannot pair with itself, so the answer is `None`.
- `[3, 3]` with target 6: two *different* 3s, at positions 0 and 1. The answer is `(0, 1)`.
- `[]` with any target: nothing to pair, so `None`.

### Step 3: brute force, try every pair

```python
def two_sum_slow(nums, target):
    for j in range(len(nums)):
        for i in range(j):
            if nums[i] + nums[j] == target:
                return (i, j)
    return None
```

The outer loop picks `j`, the second position. The inner loop, `for i in range(j)`, runs `i` through every position *before* `j`: `range(j)` counts 0, 1, and up to `j - 1`. So `i < j` is always true, and a number is never paired with itself. The first pair found has the smallest `j`, and for that `j` the smallest `i`.

It works. But count the work. For a list of `n` numbers it checks about `n * n / 2` pairs (`n` times `n`, halved). For 4 numbers that is 6 pairs. For 100,000 numbers it is about five *billion* pairs, which in Python takes minutes. That is [[far too slow|growth-rate]].

### Step 4: what does it repeat?

Look at the inner loop for one `j`. Say `nums[j]` is 7 and the target is 9. The inner loop searches every number before `j`, looking for one exact value: 9 - 7 = 2. That partner value, `target - nums[j]`, is called the **[[complement|complement-word]]**: the piece that completes the target.

So each time round, the brute force searches the same growing list for one value. Searching a list means looking at item after item. But you know a faster way to ask "have I seen this?": a dict. Asking `key in some_dict` does not look through the items one by one. It jumps straight to the answer, [[almost instantly|hash-table]], however big the dict is.

So walk the list **once**, and keep a dict called `seen` that remembers each *value* you have passed and *the position* where you first saw it. At each new number, work out its complement and ask `seen` whether that complement has already gone past.

```python
seen = {}
# at position j, with value x:
#   need = target - x
#   if need is in seen: the answer is (seen[need], j)
#   otherwise, remember x: seen[x] = j
```

Here is the gift-card shelf, `[2, 7, 11, 15]` with target 9, one step at a time:

| j | x | need = 9 - x | is need in seen? | seen afterwards |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 2 | 7 | no | `{2: 0}` |
| 1 | 7 | 2 | yes, at position 0 | answer `(0, 1)` |

That is one pass, about `n` steps instead of `n * n / 2`. For 100,000 numbers that is 100,000 steps, not five billion. Keeping extra information in a dict so you never have to search again is called [[trading memory for time|memory-for-time]], and it is one of the most useful ideas in programming.

### Step 5: check the awkward examples again

**Watch out:** two details decide whether the fast version is right.

- **Check before you store.** At each number, look up the complement *first*, and only then store the number. With `[3]` and target 6, the 3 needs a 3. If you stored it first, it would find itself and give `(0, 0)`. Checking first finds nothing, so the answer is correctly `None`. With `[3, 3]`, the second 3 finds the first one: `(0, 1)`.
- **Keep the first position of a repeated value.** Take `[1, 4, 1, 3]` with target 4. The value 1 appears at positions 0 and 2. When you reach the 3 at position 3, it needs a 1. The rule says: smallest `j` first, then the smallest `i`, so the answer is `(0, 3)`. If you had overwritten `seen[1]` with 2, you would give `(2, 3)`. So store a value only the first time you see it: `if x not in seen: seen[x] = j`.

The awkward examples from step 2 are exactly the ones that catch these two details. That is why the method writes them down first.

::: context brute-force Try everything
Brute force means solving a problem by trying every possibility, with no cleverness. It sounds like an insult, but it is a respected first step: it is easy to get right, and once you have it, you can test the fast version against it on thousands of random lists. If the two ever disagree, one of them has a bug. Engineers use a slow, trusted version as a yardstick like this all the time.
:::

::: context growth-rate How the work grows
The exact number of pairs among `n` things is `n * (n - 1) / 2`. For 100,000 numbers that is `100000 * 99999 / 2`, which is 4,999,950,000: about five billion. Programmers describe this growth with "big O": the brute force is O(n²), said "O of n squared", because doubling the list makes four times the work. The dict version is O(n): double the list, double the work. You will meet big O properly in the advanced course.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="40" y1="140" x2="340" y2="140" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="40" y1="140" x2="40" y2="15" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="190" y="162" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">length of the list</text>
  <text x="30" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93" transform="rotate(-90 30 80)">work</text>
  <path d="M40,140 Q200,138 250,15" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="255" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">every pair</text>
  <line x1="40" y1="140" x2="340" y2="105" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">one pass</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context complement-word The piece that completes it
In everyday English, a complement is the thing that completes something else. If you need 9 and you are holding 7, the complement is 2. Rewriting "find two numbers that add to 9" as "for each number, is its complement around?" turns a question about pairs into a question about single values, and a question about single values is exactly what a dict answers fast.
:::

::: context hash-table How a dict finds a key so fast
A dict works out, from the key itself, a number called a hash, and uses that number to pick a slot to store the value in. To look a key up later, it works out the same hash and goes straight to that slot, instead of checking every key. That is why `in` on a dict takes about the same time for 10 keys or 10 million. The same `in` on a list has to look at items one by one until it finds a match.
:::

::: context memory-for-time Remembering instead of redoing
The dict costs extra memory: in the worst case, one entry per number. In return, the program never searches the same ground twice. The same trade is everywhere in computing. Your computer's processor keeps a cache, a small, very fast memory holding data it used recently, so it does not have to fetch it again from the slower main memory. Whenever something is slow and repeated, ask whether you could remember the answer instead.
:::
--- task
Write `two_sum(nums, target)`. It returns a tuple `(i, j)` where `i < j` and `nums[i] + nums[j] == target`, or `None` if there is no such pair.

If there are several answers, return the one with the **smallest `j`**, and for that `j` the **smallest `i`**. It must handle a list of 100,000 numbers quickly, so use the one-pass dict method from the lesson, not the try-every-pair loops:

- walk the list once with `enumerate`, getting each position `j` and value `x`;
- work out `need = target - x` and, if `need` is in the dict `seen`, return `(seen[need], j)`;
- otherwise store `x` in `seen`, but only if it is not there already;
- after the loop, return `None`.
--- starter
def two_sum(nums, target):
    return None
--- solution
def two_sum(nums, target):
    seen = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        need = target - x
        if need in seen:
            return (seen[need], j)
        if x not in seen:
            seen[x] = j
    return None
--- hint
Start with an empty dict, `seen = {}`, and loop with `for j, x in enumerate(nums):`. For each number `x`, the partner you need is `target - x`.
--- hint
Look the partner up *before* you store `x`, so a number never pairs with itself: `if need in seen: return (seen[need], j)`.
--- hint
Then store the position only the first time you see a value: `if x not in seen: seen[x] = j`. The last line of the function, outside the loop, is `return None`.
--- check case | The classic example
two_sum([2, 7, 11, 15], 9)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | A number cannot pair with itself, but a duplicate can
two_sum([3, 2, 4, 3], 6)
=> (1, 2)
--- check case | Two equal numbers
two_sum([3, 3], 6)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | Negative numbers
two_sum([-3, 4, 3, 90], 0)
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | Smallest j first, then smallest i
two_sum([1, 4, 1, 3], 4)
=> (0, 3)
?? When a value appears twice, keep the index of the first time you saw it.
--- check case | No pair gives None
two_sum([1, 2, 3], 7)
=> None
--- check case | Empty list gives None
two_sum([], 5)
=> None
--- check case | 100,000 numbers, answer at the very end
two_sum(list(range(1, 100001)), 199999)
=> (99998, 99999)
?? Checking every pair is billions of steps here. Use a dict so each number is handled once.

+++ practice | Is there a pair at all?
--- task
Write `has_pair(nums, target)`. It returns `True` if two numbers at **different** positions in `nums` add up to `target`, and `False` otherwise. It must be fast on a list of 100,000 numbers, so walk the list once and remember what you have seen in a dict. Check for the complement before you remember the current number, so a number never pairs with itself.

For example, `has_pair([4, 9, 1], 10)` gives `True` (9 + 1), `has_pair([5], 10)` gives `False`, and `has_pair([5, 5], 10)` gives `True`.
--- starter
def has_pair(nums, target):
    return False
--- solution
def has_pair(nums, target):
    seen = {}
    for x in nums:
        if target - x in seen:
            return True
        seen[x] = True
    return False
--- hint
Asking `target - x in nums` for every number would be slow, and it would find the number itself: with `[5]` and target 10, the 5 finds the 5. Ask a dict of the numbers already passed instead.
--- hint
Keep a dict of the numbers already passed. At each number, ask whether `target - x` is in that dict first, and only then store `x` (any value will do, such as `True`).
--- check case | A pair exists
has_pair([4, 9, 1], 10)
=> True
--- check case | A number cannot pair with itself
has_pair([5], 10)
=> False
--- check case | Two equal numbers at different positions
has_pair([5, 5], 10)
=> True
--- check case | No pair
has_pair([1, 2, 3], 100)
=> False
--- check case | Empty list
has_pair([], 0)
=> False
--- check case | 100,000 numbers with no pair
has_pair(list(range(0, 200000, 2)), 7)
=> False
?? Searching the list for every number is billions of steps here. A dict answers "have I seen it?" at once.

+++ practice | Find a pair by difference
--- task
Write `pair_with_difference(nums, diff)`. It returns a tuple `(i, j)` with `i < j` and `nums[j] - nums[i] == diff`, or `None` if there is none. If there are several answers, return the one with the smallest `j`, and for that `j` the smallest `i`. Use the one-pass dict method: this time the partner you need for a value `x` is `x - diff`.

For example, `pair_with_difference([5, 1, 9, 3], 4)` gives `(0, 2)`, because 9 - 5 is 4.
--- starter
def pair_with_difference(nums, diff):
    seen = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        need = diff - x
        if need in seen:
            return (seen[need], j)
        if x not in seen:
            seen[x] = j
    return None
--- solution
def pair_with_difference(nums, diff):
    seen = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        need = x - diff
        if need in seen:
            return (seen[need], j)
        if x not in seen:
            seen[x] = j
    return None
--- hint
Restate it: you are at position `j` with value `x`, and you want an earlier value `v` with `x - v == diff`. Solve that for `v`.
--- hint
`v` is `x - diff`. Everything else is the two sum method: check first, then store the value only the first time you see it.
--- check case | A later value minus an earlier one
pair_with_difference([5, 1, 9, 3], 4)
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | A negative difference
pair_with_difference([5, 1, 9, 3], -4)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | A difference of zero needs a repeat
pair_with_difference([2, 7, 2], 0)
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | Smallest j first, then smallest i
pair_with_difference([1, 1, 3], 2)
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | No pair
pair_with_difference([1, 2, 3], 10)
=> None
--- check case | 100,000 numbers, answer at the end
pair_with_difference(list(range(100000)), 99999)
=> (0, 99999)

+++ practice | The first name signed twice
--- task
A sign-up sheet is a list of names, typed by hand, so capitals and spaces at the ends vary: `" Ada"` and `"ada"` are the same person. Write `first_repeat(names)`. It returns a tuple `(i, j)` for the **first** repeat: `j` is the smallest position whose name already appeared earlier, and `i` is the position where that name **first** appeared. If nobody signed twice, return `None`. Walk the list once with a dict.

For example, `first_repeat(["Lin", "ada", "Sam", " ADA ", "lin"])` gives `(1, 3)`.
--- starter
def first_repeat(names):
    seen = {}
    for j, name in enumerate(names):
        if name in seen:
            return (seen[name], j)
        seen[name] = j
    return None
--- solution
def first_repeat(names):
    seen = {}
    for j, name in enumerate(names):
        key = name.strip().lower()
        if key in seen:
            return (seen[key], j)
        seen[key] = j
    return None
--- hint
The starter compares names exactly, so `"ada"` and `" ADA "` look different. Compare a cleaned-up form instead.
--- hint
Make a key with `name.strip().lower()`, and use that key both to look up and to store. Because you return at the very first repeat, a key is never stored twice.
--- check case | Capitals and spaces do not hide a repeat
first_repeat(["Lin", "ada", "Sam", " ADA ", "lin"])
=> (1, 3)
--- check case | The earliest repeat wins
first_repeat(["a", "b", "b", "a"])
=> (1, 2)
--- check case | Nobody signed twice
first_repeat(["x", "y", "z"])
=> None
--- check case | An empty sheet
first_repeat([])
=> None

+++ practice | Match parts from two bins
--- task
Two bins hold parts of different lengths, `left` and `right`. Write `match_parts(left, right, target)`. It returns `(i, j)` where `left[i] + right[j] == target`, or `None` if no such pair exists. If there are several answers, return the one with the smallest `i`, and for that `i` the smallest `j`.

It must be fast when each bin has 100,000 parts, so do not try every pair. First build a dict from each value in `right` to the position where it **first** appears. Then walk `left` once.

For example, `match_parts([4, 6, 1], [3, 5, 5, 2], 9)` gives `(0, 1)`, because 4 + 5 is 9, and the first 5 in `right` is at position 1.
--- starter
def match_parts(left, right, target):
    return None
--- solution
def match_parts(left, right, target):
    first = {}
    for j, y in enumerate(right):
        if y not in first:
            first[y] = j
    for i, x in enumerate(left):
        need = target - x
        if need in first:
            return (i, first[need])
    return None
--- hint
Trying every pair of parts would be correct, but it is about ten billion steps for two bins of 100,000. Remember `right` in a dict instead of searching it again for every part.
--- hint
Store each value of `right` only the first time you see it, so the dict keeps the smallest `j`. Then, for each `x` in `left` in order, look up `target - x`.
--- check case | The first i, then the first j
match_parts([4, 6, 1], [3, 5, 5, 2], 9)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | A later left part
match_parts([10, 2], [7, 1], 3)
=> (1, 1)
--- check case | Negative lengths still add up
match_parts([-2, 8], [5, 0], 3)
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | No match
match_parts([1, 2], [3, 4], 100)
=> None
--- check case | An empty bin
match_parts([], [1, 2], 3)
=> None
--- check case | 100,000 parts in each bin
match_parts(list(range(100000)), list(range(100000, 200000)), 299998)
=> (99999, 99999)
?? Two loops over two bins of 100,000 is ten billion checks. Build the dict of right once, then walk left once.

+++ practice | Debug: the number that paired with itself
--- task
`find_pair(nums, target)` should return `(i, j)` with `i < j` and `nums[i] + nums[j] == target`, the one with the smallest `j` and then the smallest `i`, or `None`. It gives `(0, 1)` for `[2, 7, 11, 15]` and target 9, which is right.

**The bug:** `find_pair([3], 6)` gives `(0, 0)`, pairing the 3 with itself, and `find_pair([1, 4, 1, 3], 4)` gives `(2, 3)` instead of `(0, 3)`. Fix it.
--- starter
def find_pair(nums, target):
    seen = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        seen[x] = j
        need = target - x
        if need in seen:
            return (seen[need], j)
    return None
--- solution
def find_pair(nums, target):
    seen = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        need = target - x
        if need in seen:
            return (seen[need], j)
        if x not in seen:
            seen[x] = j
    return None
--- hint
Both bugs come from one line: where `seen[x] = j` sits, and when it runs. Look at the lesson's two "Watch out" details.
--- hint
Store after you check, so a number cannot find itself, and store only if `x not in seen`, so a repeated value keeps its first position.
--- check case | A number cannot pair with itself
find_pair([3], 6)
=> None
--- check case | A repeated value keeps its first position
find_pair([1, 4, 1, 3], 4)
=> (0, 3)
--- check case | Two equal numbers
find_pair([3, 3], 6)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | The classic example still works
find_pair([2, 7, 11, 15], 9)
=> (0, 1)

+++ practice | How many pairs?
--- task
Write `count_pairs(nums, target)`. It returns how many pairs of positions `i < j` there are with `nums[i] + nums[j] == target`. Equal numbers at different positions are different pairs: in `[2, 2, 2]` with target 4 there are 3 pairs.

It must handle 100,000 numbers quickly. Walk the list once, and keep a dict from each value to **how many times** you have seen it so far. At each number, every earlier copy of its complement makes one new pair.

For example, `count_pairs([1, 5, 3, 3, 1], 6)` gives `3`: 1 + 5, 5 + 1 and 3 + 3.
--- starter
def count_pairs(nums, target):
    count = 0
    return count
--- solution
def count_pairs(nums, target):
    seen = {}
    count = 0
    for x in nums:
        count += seen.get(target - x, 0)
        seen[x] = seen.get(x, 0) + 1
    return count
--- hint
When you reach a number `x`, how many pairs does it finish? One for every earlier number equal to `target - x`. So you need a count of the earlier numbers, not their positions.
--- hint
Add `seen.get(target - x, 0)` to the count first, then count `x` itself with `seen[x] = seen.get(x, 0) + 1`. Checking first means a number never pairs with itself.
--- check case | Pairs in a small list
count_pairs([1, 5, 3, 3, 1], 6)
=> 3
--- check case | Every copy pairs with every other copy
count_pairs([2, 2, 2, 2], 4)
=> 6
--- check case | A single number makes no pair
count_pairs([3], 6)
=> 0
--- check case | Empty list
count_pairs([], 6)
=> 0
--- check case | 100,000 zeros
count_pairs([0] * 100000, 0)
=> 4999950000
?? Checking every pair is five billion steps here. Count the earlier copies of each value in a dict instead.

=== py2-14 | Design: untangling a long function
--- teach
Last lesson you made a slow program fast without changing a single answer it gave. This lesson, the last of the course, makes a messy program *clear* without changing a single answer it gives.

Code that works is not finished. Code gets *read* far more often than it gets written: by your team, by the person fixing it next year, and by you in a month. A 40-line function that reads the data, does the sums, makes decisions and lays out the result all in one place is hard to read, hard to test and hard to change.

Picture a school bag with everything thrown into one big pocket: pens, lunch, homework, a charger. Nothing is lost, but finding anything is slow. So you sort it into labeled pouches. The bag holds exactly the same things afterwards. It is only easier to use.

### Refactoring

Tidying code like that is called **[[refactoring|refactoring-word]]**: changing the *shape* of code without changing what it *does*. What it does, every output for every input, is its **behavior**. A refactor changes the shape and never the behavior.

Here is the method, one step at a time.

1. **[[Pin the behavior down first|pin-behaviour]].** Before you move anything, have checks, or a few saved outputs, that prove the program still does exactly what it did.
2. **Find the separate jobs.** Read the function and name each thing it does in a few words: "read one line", "average the scores", "choose a letter", "lay out a row". Each phrase is a function waiting to be pulled out.
3. **Extract one job at a time.** To **extract** means to move a piece of code out into a function of its own, and call that function where the code used to be. Run the checks after each move.
4. **[[Name things for what they mean|good-names]]**, not how they work: `letter(avg)` rather than `check_thresholds(x)`.
5. **What remains is the story.** The top function should read like a list of the steps.

### A small refactor

Before:

```python
def total(text):
    t = 0
    for l in text.split(","):
        l = l.strip()
        if l:
            t += int(l)
    return t

print(total("3, 4,, 5"))    # 12
```

This does two jobs in one loop: it turns the text into numbers, and it adds them up. The names `t` and `l` say nothing.

After:

```python
def parse_numbers(text):
    return [int(part) for part in text.split(",") if part.strip()]

def total(text):
    return sum(parse_numbers(text))

print(total("3, 4,, 5"))    # 12   the same answer as before
```

The comprehension keeps a part only if `part.strip()` is not empty, which skips the empty piece between `,,`. (`int` does not mind the spaces around `" 4"`.) Now `parse_numbers` can be tested on its own, used again somewhere else, and fixed in one place. And `total` reads like its own description: the sum of the parsed numbers.

### Extracting a chain of ifs

A chain of `if` / `elif` that sets a variable is a job that is easy to pull out. Here is one inside a longer function:

```python
if fuel >= 90:
    status = "FULL"
elif fuel >= 20:
    status = "OK"
else:
    status = "LOW"
```

As its own function, each branch **returns** its answer instead of setting a variable:

```python
def fuel_status(fuel):
    if fuel >= 90:
        return "FULL"
    if fuel >= 20:
        return "OK"
    return "LOW"

print(fuel_status(95), fuel_status(50), fuel_status(5))    # FULL OK LOW
```

`return` ends the function at once, so once a branch returns, the lines below it never run. That is why the `elif`s can become plain `if`s, and the `else` can become the last line. Back in the long function, the whole chain shrinks to `status = fuel_status(fuel)`.

### One job each, and give it back

Each small function should do **[[one job|one-job]]**, and *return* its result, rather than print it or change something far away. Functions like that are easy to test, because you call them and look at what comes back. And they fit together like building blocks: the answer from one is what you hand to the next.

A function that finds nothing useful can say so by returning `None`, and the caller checks with `is None`, as in the mutable-default lesson. A function that has two things to give back returns a tuple, and the caller unpacks it, as in the tuples lesson:

```python
def split_pair(line):
    if not line.strip():
        return None
    key, _, value = line.partition("=")
    return key.strip(), value.strip()

print(split_pair(" speed = 3 "))   # ('speed', '3')
print(split_pair("   "))           # None
```

`partition("=")` (from the string methods lesson) gives three pieces: before the `=`, the `=` itself, and after it. The middle one is not needed, so it goes into a variable called `_`, a lone **[[underscore|underscore-name]]**, which is the Python habit for "a value I am ignoring".

### The story that remains

When every job has its own function, the top function only calls them in order, and it reads like a summary. Keeping each function [[short enough to see at once|story-length]] is a rule real flight software follows. The details, like the format spec `{avg:6.1f}` (width 6, one decimal place, from the f-string lesson), live inside the small function that lays out a row, and nowhere else.

**Watch out:** the real mistake is "improving" the behavior during a refactor. You spot something you would like to change, change it while you move code around, and now an output is different and you cannot tell whether the refactor broke it or the improvement did. Keep them apart: write the idea down and change it separately, afterwards. Small details count as behavior too. If the old code picked the top student with `if avg > best_avg`, a tie keeps the *first* student. Writing `>=` in the new code would keep the *last* one, and that is a different program.

::: context refactoring-word Where the word comes from
The word comes from maths, where factoring means rewriting an expression into a different but equal form, like rewriting 6x + 9 as 3(2x + 3). Both sides are equal; one is easier to work with. Refactoring code is the same idea. The word was made popular by Martin Fowler's 1999 book "Refactoring", which lists dozens of named moves, and "Extract Function" is the one you use most.
:::

::: context pin-behaviour Checks before changes
Tests written to record what messy code does right now, rather than what it should do, are sometimes called characterization tests, a name from Michael Feathers' book "Working Effectively with Legacy Code". The checks on this lesson's task are exactly that: they record the old report's output, so you know the moment your refactor changes it. Run them after every single move, so that when one fails, you know it was the last small thing you did.
:::

::: context good-names Names that say what, not how
`check_thresholds(x)` tells you how the code works: it compares a number to some limits. `letter(avg)` tells you what you get: the letter grade for an average. A reader cares about the *what* first. Good names also make wrong code look wrong: `letter(name)` is plainly odd, while `check_thresholds(name)` hides the mistake. Choosing names is one of the hardest and most useful skills in programming.
:::

::: context one-job Easy to test, easy to trust
A function that only takes inputs and returns an answer, without printing or changing anything outside itself, always gives the same answer for the same inputs. That is what makes it easy to test. Changing something outside the function, like printing, writing a file, or editing a list the caller passed in, is called a side effect. Programs need some side effects, but good design keeps them at the edges, in a few places, and keeps the thinking in small functions that only return.
:::

::: context underscore-name A name that means "ignore this"
`_` is a real, ordinary variable name; Python does not treat it specially in your code. Programmers use it by agreement for a value that has to go somewhere, because unpacking needs a name for every piece, but that will never be used. A reader who sees `_` knows at once they can forget about it. You will see it in loops too: `for _ in range(3):` does something three times without needing the count.
:::

::: context story-length A rule from flight software
NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory writes the software that flies its Mars rovers, and in 2006 one of its engineers, Gerard Holzmann, published ten rules for safety-critical code, known as "The Power of Ten". Rule 4 says no function should be longer than what fits on one printed sheet of paper, about 60 lines. The reason is the one in this lesson: a function you can see all at once is one you can understand, check and trust.

--- task
`report(text)` in the starter works, but it does everything in one place. Refactor it into five functions, **keeping its output exactly the same**:

1. `parse_line(line)` returns `(name, scores)` for a data line, with the name stripped and title-cased and the scores as a list of ints. It returns `None` for a blank line or a `#` comment. For example `parse_line("sam: 50, 60")` is `("Sam", [50, 60])`.
2. `average(scores)` returns the mean as a float, or `0.0` for an empty list.
3. `letter(avg)` returns `"A"` (90 and up), `"B"` (80 and up), `"C"` (70 and up) or `"F"`.
4. `format_row(name, avg)` returns one row of the report, laid out exactly as the starter's `f"{name:<10}{avg:6.1f}  {grade}"`, using `letter(avg)` for the grade.
5. `report(text)` builds the report by calling `parse_line`, `average` and `format_row`, skipping lines where `parse_line` gave `None`, and still adds the `Top:` line for the first student with the highest average.

Each piece of the new code already exists somewhere in the starter: you are moving it, not inventing it.
--- starter
def report(text):
    out = []
    best_name = None
    best_avg = -1
    for raw in text.splitlines():
        raw = raw.strip()
        if raw == "" or raw.startswith("#"):
            continue
        name, _, rest = raw.partition(":")
        name = name.strip().title()
        nums = []
        for part in rest.split(","):
            part = part.strip()
            if part:
                nums.append(int(part))
        if len(nums) == 0:
            avg = 0.0
        else:
            avg = sum(nums) / len(nums)
        if avg >= 90:
            grade = "A"
        elif avg >= 80:
            grade = "B"
        elif avg >= 70:
            grade = "C"
        else:
            grade = "F"
        out.append(f"{name:<10}{avg:6.1f}  {grade}")
        if avg > best_avg:
            best_avg = avg
            best_name = name
    if best_name is not None:
        out.append(f"Top: {best_name}")
    return "\n".join(out)
--- solution
def parse_line(line):
    line = line.strip()
    if not line or line.startswith("#"):
        return None
    name, _, rest = line.partition(":")
    scores = [int(part) for part in rest.split(",") if part.strip()]
    return name.strip().title(), scores


def average(scores):
    return sum(scores) / len(scores) if scores else 0.0


def letter(avg):
    if avg >= 90:
        return "A"
    if avg >= 80:
        return "B"
    if avg >= 70:
        return "C"
    return "F"


def format_row(name, avg):
    return f"{name:<10}{avg:6.1f}  {letter(avg)}"


def report(text):
    rows = []
    best = None
    for line in text.splitlines():
        parsed = parse_line(line)
        if parsed is None:
            continue
        name, scores = parsed
        avg = average(scores)
        rows.append(format_row(name, avg))
        if best is None or avg > best[1]:
            best = (name, avg)
    if best is not None:
        rows.append(f"Top: {best[0]}")
    return "\n".join(rows)
--- hint
Start with the easiest one: `letter(avg)` is the starter's `if` / `elif` chain, with each branch returning its letter instead of setting `grade`, like the `fuel_status` example.
--- hint
`parse_line` is the strip, the skip test (now `return None` instead of `continue`), the `partition(":")`, and the list of ints, which a comprehension like `parse_numbers` can build. Return `name.strip().title(), scores`. `average` is the `if len(nums) == 0` part, returning instead of setting `avg`.
--- hint
When the four helpers exist, the loop in `report` becomes: `parsed = parse_line(line)`; if `parsed is None`, `continue`; unpack `name, scores = parsed`; `avg = average(scores)`; append `format_row(name, avg)`; then keep the old `if avg > best_avg:` lines for the top student. Compare the output with the original on the same text.
--- check case | parse_line reads a data line
parse_line("  ada lovelace: 90, 85,100 ")
=> ("Ada Lovelace", [90, 85, 100])
--- check test | parse_line skips blanks and comments
parse_line("") is None and parse_line("   ") is None and parse_line("# header") is None
--- check case | parse_line with no scores
parse_line("sam:")
=> ("Sam", [])
--- check test | average, including the empty case
average([80, 90]) == 85.0 and average([]) == 0.0
--- check test | letter uses the right boundaries
[letter(x) for x in (95, 90, 89.9, 80, 75, 70, 69.9, 0)] == ["A", "A", "B", "B", "C", "C", "F", "F"]
--- check case | format_row lays out one row
format_row("Ada", 91.66)
=> "Ada         91.7  A"
--- check case | report is unchanged
report("# class 3B\nada: 90, 95\n\n lin : 70,80,75\nsam: 50\ngrace: 99,98")
=> "Ada         92.5  A\nLin         75.0  C\nSam         50.0  F\nGrace       98.5  A\nTop: Grace"
--- check case | A tie for top keeps the first
report("a: 80\nb: 80")
=> "A           80.0  B\nB           80.0  B\nTop: A"
--- check case | Nothing to report
report("# only a comment\n")
=> ""
--- check test | report is built from the four helpers
{"parse_line", "average", "format_row"} <= set(report.__code__.co_names)
?? report should call parse_line, average and format_row (and format_row calls letter).

+++ practice | Pull out the price
--- task
`shipping_cost(parcels)` works: it adds up the price of every parcel, where `parcels` is a list of weights in kilograms. But the pricing rule is buried inside the loop. Refactor it, **keeping every answer exactly the same**:

1. Extract `price(kg)`, which returns the price of one parcel: `5` up to 1 kg, `12` up to 10 kg, and above that `12` plus `2` for every kilogram over 10. Each branch returns its answer.
2. Make `shipping_cost(parcels)` a single line that adds up `price(kg)` for every parcel.

For example, `price(1)` is `5`, `price(10)` is `12`, `price(12)` is `16`, and `shipping_cost([0.5, 3, 12])` is `33`.
--- starter
def shipping_cost(parcels):
    total = 0
    for kg in parcels:
        if kg <= 1:
            cost = 5
        elif kg <= 10:
            cost = 12
        else:
            cost = 12 + (kg - 10) * 2
        total += cost
    return total
--- solution
def price(kg):
    if kg <= 1:
        return 5
    if kg <= 10:
        return 12
    return 12 + (kg - 10) * 2


def shipping_cost(parcels):
    return sum(price(kg) for kg in parcels)
--- hint
Move the `if` / `elif` / `else` chain into its own function. Where it set `cost`, return the value instead; once a branch returns, the lines below never run, so the `elif` can become a plain `if` and the `else` can become the last line.
--- hint
`sum` accepts a comprehension without its square brackets: `sum(price(kg) for kg in parcels)`.
--- check test | price at and around each boundary
[price(kg) for kg in (0.2, 1, 1.5, 10, 11, 12.5)] == [5, 5, 12, 12, 14, 17]
--- check case | shipping_cost is unchanged
shipping_cost([0.5, 3, 12])
=> 33
--- check case | No parcels cost nothing
shipping_cost([])
=> 0
--- check test | shipping_cost is built on price
(lambda old: (globals().__setitem__("price", lambda kg: 100), shipping_cost([1, 2]), globals().__setitem__("price", old))[1])(price) == 200
?? shipping_cost should call price for each parcel, so a change to the pricing rule happens in one place.

+++ practice | Return the lines, print at the edge
--- task
`print_manifest(items)` prints a cargo manifest: one row per `(name, kg)` item, then a total row. Because it prints, nothing can check its rows without reading the screen. Refactor it, keeping the printed output exactly the same:

1. Write `manifest_lines(items)`, which **returns** the list of rows as strings: each item as `f"{name:<10}{kg:>6.1f}"`, then the total as `f"{'TOTAL':<10}{total:>6.1f}"`.
2. Make `print_manifest(items)` print each row that `manifest_lines(items)` returns, and nothing else.

Keep the last line of the starter, which prints a manifest.
--- starter
def print_manifest(items):
    total = 0
    for name, kg in items:
        print(f"{name:<10}{kg:>6.1f}")
        total += kg
    print(f"{'TOTAL':<10}{total:>6.1f}")


print_manifest([("fuel", 120.5), ("food", 30)])
--- solution
def manifest_lines(items):
    rows = [f"{name:<10}{kg:>6.1f}" for name, kg in items]
    total = sum(kg for name, kg in items)
    rows.append(f"{'TOTAL':<10}{total:>6.1f}")
    return rows


def print_manifest(items):
    for row in manifest_lines(items):
        print(row)


print_manifest([("fuel", 120.5), ("food", 30)])
--- hint
`manifest_lines` does all the thinking and returns a list. `print_manifest` becomes a two-line loop that only prints.
--- hint
Build the item rows with a comprehension, work out the total with `sum` over the weights, and append the total row at the end.
--- check output | The printed manifest is unchanged
fuel       120.5
food        30.0
TOTAL      150.5
--- check case | manifest_lines returns the rows
manifest_lines([("water", 12.5)])
=> ["water       12.5", "TOTAL       12.5"]
--- check case | An empty manifest still has its total row
manifest_lines([])
=> ["TOTAL        0.0"]
--- check test | print_manifest is built on manifest_lines
"manifest_lines" in print_manifest.__code__.co_names

+++ practice | Untangle the busiest system
--- task
`busiest_system(text)` reads a log where each line is `system: message`, skipping blank lines and `#` comments, and returns the name of the system with the most lines, in capitals. On a tie it returns the system that was seen **first**, and with no events at all it returns `None`. Refactor it into three functions, keeping every answer the same:

1. `parse_event(line)` returns a tuple `(system, message)`, with the system stripped and in capitals and the message stripped, or `None` for a blank line or a comment.
2. `count_systems(text)` returns a dict from each system to its number of lines, in first-seen order, using `parse_event`.
3. `busiest_system(text)` uses `count_systems` and returns the busiest system, or `None` when there are none. `max` with `key=` keeps the first of equal keys, which is exactly the old tie rule.

For example, `parse_event(" nav : drift 2m ")` is `("NAV", "drift 2m")`.
--- starter
def busiest_system(text):
    counts = {}
    for line in text.splitlines():
        line = line.strip()
        if line == "" or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        system, _, message = line.partition(":")
        system = system.strip().upper()
        counts[system] = counts.get(system, 0) + 1
    best = None
    for system, n in counts.items():
        if best is None or n > counts[best]:
            best = system
    return best
--- solution
def parse_event(line):
    line = line.strip()
    if not line or line.startswith("#"):
        return None
    system, _, message = line.partition(":")
    return system.strip().upper(), message.strip()


def count_systems(text):
    counts = {}
    for line in text.splitlines():
        event = parse_event(line)
        if event is None:
            continue
        system, _ = event
        counts[system] = counts.get(system, 0) + 1
    return counts


def busiest_system(text):
    counts = count_systems(text)
    if not counts:
        return None
    return max(counts.items(), key=lambda pair: pair[1])[0]
--- hint
`parse_event` is the strip, the skip test (now `return None`), and the `partition`, returning a tuple. `count_systems` is the loop that counts, calling `parse_event` and skipping `None`.
--- hint
In `busiest_system`, an empty dict counts as false, so return `None` first when there are no counts. Then `max(counts.items(), key=lambda pair: pair[1])` hands back the `(system, count)` pair with the biggest count, the first one on a tie.
--- check case | parse_event reads one line
parse_event(" nav : drift 2m ")
=> ("NAV", "drift 2m")
--- check test | parse_event skips blanks and comments
parse_event("") is None and parse_event("  ") is None and parse_event("  # note") is None
--- check case | count_systems counts per system, in first-seen order
count_systems("nav: a\n# skip\npower: b\nNav: c\n\n")
=> {"NAV": 2, "POWER": 1}
--- check case | busiest_system is unchanged
busiest_system("nav: a\npower: b\nNav: c\n")
=> "NAV"
--- check case | A tie keeps the system seen first
busiest_system("comms: a\nnav: b\nnav: c\ncomms: d")
=> "COMMS"
--- check case | No events gives None
busiest_system("# only a comment\n\n")
=> None
--- check test | busiest_system is built on count_systems
"count_systems" in busiest_system.__code__.co_names and "parse_event" in count_systems.__code__.co_names

+++ practice | Keep the zeros
--- task
`summary(readings)` works, and it has edge cases that callers rely on: `None` and negative readings are ignored, but a reading of **zero is real** and counts; and with no real readings it returns `"no data"`. Refactor it into three functions, keeping every answer exactly the same:

1. `valid(readings)` returns a list of the real readings, in order, using one list comprehension.
2. `span(values)` returns a tuple `(lowest, highest)` of a non-empty list, using `min` and `max`.
3. `summary(readings)` uses `valid` and `span` and returns the same text as before: `"<count> readings, <lowest> to <highest>"`, or `"no data"`.

For example, `summary([4, None, 0, -3, 9])` is `"3 readings, 0 to 9"`.
--- starter
def summary(readings):
    good = []
    for r in readings:
        if r is not None and r >= 0:
            good.append(r)
    if len(good) == 0:
        return "no data"
    low = good[0]
    high = good[0]
    for r in good:
        if r < low:
            low = r
        if r > high:
            high = r
    return f"{len(good)} readings, {low} to {high}"
--- solution
def valid(readings):
    return [r for r in readings if r is not None and r >= 0]


def span(values):
    return min(values), max(values)


def summary(readings):
    good = valid(readings)
    if not good:
        return "no data"
    low, high = span(good)
    return f"{len(good)} readings, {low} to {high}"
--- hint
Be careful with the filter in `valid`. `if r` looks shorter, but zero counts as false, so it would throw the zeros away and change the behavior. Copy the old condition exactly.
--- hint
`span` is one line returning a tuple. In `summary`, unpack it: `low, high = span(good)`.
--- check case | valid keeps zeros and drops None and negatives
valid([0, None, -1, 5, 0])
=> [0, 5, 0]
--- check case | span of one value
span([4])
=> (4, 4)
--- check case | summary is unchanged
summary([4, None, 0, -3, 9])
=> "3 readings, 0 to 9"
--- check case | Only zeros are still data
summary([0, 0])
=> "2 readings, 0 to 0"
--- check test | No real readings gives "no data"
summary([]) == "no data" and summary([None, -2]) == "no data"
--- check test | summary is built on valid and span
{"valid", "span"} <= set(summary.__code__.co_names)

+++ practice | Debug: the refactor that changed a tie
--- task
`top_scorer(results)` takes lines like `"ada = 90"` and returns the name with the highest score, or `None` for an empty list. Before it was refactored, a tie went to the name that came **first**: `top_scorer(["ada = 90", "lin = 90"])` gave `"ada"`. The refactored version below gives `"lin"`, so the refactor changed the behavior.

Find the change and put the old behavior back. Keep the two functions.
--- starter
def parse_result(line):
    name, _, score = line.partition("=")
    return name.strip(), int(score)


def top_scorer(results):
    best = None
    for line in results:
        name, score = parse_result(line)
        if best is None or score >= best[1]:
            best = (name, score)
    return best[0] if best else None
--- solution
def parse_result(line):
    name, _, score = line.partition("=")
    return name.strip(), int(score)


def top_scorer(results):
    best = None
    for line in results:
        name, score = parse_result(line)
        if best is None or score > best[1]:
            best = (name, score)
    return best[0] if best else None
--- hint
Look at the comparison that decides whether a new line takes over as the best. What happens when the new score is exactly equal?
--- hint
`>=` lets an equal score replace the best, so the last of a tie wins. `>` only replaces on a strictly higher score, so the first one stays.
--- check case | A tie goes to the name that came first
top_scorer(["ada = 90", "lin = 90"])
=> "ada"
--- check case | The highest score wins
top_scorer(["ada = 70", "lin = 95", "sam = 80"])
=> "lin"
--- check case | No results
top_scorer([])
=> None
--- check case | parse_result is unchanged
parse_result(" sam=7 ")
=> ("sam", 7)

+++ practice | Refactor a telemetry check
--- task
`check_pass(text)` works, but it does everything in one place. Each line of `text` is a channel, like `"tank_p = 72, 85.5, 90"`: a name, `=`, and readings separated by commas. Blank lines and `#` comments are skipped. Refactor it into four functions, **keeping its output exactly the same**:

1. `parse_channel(line)` returns `(name, readings)`: the name stripped and in capitals, the readings as a list of floats (empty pieces skipped). It returns `None` for a blank line or a comment.
2. `status(readings)` returns `"NO DATA"` for an empty list; otherwise, looking at the highest reading, `"ALARM"` above 100, `"WARN"` above 80, or `"OK"`.
3. `format_row(name, state)` returns one row, laid out exactly as the starter's `f"{name:<8}{status}"`.
4. `check_pass(text)` builds the report from the other three, and still ends with the line `"<n> need attention"`, where `n` counts the channels whose status is not `"OK"`.

For example, `parse_channel(" tank_p = 72, , 90 ")` is `("TANK_P", [72.0, 90.0])`.
--- starter
def check_pass(text):
    out = []
    flagged = 0
    for raw in text.splitlines():
        raw = raw.strip()
        if raw == "" or raw.startswith("#"):
            continue
        name, _, rest = raw.partition("=")
        name = name.strip().upper()
        values = []
        for part in rest.split(","):
            part = part.strip()
            if part:
                values.append(float(part))
        if len(values) == 0:
            status = "NO DATA"
        else:
            peak = max(values)
            if peak > 100:
                status = "ALARM"
            elif peak > 80:
                status = "WARN"
            else:
                status = "OK"
        if status != "OK":
            flagged += 1
        out.append(f"{name:<8}{status}")
    out.append(f"{flagged} need attention")
    return "\n".join(out)
--- solution
def parse_channel(line):
    line = line.strip()
    if not line or line.startswith("#"):
        return None
    name, _, rest = line.partition("=")
    readings = [float(part) for part in rest.split(",") if part.strip()]
    return name.strip().upper(), readings


def status(readings):
    if not readings:
        return "NO DATA"
    peak = max(readings)
    if peak > 100:
        return "ALARM"
    if peak > 80:
        return "WARN"
    return "OK"


def format_row(name, state):
    return f"{name:<8}{state}"


def check_pass(text):
    rows = []
    flagged = 0
    for line in text.splitlines():
        channel = parse_channel(line)
        if channel is None:
            continue
        name, readings = channel
        state = status(readings)
        if state != "OK":
            flagged += 1
        rows.append(format_row(name, state))
    rows.append(f"{flagged} need attention")
    return "\n".join(rows)
--- hint
Start with the smallest job: `format_row` is one line. Then `status`, which is the `if` chain, returning its answer from each branch, with the empty case first.
--- hint
`parse_channel` is the strip, the skip test (returning `None`), the `partition("=")`, and a comprehension that turns the non-empty pieces into floats (`float` copes with the spaces around a number).
--- hint
Watch the names in `check_pass`: the starter used `status` as a variable, and now `status` is a function. Call the variable something else, such as `state`, or the function gets replaced by a string halfway through the loop.
--- check case | parse_channel reads a channel
parse_channel(" tank_p = 72, , 90 ")
=> ("TANK_P", [72.0, 90.0])
--- check test | parse_channel skips blanks and comments
parse_channel("") is None and parse_channel("   # spare") is None
--- check test | status at and around each boundary
[status(x) for x in ([], [80], [80.5], [100], [100.1], [5, 120, 7])] == ["NO DATA", "OK", "WARN", "WARN", "ALARM", "ALARM"]
--- check case | format_row lays out one row
format_row("VOLTS", "OK")
=> "VOLTS   OK"
--- check case | check_pass is unchanged
check_pass("# pass 12\ntank_p = 72, 85.5, 90\n\nvolts = 28, 27.5\nlox_t = 101\nspare =\n")
=> "TANK_P  WARN\nVOLTS   OK\nLOX_T   ALARM\nSPARE   NO DATA\n3 need attention"
--- check case | Nothing but comments
check_pass("# empty pass\n")
=> "0 need attention"
--- check test | check_pass is built on the other three
{"parse_channel", "status", "format_row"} <= set(check_pass.__code__.co_names)

=== py2-gate | Python, intermediate: mastery gate
--- teach
The gate covers the whole intermediate course: comprehensions, tuples and unpacking, string formatting, dicts that count and group, sorting with keys, flexible arguments, the mutable default trap, exceptions you design, tracebacks, classes that behave, the standard library, text and CSV, fast lookups with a dict, and refactoring. Every problem is new, most of them join two or more lessons, and there are no hints, so plan each one before you type. The questions after the problems check that you understand why the code works, not only that it runs. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hardest, from memory, without opening the lesson.
