+++ problem | A word frequency table
--- task
Write `frequency_table(text, n)`. It returns the `n` most common words of `text` as a list of rows, one string per word.

- Words are compared in lowercase. Every character that is not a letter or a digit counts as a gap between words, so `"rocket,"` and `"Rocket!"` are both the word `rocket`.
- Rows go from the highest count to the lowest. Words with the same count go in A to Z order.
- Each row is the word pushed left in a column 12 wide, then the count pushed right in a column 4 wide: `f"{word:<12}{count:>4}"`.
- If there are fewer than `n` different words, return a row for each of them. Empty text gives `[]`.

For example, `frequency_table("The rocket, the ROCKET! And the pad.", 2)` gives two rows: `"the"` padded to 12 characters followed by `"   3"`, then `"rocket"` padded to 12 characters followed by `"   2"`.
--- starter
def frequency_table(text, n):
    return []
--- solution
from collections import Counter


def frequency_table(text, n):
    cleaned = "".join(c if c.isalnum() else " " for c in text.lower())
    counts = Counter(cleaned.split())
    ranked = sorted(counts.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return [f"{word:<12}{count:>4}" for word, count in ranked[0:n]]
--- check case | The top words, highest count first
frequency_table("The rocket, the ROCKET! And the pad.", 2)
=> ["the         " + "   3", "rocket      " + "   2"]
--- check case | Equal counts go A to Z
frequency_table("b a c b a c d", 3)
=> ["a           " + "   2", "b           " + "   2", "c           " + "   2"]
--- check case | Asking for more rows than there are words
frequency_table("go go GO", 5)
=> ["go          " + "   3"]
--- check case | Empty text
frequency_table("", 3)
=> []
--- check case | Only punctuation
frequency_table("!!! ... ???", 2)
=> []

+++ problem | Merge overlapping windows
--- task
A ground station can talk to a satellite during time windows, each a tuple `(start, end)` in minutes. The windows arrive in any order, and some overlap. Write `merge_windows(windows)`. It returns a new list of windows, sorted by start, where every group of overlapping windows has been merged into one. Two windows that only touch, like `(1, 4)` and `(4, 5)`, count as overlapping. Do not change the list passed in.

For example, `merge_windows([(8, 10), (1, 3), (2, 6), (15, 18)])` gives `[(1, 6), (8, 10), (15, 18)]`, and `merge_windows([])` gives `[]`.
--- starter
def merge_windows(windows):
    return sorted(windows)
--- solution
def merge_windows(windows):
    merged = []
    for start, end in sorted(windows):
        if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:
            last_start, last_end = merged[-1]
            merged[-1] = (last_start, max(last_end, end))
        else:
            merged.append((start, end))
    return merged
--- check case | Overlapping windows are merged, in order
merge_windows([(8, 10), (1, 3), (2, 6), (15, 18)])
=> [(1, 6), (8, 10), (15, 18)]
--- check case | Touching windows are merged
merge_windows([(1, 4), (4, 5)])
=> [(1, 5)]
--- check case | A window inside another
merge_windows([(1, 10), (2, 3), (4, 12)])
=> [(1, 12)]
--- check case | Separate windows stay separate
merge_windows([(5, 6), (1, 2)])
=> [(1, 2), (5, 6)]
--- check case | No windows
merge_windows([])
=> []
--- check test | The list passed in is not changed
(lambda w: (merge_windows(w), w)[1] == [(3, 4), (1, 3)])([(3, 4), (1, 3)])

+++ problem | A rover that reports its mistakes
--- task
A rover starts at `(0, 0)` facing north, `"N"`. It understands three commands, one per line:

- `"move N"`: move `N` steps forward, where `N` is a whole number, 0 or more. Facing `"N"` adds to `y`, `"E"` adds to `x`, `"S"` takes from `y`, `"W"` takes from `x`.
- `"turn left"` or `"turn right"`: turn 90 degrees. Turning right from `"N"` faces `"E"`, then `"S"`, then `"W"`, then `"N"` again.
- `"report"`: adds the line `f"{x},{y} {heading}"` to the output, such as `"2,3 E"`.

Write these pieces:

1. `class CommandError(Exception)`, the base class.
2. `class UnknownCommand(CommandError)`, made as `UnknownCommand(word)`, with the message `f"unknown command: {word}"` and the attribute `word`.
3. `class BadArgument(CommandError)`, made as `BadArgument(word, arg)`, with the message `f"bad argument for {word}: {repr(arg)}"`.
4. `parse_command(line)` strips the line and cuts it at the first space into the command word and its argument (also stripped). It returns `("move", steps)` with `steps` as an `int`, `("turn", "left")` or `("turn", "right")`, or `("report", None)`. It raises `BadArgument` if `move` has anything but digits as its argument (so `"move"`, `"move -2"` and `"move two"` all fail), if `turn` has anything but `left` or `right`, or if `report` has any argument at all; and `UnknownCommand` for any other word.
5. `run(commands)` returns the list of output lines. Blank lines are skipped. A line that raises a `CommandError` adds `f"error: {err}"` to the output and changes nothing else.

For example, `run(["move 3", "turn right", "move 2", "report", "fly"])` gives `["2,3 E", "error: unknown command: fly"]`.
--- starter
class CommandError(Exception):
    pass


def parse_command(line):
    word, _, arg = line.partition(" ")
    return word, arg


def run(commands):
    return []
--- solution
class CommandError(Exception):
    pass


class UnknownCommand(CommandError):
    def __init__(self, word):
        super().__init__(f"unknown command: {word}")
        self.word = word


class BadArgument(CommandError):
    def __init__(self, word, arg):
        super().__init__(f"bad argument for {word}: {repr(arg)}")
        self.word = word
        self.arg = arg


HEADINGS = ["N", "E", "S", "W"]
STEPS = {"N": (0, 1), "E": (1, 0), "S": (0, -1), "W": (-1, 0)}


def parse_command(line):
    word, _, arg = line.strip().partition(" ")
    arg = arg.strip()
    if word == "move":
        if not arg.isdigit():
            raise BadArgument(word, arg)
        return word, int(arg)
    if word == "turn":
        if arg not in ("left", "right"):
            raise BadArgument(word, arg)
        return word, arg
    if word == "report":
        if arg:
            raise BadArgument(word, arg)
        return word, None
    raise UnknownCommand(word)


def run(commands):
    x, y, facing = 0, 0, 0
    out = []
    for line in commands:
        if not line.strip():
            continue
        try:
            word, value = parse_command(line)
        except CommandError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        heading = HEADINGS[facing]
        if word == "move":
            dx, dy = STEPS[heading]
            x, y = x + dx * value, y + dy * value
        elif word == "turn":
            facing = (facing + (1 if value == "right" else -1)) % 4
        else:
            out.append(f"{x},{y} {heading}")
    return out
--- check case | Move, turn, move and report
run(["move 3", "turn right", "move 2", "report"])
=> ["2,3 E"]
--- check case | Turning left, and all the way round
run(["turn left", "move 4", "report", "turn left", "turn left", "move 10", "report"])
=> ["-4,0 W", "6,0 E"]
--- check case | Every kind of mistake is reported and skipped
run(["fly 3", "move -2", "move", "turn up", "report now", "move 0", "report"])
=> ["error: unknown command: fly", "error: bad argument for move: '-2'", "error: bad argument for move: ''", "error: bad argument for turn: 'up'", "error: bad argument for report: 'now'", "0,0 N"]
--- check case | Blank lines are skipped, extra spaces are fine
run(["", "   ", "  move   5 ", "report"])
=> ["0,5 N"]
--- check test | parse_command returns the word and its value
parse_command("move 12") == ("move", 12) and parse_command(" turn left ") == ("turn", "left") and parse_command("report") == ("report", None)
--- check test | parse_command raises the right errors
raises(UnknownCommand, lambda: parse_command("jump")) and raises(BadArgument, lambda: parse_command("move two")) and raises(CommandError, lambda: parse_command("turn"))
--- check test | The errors are a family and carry their data
issubclass(UnknownCommand, CommandError) and issubclass(BadArgument, CommandError) and UnknownCommand("fly").word == "fly" and str(BadArgument("turn", "up")) == "bad argument for turn: 'up'"

+++ problem | Ratios in lowest terms
--- task
Write a class `Ratio`, made as `Ratio(num, den=1)` from two whole numbers, like `Ratio(2, 4)`. It always keeps itself in lowest terms, with the sign on the top:

- If `den` is 0, `__init__` raises `ZeroDivisionError`.
- If `den` is negative, both signs flip: `Ratio(1, -2)` is stored as `-1` over `2`.
- Both numbers are divided by their greatest common divisor (use `math.gcd`), so `Ratio(2, 4)` is stored as `1` over `2`, and `Ratio(0, 5)` as `0` over `1`.

Store the results in `self.num` and `self.den`. The class also has:

- a read-only property `value`: the ratio as a float, `self.num / self.den`;
- `plus(other)`: returns a **new** `Ratio` that is the sum of the two;
- `__repr__`: `repr(Ratio(2, 4))` is `"Ratio(1, 2)"`;
- `__eq__`: equal when the stored numbers match; for anything that is not a `Ratio`, return `NotImplemented`.
--- starter
class Ratio:
    def __init__(self, num, den=1):
        self.num = num
        self.den = den
--- solution
import math


class Ratio:
    def __init__(self, num, den=1):
        if den == 0:
            raise ZeroDivisionError("the denominator cannot be zero")
        if den < 0:
            num, den = -num, -den
        common = math.gcd(num, den)
        self.num = num // common
        self.den = den // common

    @property
    def value(self):
        return self.num / self.den

    def plus(self, other):
        return Ratio(self.num * other.den + other.num * self.den, self.den * other.den)

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Ratio({self.num}, {self.den})"

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Ratio):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.num, self.den) == (other.num, other.den)
--- check test | Lowest terms, with the sign on top
repr(Ratio(2, 4)) == "Ratio(1, 2)" and repr(Ratio(1, -2)) == "Ratio(-1, 2)" and repr(Ratio(-6, -9)) == "Ratio(2, 3)"
--- check test | Zero, and the default denominator
repr(Ratio(0, 5)) == "Ratio(0, 1)" and repr(Ratio(3)) == "Ratio(3, 1)"
--- check test | A zero denominator raises ZeroDivisionError
raises(ZeroDivisionError, lambda: Ratio(1, 0))
--- check test | Equal ratios compare equal, others do not
Ratio(1, 2) == Ratio(2, 4) and Ratio(1, 2) != Ratio(1, 3) and (Ratio(1, 2) == 0.5) is False
--- check case | value is a float
Ratio(3, 4).value
=> 0.75
--- check test | plus returns a new ratio in lowest terms
(lambda a: a.plus(Ratio(1, 3)) == Ratio(5, 6) and a == Ratio(1, 2) and repr(a.plus(Ratio(-1, 2))) == "Ratio(0, 1)")(Ratio(1, 2))

+++ problem | Report cards from a CSV
--- task
A school exports marks as CSV with the columns `name`, `subject` and `score`. Some scores are empty, where a test was missed. Write `report_card(csv_text)`. It returns new CSV text, with `\n` line endings, that has:

- a header row: `name,average,best`;
- one row per student: the name, the average of their scores with exactly 1 decimal place (the format spec `.1f`), and the subject of their highest score. If two subjects tie for highest, the one that came first in the file wins.

Rows go from the highest average to the lowest; students with the same average go in A to Z order. Empty scores are skipped, and a student with no scores at all is left out. Read with `csv.DictReader` and write with `csv.writer`, so names or subjects with commas in them are quoted properly.

For example, for this CSV:

```
name,subject,score
lin,maths,70
ada,maths,90
lin,art,90
ada,art,
```

`report_card` returns `"name,average,best\nada,90.0,maths\nlin,80.0,art\n"`.
--- starter
import csv
import io


def report_card(csv_text):
    return "name,average,best\n"
--- solution
import csv
import io
from collections import defaultdict


def report_card(csv_text):
    marks = defaultdict(list)
    for row in csv.DictReader(io.StringIO(csv_text)):
        if row["score"].strip():
            marks[row["name"]].append((row["subject"], float(row["score"])))
    students = []
    for name, scores in marks.items():
        average = sum(score for _, score in scores) / len(scores)
        best = max(scores, key=lambda mark: mark[1])[0]
        students.append((name, average, best))
    students.sort(key=lambda s: (-s[1], s[0]))
    out = io.StringIO()
    writer = csv.writer(out, lineterminator="\n")
    writer.writerow(["name", "average", "best"])
    for name, average, best in students:
        writer.writerow([name, f"{average:.1f}", best])
    return out.getvalue()
--- check case | Averages, best subjects, highest average first
report_card("name,subject,score\nlin,maths,70\nada,maths,90\nlin,art,90\nada,art,\n")
=> "name,average,best\nada,90.0,maths\nlin,80.0,art\n"
--- check case | Equal averages go A to Z, and a tie for best keeps the first subject
report_card("name,subject,score\nsam,art,80\nbo,chem,60\nsam,bio,80\nbo,phys,100\n")
=> "name,average,best\nbo,80.0,phys\nsam,80.0,art\n"
--- check case | A student with only missed tests is left out
report_card("name,subject,score\nzed,art,\nami,art,55\n")
=> "name,average,best\nami,55.0,art\n"
--- check case | Commas inside a field are quoted
report_card('name,subject,score\n"Lovelace, Ada",maths,99\n')
=> 'name,average,best\n"Lovelace, Ada",99.0,maths\n'
--- check case | No rows gives just the header
report_card("name,subject,score\n")
=> "name,average,best\n"

+++ problem | The first repeat that is close enough
--- task
A stream of events may repeat. A repeat matters only if it comes soon after the last time the same event happened. Write `first_close_repeat(events, k)`. It returns a tuple `(i, j)` for the first position `j` where `events[j]` also happened at an earlier position `i` no more than `k` places before it (`j - i <= k`). Here `i` is the **most recent** earlier position of that event. If there is no such repeat, return `None`.

It must be fast on 100,000 events, so walk the list once with a dict.

For example, `first_close_repeat(["a", "b", "c", "a", "a"], 1)` gives `(3, 4)`: the `"a"` at position 3 is too far from the one at 0, but the `"a"` at position 4 is right after the one at 3.
--- starter
def first_close_repeat(events, k):
    return None
--- solution
def first_close_repeat(events, k):
    last = {}
    for j, event in enumerate(events):
        if event in last and j - last[event] <= k:
            return (last[event], j)
        last[event] = j
    return None
--- check case | A repeat within k
first_close_repeat(["a", "b", "a", "c", "b"], 2)
=> (0, 2)
--- check case | Only the most recent earlier position counts
first_close_repeat(["a", "b", "c", "a", "a"], 1)
=> (3, 4)
--- check case | Repeats that are all too far apart
first_close_repeat(["a", "b", "a", "c", "b"], 1)
=> None
--- check case | k of zero never matches
first_close_repeat(["x", "x"], 0)
=> None
--- check case | No events
first_close_repeat([], 3)
=> None
--- check case | 100,000 events, repeat at the end
first_close_repeat(list(range(100000)) + [99990], 10)
=> (99990, 100000)

+++ problem | An event log you can copy safely
--- task
An event log is a list of entries. Each entry is a tuple `(event, tags, fields)`: the event name, a tuple of tags, and a dict of named values. Write three functions:

1. `record(event, *tags, log=None, **fields)` adds one entry to the end of `log` and returns `log`. `tags` and `fields` are stored exactly as they arrive: a tuple and a dict. With no log given, it starts a **new** list for this call. A log the caller passes in, even an empty one, is the one added to.
2. `describe(entry)` returns the entry as one line: the event, then each tag as `#tag`, then each field as `name=value`, in order, all separated by single spaces. For example, `describe(("burn", ("main", "t0"), {"secs": 30}))` gives `"burn #main #t0 secs=30"`.
3. `copy_log(log)` returns a **new** log with the same entries, in order, made by calling `record` for each entry with its tags and fields spread back out as arguments. The copy must not share any list or dict with the original.

For example, `record("burn", "main", log=my_log, secs=30)` adds `("burn", ("main",), {"secs": 30})` to `my_log`.
--- starter
def record(event, *tags, log=[], **fields):
    log.append((event, tags, fields))
    return log


def describe(entry):
    return str(entry)


def copy_log(log):
    return log
--- solution
def record(event, *tags, log=None, **fields):
    if log is None:
        log = []
    log.append((event, tags, fields))
    return log


def describe(entry):
    event, tags, fields = entry
    line = event
    for tag in tags:
        line += f" #{tag}"
    for name, value in fields.items():
        line += f" {name}={value}"
    return line


def copy_log(log):
    copy = []
    for event, tags, fields in log:
        record(event, *tags, log=copy, **fields)
    return copy
--- check test | Two new logs are independent
record("burn") == [("burn", (), {})] and record("coast") == [("coast", (), {})]
--- check test | A log you pass in, even empty, is added to and returned
(lambda mine: record("burn", "main", "t0", log=mine, secs=30) is mine and mine == [("burn", ("main", "t0"), {"secs": 30})])([])
--- check case | describe lists the tags and fields
describe(("burn", ("main", "t0"), {"secs": 30, "ok": True}))
=> "burn #main #t0 secs=30 ok=True"
--- check case | describe with no tags or fields
describe(("idle", (), {}))
=> "idle"
--- check test | copy_log copies every entry, in order
(lambda log: copy_log(log) == log)([("a", ("x",), {"n": 1}), ("b", (), {})])
--- check test | The copy shares no list or dict with the original
(lambda log: (lambda c: c is not log and c[0][2] is not log[0][2])(copy_log(log)))([("a", ("x",), {"n": 1})])

+++ problem | Run-length encode and decode
--- task
Write two functions that squeeze text and unsqueeze it again. The text never contains digits.

1. `encode(text)` returns each run of a repeated character as the character followed by the length of the run, all glued together. Use `groupby`. For example, `encode("aaabccdddd")` gives `"a3b1c2d4"`, and a run of 12 x's gives `"x12"`.
2. `decode(code)` turns such a code back into the text. A count can have more than one digit. For example, `decode("z12y1")` gives twelve z's followed by `"y"`.

Both give `""` for `""`, and `decode(encode(text))` must always give back `text`, spaces included.
--- starter
def encode(text):
    return text


def decode(code):
    return code
--- solution
from itertools import groupby


def encode(text):
    return "".join(f"{ch}{len(list(run))}" for ch, run in groupby(text))


def decode(code):
    pieces = []
    ch = ""
    digits = ""
    for c in code:
        if c.isdigit():
            digits += c
        else:
            if ch:
                pieces.append(ch * int(digits))
            ch, digits = c, ""
    if ch:
        pieces.append(ch * int(digits))
    return "".join(pieces)
--- check case | encode counts each run
encode("aaabccdddd")
=> "a3b1c2d4"
--- check case | A run longer than nine
encode("x" * 12 + "y")
=> "x12y1"
--- check case | decode reverses it
decode("a3b1c2d4")
=> "aaabccdddd"
--- check case | decode reads counts with more than one digit
decode("z12y1")
=> "z" * 12 + "y"
--- check test | Empty text both ways
encode("") == "" and decode("") == ""
--- check test | A round trip gives back the text, spaces included
decode(encode("hello   wooorld  ")) == "hello   wooorld  "

+++ problem | Search a small library
--- task
Write two functions for searching a list of documents.

1. `build_index(docs)` returns a dict from each word to the list of document numbers it appears in, counting documents from 1. Words are compared in lowercase, and every character that is not a letter or a digit counts as a gap between words. Each document number appears at most once in a word's list, in increasing order. The dict's keys are in sorted order.
2. `search(index, query)` returns the list of document numbers that contain **every** word of `query` (split on spaces, compared in lowercase), in increasing order. A word not in the index matches nothing, and an empty query gives `[]`.

For example, with `docs = ["The rocket launched.", "A rocket, a pad!", "Pad 39 is ready"]`, the index maps `"rocket"` to `[1, 2]` and `"pad"` to `[2, 3]`, and `search(index, "rocket pad")` gives `[2]`.
--- starter
def build_index(docs):
    return {}


def search(index, query):
    return []
--- solution
def build_index(docs):
    index = {}
    for number, doc in enumerate(docs, start=1):
        cleaned = "".join(c if c.isalnum() else " " for c in doc.lower())
        for word in cleaned.split():
            pages = index.setdefault(word, [])
            if number not in pages:
                pages.append(number)
    return dict(sorted(index.items()))


def search(index, query):
    found = None
    for word in query.lower().split():
        pages = index.get(word, [])
        found = pages if found is None else [p for p in found if p in pages]
    return [] if found is None else found
--- check case | The index, with sorted keys
build_index(["The rocket launched.", "A rocket, a pad!", "Pad 39 is ready"])
=> {"39": [3], "a": [2], "is": [3], "launched": [1], "pad": [2, 3], "ready": [3], "rocket": [1, 2], "the": [1]}
--- check test | The keys really are in sorted order
list(build_index(["b a", "c"])) == ["a", "b", "c"]
--- check case | A word twice in one document is listed once
build_index(["go go GO"])
=> {"go": [1]}
--- check case | search needs every word
search(build_index(["The rocket launched.", "A rocket, a pad!", "Pad 39 is ready"]), "rocket pad")
=> [2]
--- check case | search ignores capitals
search(build_index(["The rocket launched.", "A rocket, a pad!", "Pad 39 is ready"]), "PAD")
=> [2, 3]
--- check test | An unknown word or an empty query finds nothing
(lambda ix: search(ix, "mars") == [] and search(ix, "rocket mars") == [] and search(ix, "") == [])(build_index(["The rocket launched.", "A rocket, a pad!"]))

+++ problem | Fix the telemetry parser
--- task
Each telemetry packet is a line like `"alt = 900; v = 3.5"`: fields separated by `;`, each `name = value`. `parse_packet(line, fields)` fills the dict `fields` with the packet's values and returns it. `max_altitude(lines)` returns the highest `alt` of all the packets.

Two bug reports came in:

1. `max_altitude(["alt=900; v=3", "alt=1200; v=4"])` returns `"900"`, a string, instead of `1200.0`.
2. Parsing two packets one after the other mixes them up: after `parse_packet("a=1")`, a call to `parse_packet("b=2")` returns `{"a": ..., "b": ...}`.

Fix both causes at the source, in `parse_packet`: values must be stored as floats, and a call without a dict must start a new one, while a dict the caller passes in, even an empty one, is still filled and returned. Then make `max_altitude` skip packets with no `alt` field, and return `None` if no packet has one. Blank parts, such as the one after a trailing `;`, are skipped.
--- starter
def parse_packet(line, fields={}):
    for part in line.split(";"):
        name, _, value = part.partition("=")
        fields[name.strip()] = value.strip()
    return fields


def max_altitude(lines):
    return max(parse_packet(line)["alt"] for line in lines)
--- solution
def parse_packet(line, fields=None):
    if fields is None:
        fields = {}
    for part in line.split(";"):
        if not part.strip():
            continue
        name, _, value = part.partition("=")
        fields[name.strip()] = float(value)
    return fields


def max_altitude(lines):
    packets = [parse_packet(line) for line in lines]
    altitudes = [packet["alt"] for packet in packets if "alt" in packet]
    return max(altitudes) if altitudes else None
--- check case | Values are floats
parse_packet("alt = 900; v = 3.5")
=> {"alt": 900.0, "v": 3.5}
--- check test | Two packets parsed one after the other do not mix
parse_packet("a=1") == {"a": 1.0} and parse_packet("b=2") == {"b": 2.0}
--- check test | A dict you pass in, even empty, is filled and returned
(lambda d: parse_packet("t=5;", d) is d and d == {"t": 5.0})({})
--- check case | The highest altitude, as a number
max_altitude(["alt=900; v=3", "alt=1200; v=4"])
=> 1200.0
--- check case | Packets with no altitude are skipped
max_altitude(["v=3", "alt=50", "temp=20"])
=> 50.0
--- check case | No altitude at all gives None
max_altitude(["v=1", "v=2"])
=> None

+++ question | A set comprehension with a filter
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
nums = [3, -1, 4, -1, 5]
print({n % 2 for n in nums if n > 0})
```
--- answer
{0, 1}
{1, 0}
--- why
The filter keeps only 3, 4 and 5. Their remainders after dividing by 2 are 1, 0 and 1, and a set keeps each value once, so two values are left: 0 and 1. The -1s never reach the front of the comprehension, because the `if` after the `for` drops them first.

+++ question | Star unpacking a string
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
first, *rest = "abc"
print(first, rest)
```
--- answer
a ['b', 'c']
a ["b", "c"]
--- why
A string can be unpacked like any sequence, one character per name. `first` gets `"a"`, and the starred name always collects the leftovers into a **list**, even when they came from a string or a tuple, so `rest` is `['b', 'c']`.

+++ question | Zipping lists of different lengths
--- ask
What is the value of `dict(zip(["a", "b", "c"], [1, 2]))`?
--- choice correct
`{'a': 1, 'b': 2}`
--- choice
`{'a': 1, 'b': 2, 'c': None}`
--- choice
It raises a `ValueError`, because the lists have different lengths.
--- choice
It raises an `IndexError` when it runs out of numbers.
--- why
`zip` stops at the shortest input, silently, so `"c"` never gets paired and is dropped without any error. Only `zip(..., strict=True)` raises a `ValueError` when the lengths differ. Nothing fills the gap with `None`.

+++ question | Three format specs
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
print(f"{1234.5:,.1f} {0.25:.0%} {7:03d}")
```
--- answer
1,234.5 25% 007
--- why
`,.1f` adds a thousands comma and shows one decimal place. `.0%` multiplies by 100 and shows no decimals, then adds the percent sign. `03d` is a whole number at least 3 wide, padded with zeros in front.

+++ question | Why the grouped items vanish
--- ask
This loop is meant to group words by their first letter, but `groups` is still `{}` at the end. Why?

```python
groups = {}
for word in ["ant", "bee", "asp"]:
    groups.get(word[0], []).append(word)
```
--- choice
`append` cannot be called on a value that came out of a dict.
--- choice correct
For a missing key, `get` hands back a new empty list without storing it, so each word is appended to a list that is thrown away.
--- choice
`get` always hands back a copy of the stored list, so changes to it never reach the dict.
--- choice
Keys must be added with `groups[key] = ...` before a loop, and a dict cannot grow inside one.
--- why
`get` only reads: the default is handed back, never stored. `setdefault(key, [])` stores the empty list first and then hands back that same list, so the append lands in the dict. (When the key does exist, `get` hands back the stored list itself, not a copy.)

+++ question | Sorting by one part of a tuple
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
pairs = [(2, "b"), (1, "z"), (2, "a"), (1, "y")]
print(sorted(pairs, key=lambda p: p[0]))
```
--- answer
[(1, 'z'), (1, 'y'), (2, 'b'), (2, 'a')]
[(1, "z"), (1, "y"), (2, "b"), (2, "a")]
--- why
The key looks only at the number, so `(1, "z")` and `(1, "y")` tie, and so do the two 2s. Python's sort is stable: tied items keep the order they started in. The letters are never compared, so `"z"` stays ahead of `"y"`.

+++ question | Collecting and spreading
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def f(*args, **kwargs):
    return len(args), len(kwargs)

data = [1, 2, 3]
opts = {"a": 1}
print(f(data, **opts), f(*data, opts))
```
--- answer
(1, 1) (4, 0)
--- why
In the first call, `data` goes in whole, as one positional argument, and `**opts` spreads the dict into one keyword argument `a=1`. In the second, `*data` spreads the list into three positional arguments, and `opts`, with no stars, is a fourth positional argument. The stars in the `def` collect; the stars in a call spread.

+++ question | A default that remembers
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def add(x, box=[]):
    box.append(x)
    return len(box)

print(add("a"), add("b"), add("c", []), add("d"))
```
--- answer
1 2 1 3
--- why
The default list is made once, when the `def` line runs, and every call that leaves `box` out shares it. `"a"` and `"b"` go into that shared list. The third call passes its own new list, so it gives 1 and leaves the shared list alone. The fourth call is back on the shared list, which now holds three items.

+++ question | The except that never runs
--- ask
`load("x")` returns `"error"` and never `"not a number"`. Why?

```python
def load(text):
    try:
        value = int(text)
    except Exception:
        return "error"
    except ValueError:
        return "not a number"
    return value
```
--- choice correct
The `except` blocks are tried from top to bottom, and `ValueError` is a kind of `Exception`, so the first block catches it before the second is reached.
--- choice
`int("x")` raises a `TypeError`, not a `ValueError`, so only the general block matches.
--- choice
Every matching `except` block runs, and the first `return` wins.
--- choice
An `except Exception` block always runs, whatever happened in the `try`.
--- why
Python runs the first `except` whose class matches, and a parent class matches all of its children. Put the most specific classes first: `except ValueError` above `except Exception`. The general block only runs when there is an error; when `int` succeeds, neither runs.

+++ question | Where the bug in a traceback lives
--- ask
A traceback ends like this:

```
  File "main.py", line 12, in average_speed
    return trip["km"] / trip["hours"]
TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for /: 'str' and 'float'
```

`trip` is a dict made by another function, `read_trip`. Line 12 is the right formula. Where should you look for the bug first?
--- choice
Line 12, because a traceback always points at the line with the bug.
--- choice correct
The code that made `trip`, because line 12 is correct but was handed a string where a number belongs.
--- choice
The first line of the traceback, because the earliest call is where the problem started.
--- choice
Nowhere in this program: the error comes from a bug inside Python's `/` operator.
--- why
The last frame is where the program stopped, not necessarily where the mistake was made. "`str` and `float`" means the distance is still text. Ask who made that value and walk back to it: converting it where it is made (in `read_trip`) fixes every caller, while patching line 12 hides the symptom.

+++ question | A property that calls itself
--- ask
Reading `c.speed` on this class stops with `RecursionError`. Why?

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, speed):
        self._speed = speed

    @property
    def speed(self):
        return self.speed
```
--- choice correct
Inside the getter, `self.speed` reads the property again, which runs the getter again, forever, until Python stops the pile of calls.
--- choice
A property must also have a setter, and reading one without a setter recurses.
--- choice
`__init__` stored `_speed`, so `speed` does not exist and Python keeps searching for it.
--- choice
Properties cannot return a value that was set in `__init__`.
--- why
`self.speed` is the property itself, so the getter calls itself with no way out, adding a frame each time until the recursion limit. The getter must return the stored value under its private name, `self._speed`. The setter, if there is one, has the same rule.

+++ question | The cost of looking in a list
--- ask
What is the running time of this function for a list of `n` numbers, in the worst case?

```python
def any_pair(nums, target):
    for i, x in enumerate(nums):
        if target - x in nums[i + 1:len(nums)]:
            return True
    return False
```
--- choice
O(n), because the loop goes through the list once.
--- choice
O(1), because `in` answers straight away.
--- choice correct
O(n²), because `in` on a list checks the items one by one, inside a loop that runs n times.
--- choice
O(log n), because each check looks at a shorter piece of the list.
--- why
There is only one `for`, but `in` on a list is a hidden loop: it compares item after item. n steps, each costing up to n, is about n × n work. Remembering the numbers already seen in a dict makes each check take about the same time whatever the size, so the whole function becomes O(n).
